#10203. Your Pose Model Gives You Coordinates, Not Meaning

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Pose detection in the browser is a solved problem. You can load a model, point it at a webcam, and get a skeleton tracking a human body in real time, on a laptop, with no server involved.

And then you have a skeleton that follows someone around the screen, which is a toy.

The gap between that toy and a feature someone would pay for is smaller than most developers expect, and it isn't made of machine learning. It's made of trigonometry you already know. This article builds a working bicep-curl rep counter to show exactly where that line falls — and more importantly, which side of it your bugs live on.

I'm using MediaPipe Pose Landmarker from Google AI Edge, which is Apache-2.0 licensed and runs entirely in the tab. Here's the whole setup:

Point that at a

Thirty-three points. Each one is a normalised coordinate — x and y run 0 to 1 across the frame, z is a relative depth estimate — plus a visibility score saying how confident the model is that it can actually see that joint.

That's the entire output. Coordinates and confidences.

The model has no concept of exercise. It doesn't know what an arm is for. It has never heard of a bicep curl. It reports where it thinks your joints are, thirty-odd times a second, and stops.

Everything that turns that into a product is yours, and it's ordinary code.

A bicep curl is a change in the angle at the elbow. The elbow angle is defined by three of those 33 points: shoulder, elbow, wrist. In MediaPipe's pose topology those are indices 11, 13 and 15 on the left side, 12, 14 and 16 on the right.

Getting the angle is two vectors and a dot product:

This is the same maths you'd use to point a game character in a direction. Nothing about it is machine learning, and that matters for a reason I'll come back to.

One thing to notice: I'm ignoring z entirely. The depth estimate is relative, not metric — it's good enough for working out which parts of a body are nearer the camera, and not good enough to measure anything. For a 2D elbow angle, using it adds noise and buys nothing.

Here's where almost everyone's first attempt breaks.

The naive version looks at the angle and counts whenever the arm is up:

Run that at 30 frames a second and one slow curl gives you about twenty reps. You haven't counted repetitions. You've counted frames in which the arm happened to be bent.

A rep isn't a state. It's a transition between states:

Fifteen lines, and that's the feature. The counter increments exactly once per cycle, on the edge, because the guard on phase makes every other frame in that cycle a no-op.

If you used a single threshold — say, 100 degrees — you'd get a counter that fires repeatedly whenever someone holds their arm near 100 degrees and the estimate jitters by a degree either side. Every tiny fluctuation across the line is a transition, and every transition is a rep.

Two widely separated thresholds give you hysteresis. Once the arm is "up," it has to travel all the way past 150 degrees before the counter will consider another rep. Noise of a few degrees can't produce a count because no amount of jitter near 55 crosses 150.

This is the same trick a thermostat uses to avoid switching the heating on and off every few seconds, and it's the single most useful pattern in this whole category of work. Any time you turn a continuous signal into discrete events, you want two thresholds, not one.

visibility is the field people skip, and skipping it is how you get phantom reps.

When a joint goes out of frame or gets occluded, the model still returns a coordinate for it. It is obliged to return 33 points. What it does is return a guess, with a low visibility score attached — and a guessed wrist position can swing wildly between frames, which looks exactly like a very fast curl.

Returning null and having update bail out means an occluded frame holds the current state rather than corrupting it. The arm stays "up" until the model can actually see it come down.

If you want to be stricter, smooth the angle over the last three or four frames with a rolling median before it reaches the state machine. A median rejects single-frame outliers in a way a mean doesn't.

Once you've written one of these, you start recognising the shape everywhere:

None of those require a different model. They're the same 33 coordinates with different geometry sitting on top, and the geometry is a few dozen lines each.

Here's why I keep insisting on the distinction between coordinates and meaning.

When my rep counter miscounts, I don't retrain anything. I don't collect a dataset, I don't label examples, I don't spin up a training job. I look at two things: my thresholds, and my state machine. One of them is wrong.

That's a normal bug in normal code, and you already know how to find it. You can log the angle. You can set a breakpoint on the transition. You can write a unit test that feeds a synthetic sequence of angles through update and asserts the count — no camera, no model, no fixtures:

The hard part of this problem — turning pixels into joint positions — was solved by a research team and shipped to you as a file you can download. The part that's left is the part you're already good at.

You are not blocked on machine learning knowledge. You were blocked on nobody telling you this was available.

I set delegate: 'GPU' above without comment, and it deserves one. MediaPipe Tasks lets you choose between a CPU path and a GPU path, and availability of the GPU path varies by browser, platform and graphics driver.

The practical consequence is that you have to test the CPU path, because for some meaningful share of your users it isn't a fallback — it's the only path they will ever take. If you develop on a machine where the GPU delegate works and never exercise the CPU one, you have shipped something you've never run.

That, and the question of how often you should run the model at all, is a bigger subject than this article. It's the next one.

Jay Nirmal is a Senior Software Engineer at Meazure Learning with over eight years of experience in distributed systems, cloud infrastructure, WebRTC, and production reliability. He leads engineering initiatives supporting large-scale digital assessment platforms, focusing on high-availability systems, real-time media infrastructure, recording reliability, and assessment integrity. Jay holds a Master of Science in Information Systems from Northeastern University and a Bachelor of Engineering in Computer Engineering from the University of Mumbai.

A year after being announced, the Odyssey G6 is finally being released.

A year after being announced, the Odyssey G6 is finally being released.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After being teased in December 2025 and getting an arguably unnecessary pre-release spec bump in August, the Samsung Odyssey G6 G60H rolls out next month for $899.99. The 27-inch gaming monitor will have an absurd 1,100Hz refresh rate, narrowly edging out LG’s UltraGear 25G590B, which tops out at 1,000Hz.

Is there any value in these ultra-fast refresh rates? That’s up for debate. But whether or not you can actually see the difference, it does give the company bragging rights. So there is that, I guess.

The G60H will only be able ot hit 1,100Hz in 1080p. Bumping the resolution up to 1440p will drop the refresh rate to 600Hz, which is still absurdly fast. The Dual Mode IPS panel is also the first to feature Samsung’s new Odyssey Clear Motion tech, which reduces motion blur during games. Eventually, the company plans to bring Clear Motion to more gaming monitors. Black Equalizer also makes it easier to see details in dark scenes.

Beyond that, the G60H also supports HDR10+, Nvidia G-Sync, and AMD FreeSync Premium.

#10201. Motorola Signature 27 hands-on: Pure elegance on display

It’s hard to believe we finally got a proper premium phone from Motorola again, and it feels a bit like the return of the Moto X.

Motorola’s slow regeneration in North America from a mostly budget device-minded company to one offering the most expensive non-folding flagship phone might become the thing of legend. That’s not to say the company hasn’t shown true greatness in the past — the Moto X, Moto Z, and Razr lines are proof of that — but it’s always shied away from pulling out all the stops until the Motorola Signature 27 arrived.

Freshly unboxed and sitting in my hands, the textile-clad Capulet Olive Motorola Signature 27 colorway has impressed me in every way, shape, and form over the past 24 hours. It’s a much longer period of time than I usually get with hands-on opportunities and, yet, the newness of the experience has yet to wear off.

Part and parcel to that experience is the design. For whatever reason, this is the year Motorola seems to have truly figured out what makes a phone nice to hold. The textile back, ribbed metal frame, rounded corners, and curved edges on the front and back of the phone are purely blissful to hold. It’s a reminder that a phone can be as good looking as it feels instead of being a flat, boring, off-balance slab like too many phones are now.

The weight also surprised me. On paper this sits right in the middle of all the Ultra/Pro Max-named phones, yet it feels lighter for some intangible reason. The camera hump makes it slightly top-heavy, and it’s almost as wobble-free as a Pixel (there’s just one off-center camera hump).

The new 120Hz LTPO tandem OLED display is as top-notch as you’re going to get. I had all of maybe 2 hours of direct sunlight yesterday to test it with, and boy does it ever get bright. The rest of the time, I’ve been either inside or out in overcast weather, all of which it looked brilliant in. And it uses a combination of DC dimming above 20% brightness, and 2160Hz PWM dimming below 20% brightness, making it super comfortable for me.

Motorola’s software has evolved again with this release, and the verdict is still out on whether or not this is actually better than the company’s Android 16 designs. The build of Android 17 that ships with this phone utilizes some major design changes we’ve only seen on a few random low-end Motorola phones, and I’m not sure I care for the gentle gradients on the quick toggle icons and some other system UI elements. It feels like a weirdly cheap effect that clashes with Material Design’s flatter motif.

Despite that, this is the most Pixelly (is that a word?) a Motorola phone has ever felt. Features like Rambler, Sign to Text, and Create My Widget are here and work impeccably well. Gemini Intelligence is also on the way, and while there’s no specific ETA for that at the moment, it’s exciting to know that the most advanced Android features — which are currently only available on a very small number of devices — are coming to this phone.

This may also be the fastest phone I’ve used to date. Everything is unbelievably snappy. Tap an icon and the thing opens instantly. Animations are zippy, and the new lock screen and customization options feel cohesive. Plus, all the favorite Moto actions and gestures are still here, which I greatly appreciate.

I’ve got some lofty expectations for this camera hardware, too, and it’s because Motorola is talking up such a big game. DXOMark’s camera review rated it the 2nd-best smartphone camera in the world, just under the Huawei Pura 80 Ultra and above the iPhone 18 Pro. Some people don’t take much stock in DXOMark, but it’s still worth noting that they’ve praised the heck out of the camera and Motorola is also pushing this as a winner in a way they’ve never done before.

The hype for the Motorola Signature 27 certainly seems to be realized, and now only time will truly tell if it can live up to the lofty expectations Motorola’s $1,599 price has set for it. If nothing, it’s certainly off to a good start and has made an excellent first impression.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

3D printing is a booming hobby at the moment thanks to the prices of quality 3D printers dropping constantly. However, once you've dived into the world of 3D printing, making sure you maintain your printer is a priority.

Regular maintenance improves the quality of your prints, and if you also want to tidy up the finished look of your creations, it's worth building a small toolkit comprised of a few useful tools. Although some manufacturers may throw in a few cheap tools with a new 3D printer, they don't cover everything, or may be of poor quality, so it's still worth investing a few bucks into a dedicated 3D printer toolkit.

We've trawled through a host of Black Friday deals and used our expert knowledge to source the tools you need to up your 3D printing game.

Grab a complete 74-piece 3D printer tool kit from Creality. Packed with everything that you need to maintain your printer in tip-top condition, this toolkit features everything from a 34-piece screwdriver set to a magnetic project mat. Perfect for either a novice or an expert 3D printer enthusiast,

This massive 74-piece toolkit includes everything that you could ever need to keep your 3D printer running at optimal efficiency. Snips, scrapers, pliers, and more, all contained in a handy tool wrap.

A pair of snips is essential for cutting your filament to length, cleaning out leftover supports, or tidying up your prints. If you're lucky, your printer may come with a pair included, but if not, then picking up an inexpensive set of snips is a must.

A handy pair of snips to trim, cut, and remove plastic from places where it shouldn’t be. Maintain your printer and clean up your prints.

If you weren't already aware, Hex keys are essential 3D printer tools. The majority of modern 3D printers are held together by machine screws that sport an Allen / hex head. Having a set means you can perform repairs and maintenance easily.

This 9-piece metric Hex key set is perfect for maintaining your printer. Key sizes range from 1.5 to 10 mm, with a long ball end.

A 3D printer's print bed is super grippy so that your prints don't move during printing. This can sometimes result in the print needing a little more persuasion to come free. That's where a handy scraper can come in useful.

Two angled scrapers, and two smaller scrapers, one with a metal blade, the other with a plastic blade to protect your print bed from scratches.

A good general-purpose precision screwdriver set is so handy for 3D printing and maker/crafting in general. If you're adding screwable components to your prints, these large kits have all the sizes you need to complete the task.

A 49-piece screwdriver set that includes all the bits that you will ever need to keep your 3D printer running smoothly.

This slimline and compact HOTO screwdriver comes with 24 double-ended bits.

A go-to screwdriver set for the office, workshop and your go-bag. With all the bits you will need to fix a multitude of problems.

Probably the first problem you will encounter with a 3D printer is clogging. It's a guarantee that the hot plastic will clog the nozzle at some point, and all printing has to cease until you remove the blockage. Removing the printer nozzle and unclogging the nozzle is made so much easier with the right tools.

Creality's Clog Poke does exactly what it says on the packaging. Remove the PTFE tube and push this clog poke into the throat of the extruder, forcing molten plastic out of the nozzle.

A complete toolkit solution to maintain the business end of your 3D printer. Nozzle wrenches, needles, tweezers, and two handy brass-bristled brushes.

A sharp but safe blade trims unwanted plastic from prints, leaving a clean edge.

I’m a little jealous that you have the opportunity to save $70 on the monitor I’ve been using for work and play for several months. Best Buy and Dell are selling the 27-inch 1440p monitor for $279.99 ahead of Black Friday.

If you’re on a tight budget, but want spectacular contrast and brightness control that only an OLED can provide, here’s a deal for you. Alienware’s 1440p 27-inch QD-OLED monitor with 240Hz refresh rate is selling at its lowest price yet. Read our hands-on.

I think it’d be a sweet gift to yourself or to a gamer in your life this holiday.

One of the two companies is one of Nvidia's "preferred partners," while the other was renamed after its Chinese parent company was blackisted.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

While Nvidia is only allowed to sell its most advanced AI chips to whitelisted companies, it appears that two of its Californian customers are actually fronts for Chinese companies. Further, one of the Californian companies was renamed after its Chinese parent company was blacklisted, CBS News reports. The other one, which is on Nvidia's list of "preferred partners," is owned by a Chinese firm whose CEO graduated from a Chinese military university. It's reported that U.S.-based, Chinese-owned companies have been used as a way to work around export controls designed to prevent Nvidia's chips from being sold wholesale to China. Chips are routinely sold to companies in the U.S. before being leased or shipped to other companies in Southeast Asia.

While Nvidia isn't allowed to sell its most capable AI chips to China, it can still do business with the country. It can sell graphics cards for use in personal gaming PCs as well as AI chips that don't use the company's latest, most capable technologies. But that hasn't stopped those chips from turning up in the most unlikely of places. Illegal Nvidia Blackwell Ultra B300 GPUs have been spotted in use inside China Telecom's network, and last year alone, it's believed that around $1 billion worth of Nvidia chips were smuggled into China. Earlier this year, nine people were indicted over the illegal export of Nvidia GPUs, while even older Nvidia hardware has seen huge price increases due to the unavailability of newer hardware.

Megaspeed is one company that is accused of facilitating the breaking of export controls. The company is under criminal investigation currently, and it was recently blacklisted as a result. The company also happens to be a former employer of Alice Huang, the woman whom Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to having an affair with when he resigned from the company earlier this month. Alice Huang is also believed to be a Chinese Spy, while her husband has even closer connections to a Chinese spy agency.

Back in the U.S., RiCloud AI is an Nvidia customer owned by Yuxing InfoTech Investment Holdings Limited, an outfit with headquarters in Shenzhen, China. Yuxing's CEO and co-owner, Cong Yu, reportedly has a degree in National Defense Mobilization from the People's Liberation Army Command College, a university operated by the Chinese military. Among RiCloud AI's customers is Agencia Comercial Spirits, a small whisky importer that pivoted to AI in 2025. Since then, it has committed to AI data center deals worth up to $598 million.

Airvres is currently listed as an "elite" partner on Nvidia's website, yet it was previously called Inspur, the same name used by its Chinese parent company. The name change came after Inspur-related firms were added to a federal blocklist in March 2023. Airvres reportedly exported at least $5.6 billion of chips to Southeast Asia between April 2024 and February 2026, including Nvidia's top-of-the-line Blackwell silicon. The chips went to two companies in Malaysia, one of which was Alice Huang's Megaspeed.

#10190. How to Build Conference Talk Slides With AI Without Looking Generic

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Conference talks fail on slides more often than on content. The speaker knows the material and has something worth saying, but the deck is a wall of bullets, or a template everyone in the room has seen before, or forty slides for a twenty-minute slot. AI presentation makers can now produce a complete deck from an outline in minutes, which solves the time problem and creates a new one: the output tends toward exactly the uniform, stock-image, bullet-heavy style that audiences have learned to tune out.

This article explains how conference slides differ from other decks, why AI-generated decks look generic and what specifically causes it, a workflow that uses an AI presentation maker for speed while keeping the talk distinctive, how to handle the slide types that matter most in a talk (the opening, the demonstration, the data, the close), and a checklist to run before the talk.

A conference talk is spoken, timed and performed to a room. That changes the rules compared with a reading deck or a business presentation.

The causes are specific and therefore fixable.

Template uniformity. Every slide uses the same layout family, so the deck has no visual rhythm. Audiences read the sameness as mass-produced.

Bullet default. Given a paragraph, the tool produces three to five bullets. Given twenty paragraphs, twenty bullet slides.

Stock imagery. Icons and photos chosen by keyword match the topic superficially and add no meaning. A talk about database indexing gets a picture of a filing cabinet.

Label titles. “Introduction”, “Background”, “Key Challenges”, “Conclusion”. Titles that name the category rather than make the point.

Flattened emphasis. The tool does not know which of your ideas is the one the audience should leave with, so every slide gets equal weight.

No pacing. Every slide carries the same amount of content, so there is no build, no pause, no payoff.

Each of these is a default that can be overridden, either in how the input is prepared or in a short editing pass afterwards.

Step 1: Write the talk before the slides. Not a script necessarily, but the argument: what the audience knows at the start, what they know at the end, and the four to six moves that get them there. Write the one sentence you want them to repeat to a colleague afterwards. If this is not clear, no deck will fix it.

Step 2: Write the slide titles as the things you will say. Each title is a sentence, in your voice, that states the point of that moment in the talk. “Indexes make reads fast by making writes slower” is a title. “Index trade-offs” is not. Read the titles in order; they should be the talk in miniature.

Step 3: Mark the pacing. Next to each title, note whether the slide is a hold (a key point, stays up while you talk for a minute or more), a beat (a quick slide in a sequence), or a reveal (something shown, a demo, a result). This tells you where to put effort.

Step 4: Generate. Paste the titles with one or two sentences of content under each into an ai presentation maker. Ask for a slide count close to your title count. Pick the plainest theme available; distinctiveness will come from your content, not the template.

Step 5: Strip the bullets. On every hold slide, delete the bullets and leave the title, or the title plus one image or one number. On beat slides, keep at most one short line. The content that was in the bullets goes into speaker notes, which is where it belonged.

Step 6: Replace every stock image. Delete icons. Replace keyword-matched photos with your own material: a screenshot, a diagram you drew, a chart from your data, a photograph you took, a single large word. If a slide has no genuine visual, a title alone on a plain background is better than a filler image.

Step 7: Build the reveals by hand. Demos, before-and-after comparisons, the key chart. These are the slides the audience will remember, and they need more care than a generator can give. See the section below.

Step 8: Break the layout rhythm deliberately. Make the two or three most important slides look different: full-bleed image, a single enormous number, a black slide with one line of white text. Contrast is what makes a moment land.

Step 9: Rehearse with a timer. The slide count will be wrong in the first rehearsal. Cut, merge or split until the pacing matches the time.

The opening. Not a title slide with your name and a logo while you say hello. Open on something that earns attention: a surprising number, a question, a screenshot of the problem, a single provocative sentence. Your name and affiliation can come on slide two, or be said rather than shown.

The problem statement. One slide, one sentence, stays up while you explain why the audience should care. No bullets.

The demonstration. If the talk has a demo, record it. Live demos fail in rooms with unreliable connections and unfamiliar projectors. A recorded demo with the audio off, narrated live, gives the same effect with none of the risk. If you must go live, have the recording ready as a fallback.

The data. One chart per slide, one message per chart, the message in the title. Strip gridlines, legends and secondary series. Highlight the one line or bar that matters in colour and grey out the rest. Label directly. Type large enough to read from the back.

The “so what.” After the evidence, a slide that states what it means in one sentence. This is a hold slide; give it time.

The close. The one sentence you want them to repeat, alone on the final slide. Not “Thank you” or “Questions?”; those can be said. The last thing on screen while you take questions should be your point.

Because the slides are now sparse, two things need handling.

First, the speaker notes must carry what you intend to say, in cue form: the key phrases, the number you must not forget, the transition to the next slide. The generator will have put the stripped bullet content there; edit it into cues rather than prose.

Second, the shared deck. Attendees who download a sparse deck afterwards get little from it. Options: export with notes visible; publish a companion blog post or document; or build a second “reading” version with more text on each slide. Many speakers do the first and link to a post.

For a twenty-minute talk, fifteen to twenty-five slides is typical once hold and beat slides are counted. For a forty-five-minute talk, thirty to fifty. Build-up sequences of several quick beats inflate the count without inflating the time, which is fine. What is not fine is a deck of forty dense slides for twenty minutes; that is a talk that will run over and leave the audience behind.

Rehearse at least twice with a timer, once alone and once with a listener. Cut the content you rush. Nobody has ever complained that a talk finished two minutes early.

Lightning talks (five to ten minutes). Five to eight slides, almost all holds. One idea, one piece of evidence, one takeaway. Cut everything else.

Workshops and tutorials. More slides, more text, because attendees will refer back and follow along. The reading-deck rules apply more here; still, keep each step to one slide.

Panel contributions. Often no slides at all, or two or three for a specific point. Resist bringing a full deck.

Keynotes. Fewer slides, longer holds, more full-bleed images and single sentences. The speaker carries almost everything; the slides punctuate.

Remote talks. The screen is small and the audience is distracted. Larger type still, more frequent slide changes to hold attention, and a recorded demo is essential because screen-sharing a live one adds another failure point.

How much of the deck should the AI tool produce? The structural first draft: layouts, consistency, speaker notes populated. Expect to edit every slide and rebuild the key ones.

Is it acceptable to have slides with only a title? Yes, and it is often the strongest format for a spoken talk. The audience listens instead of reading.

What about code on slides? Short excerpts only, in a large monospace type, with the relevant lines highlighted and the rest dimmed. Anything longer belongs in the companion post.

Should I use animations? Simple builds (revealing elements in sequence) help pacing. Transitions and effects distract. Keep to the former.

How do I make the deck distinctive if I cannot design? Your material is what makes it distinctive: your screenshots, your data, your diagrams, your sentences. Design restraint plus real content beats decorated templates every time.

#10188. GTA VI’s pretentious puzzler makes indie developers feel ‘part of larger culture’

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tenebrae Lux is a gag that has inspired some larger conversations about the gap between indie games and big-budget blockbusters.

Tenebrae Lux is a gag that has inspired some larger conversations about the gap between indie games and big-budget blockbusters.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One of the most intriguing games of the year is called Tenebrae Lux. It’s a moody, black-and-white title with obtuse puzzles and a main character that constantly narrates to himself with dramatic language. Its credits attribute pretty much everything to a developer named Johannes Zuch. It’s generated a lot of discussion for seemingly mocking games like Jonathan Blow’s The Witness or The Chinese Room’s Dear Esther. It’s also a game that only exists inside of Grand Theft Auto VI.

Developer Rockstar Games has stuffed a lot of satirical media inside of GTA VI, including TV shows you can watch, books you can read, and radio shows you can listen to. It’s all in service of making the world feel like a funhouse-mirror version of our current reality — Ted Lasso star Jason Sudeikis plays a right-wing radio host, for example. There are more games you can play inside GTA VI in addition to Tenebrae Lux, including a zombie-themed roguelike. But Tenebrae Lux, which you have to see as part of GTA VI’s main story, is the one that has struck a chord, including with indie developers like Sam Barlow and Robert Yang.

“I have to admit it’s kind of funny for the biggest blockbuster game of all time to go after arthouse indie,” Yang tells The Verge, noting that it’s “like a fighter jet getting obsessed with an ant, petty in a funny way.” (Given that GTA VI earned $3.79 billion in its first day, the gulf between Rockstar and indie game studios that are struggling to get by is vast.)

But Yang suggested that Rockstar developers are probably well versed in the types of games they’re lampooning in GTA VI. “I don’t think gamers understand how much indie experimental stuff any decent AAA dev plays,” Yang said, pointing out that Rockstar’s environmental artists and level designers “all probably love Dear Esther,” the influential first-person game that helped popularize the “walking sim” concept when it launched in 2012.

“I was mostly taken with what it meant that the millions of people playing the game were having to sit down and play through an extended gag about indie games,” Barlow tells The Verge. “That made me feel like we were part of larger culture.” He added that he hopes some of these players might “seek out what these arty indie games might be.”

Rockstar is self-aware enough to use its video games to poke fun at the medium itself. As part of a long conversation between protagonist Jason Duval and his friend Cal Hampton before they start playing Tenebrae Lux, Cal hypes the game by saying that none of it is “just sitting around waiting for people to finish talking.” Initially, you only play Tenebrae Lux for a few minutes, which is enough time to get stumped on the initial puzzles, before the game cuts back to Jason being bored. “Got anything else, a little less, uh… y’know?” he asks. They play the zombie game, titled Street Trash 3 Dezombifier, right after. (You can go back and play the entirety of Tenebrae Lux later if you want.)

Barlow said that the cut “felt very true to the character and story. I gather there is more to the game, but it felt natural to me to hit a wall with the pretentious puzzle design and then move on!”

Yang also pointed out that from a design perspective, GTA VI has more in common with Tenebrae Lux than Street Trash 3 Dezombifier. GTA VI is “basically a walking sim where you walk around Florida and listen to people talk,” Yang said. “What’s the difference? Well, just like a billion dollars I guess.”

Soldering is a skill that every hobbyist should master. It doesn't matter whether you're taking your first steps or you're a seasoned pro, because you're going to need an essential bit of kit: a soldering iron.

Luckily, we've put together a good selection of soldering iron deals that are on sale for Black Friday. This is the perfect chance to grab the bull by the horns and upgrade with an iron that will last you through plenty of hefty projects. We've also got recommendations for solder, as well as kit to keep your workbench safe from fumes and heat.

Check out the full list of suggestions below.

The Fnirsi HS-01 is a great soldering iron for anyone who travels or who needs a soldering iron on the go, as our review makes clear.

It's USB-C with an included power supply, or you can use it with a power bank capable of 65W output. It's good for easy soldering, eliminating the need to search for a DC jack or outlet. The flexibility of both a DC and USB-C jack is nice to have, although with so many USB-C PD power banks on sale now, it's a luxury, not a necessity.

The Fnirsi HS-01 is a great soldering iron and well worth the money for those who solder in workshops or in the field.

This kit comes with the soldering iron, but there's also a 96W power supply, a high-quality USB-C cable, a stand, and a selection of soldering iron tips for precision soldering, along with larger tips for when you need to lay down the heat.

Controlling the Fnirsi HS-01 is all about the buttons, which let you control the temperature, as well as navigate the built-in OLED screen. Set your temperature, and the HS-01 will get up to the target temperature in seconds. It's also designed to let you switch holding positions if you're right or left-handed.

A great selection of soldering tips, a large LCD screen, clear controls and a bonus hot air rework wand make the Yihua 995D+ the best option for those who solder for a living.

The Yihua 995D+ can reach temperatures of 480 °C (896 °F), which gives more than enough heat to solder and reflow even the heaviest solder joint. This soldering station has excellent thermal properties, letting you start soldering speaker wire with ease.

The stand is compatible with right and left-handed users, as it's separated from the main station, so you'll have free access to the soldering iron while reducing the number of cables trailing across your workbench. It has three preset channels to let you switch between jobs quickly, and it feels great to handle, thanks to a non-slip coating and flexible silicone cord to stop any melting accidents. You've also got a good number of tips, from precision tips to heavy tips for large projects.

The hot air gun has a similar cable for protection and is easy to handle. You'll need to select the correct controls before using the central knob to handle temperature and airflow. After that, it's easy to control both.

The Yihua 995D+ is an excellent soldering station for both beginners and pros alike. It gets the job done, looks good, and keeps more cash in your pocket.

The five flexible alligator clips are used to clamp and hold circuit boards a little nearer to the user. If you need more rigidity, the six posts are used to clamp to the edge of the board you're soldering, which is useful if you're soldering just one side. This is ideal for a maker with plenty of tasks on his sheet.

You'll want lead-free solder if you're planning on soldering. It's the gold standard, it's safer, and it avoids the environmental issues that come with traditional leaded solder.

Lead-free solder is the gold standard these days. Any modern electronics will have components soldered using lead-free solder. It's safer than traditional leaded, and avoids the environmental issues, such as lead leakage during bad disposal. It's the safest option if you're planning on making anything you go on to sell, too.

Lead-free solder is better for the environment, and it means you can build and sell products that are made using it.

This 0.6mm diameter solder has a flux rosin core so it flows neatly around your solder joints.

That said, there's still a case for leaded solder, especially if you're using it for yourself (and you take precautions).

Lead solder is still great for personal use, and this 0.8mm rosin core 63/38 tin / lead mix will melt great and produce shiny and reliable solder joints.

This silicone repair mat will keep your workbench free from scratches and soldering iron burns. The included magnetic notches and slots will make sure every screw goes back in at the end.

Silicone soldering mats are an essential buy for a hobbyist. They'll keep the heat from your soldering iron away from the workbench, and often have extra positions to keep screws in one place and to stop magnetics from falling off your bench.

Good for taking apart a laptop, for instance. It'll keep your screws safe, the bench burn-free, and it'll help you from scratching the chassis, too. When you're done, you can just roll it up and put it away.

Lead-free or not, you're going to see fumes when you're soldering. It isn't a great idea to breathe these in, so a fume extractor can help you to improve the air quality around you as you work.

Keep your lungs healthy by removing the flux fumes and solder smoke from the air. This tiltable solder fume extractor works from up to five inches away and pulls the offensive fumes through an activated carbon filter to keep the air fresh. You get extra filters in the box

Extractors work, as you'd expect, by using a fan to suck smoke and fumes from the air, directly into an activated carbon filter, with clean air passing through the back of it. This extractor uses a high-speed fan to do it, keeping high suction from up to five inches away: plenty of space for you to solder in comfort.

This extractor comes with four rubber feet, so it won't move around, and the locking tilt mechanism helps you point it to the right places as you work. It also has fenders on both sides of the fan to stop any breezes from moving fumes away from the unit, as it keeps you safe.

#10179. Why the Agent Isn’t the Hard Part: Where Developers Go Wrong With Agentic Workflows

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can build an AI agent in an afternoon now. Pick a model, write a loop, hand it a few tools and watch it book a meeting or open a pull request. The demo works, and everyone in the room is impressed.

Then it meets a real system with real permissions and real customers, and things get quiet. Gartner has predicted that over 40% of agentic AI projects will be canceled by the end of 2027, citing rising costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Its analyst Anushree Verma described most current projects as early-stage experiments that are driven by hype and often misapplied. A forecast is not a measurement, but the pattern it describes matches what many teams report: the agent is rarely what breaks. The workflow around it is.

This article covers where that workflow tends to fail, with a focus on permissions, tool connections and human approval. It includes a small, runnable policy gate in JavaScript, compares the main safeguards and notes their limits. Standards and guidance change quickly in this area, so check the linked sources for the current versions.

A year or two ago the model was often the differentiator. Today many teams can reach the same frontier models through the same kinds of APIs, so if the intelligence is similar, the advantage has to come from somewhere else.

Anthropic’s guide to building effective agents reached a similar conclusion after working with teams building agents. It reports that the most successful implementations were not using complex frameworks or specialized libraries, and were building with simple, composable patterns instead. It also separates workflows, where code paths are predefined, from agents, where the model chooses its own steps, and advises using the simplest approach that works and adding complexity only when measurement shows it helps.

That should change where developers spend their time. Frameworks matter, but three less glamorous questions matter more: what can the agent touch, when does it stop, and who checks its work?

The OWASP guidance on Excessive Agency names what goes wrong here. It covers agents that can do more than they need to, and it traces the problem to three root causes: excessive functionality, excessive permissions and excessive autonomy.

In practice this looks familiar. An agent that only needs to read tickets is given a token that can also close them. A summarization tool connects with an admin account because that was quicker to set up. A generic “run shell command” tool is exposed when the agent only ever needs to write one file.

The fix is ordinary engineering discipline applied to a new kind of caller. OWASP’s recommendations include giving each tool only the functions and permissions it needs, preferring narrow tools over open-ended ones, running actions under the user’s own scoped identity, requiring human approval for high-impact actions and enforcing authorization in the downstream system rather than letting the model decide. The last point is the important one: a model can be talked out of a rule written in a prompt, but an API that checks scopes cannot.

Make the risky properties of a tool explicit in its definition so your orchestration layer can act on them:

Nothing clever is happening there, which is the point. The decision about what needs a human is made by a developer at design time, not by a model at runtime.

The definitions above only help if something enforces them. The sketch below is a policy gate that sits between the model and your tools. It checks that the tool exists, that the caller was granted the scope, and whether the action is reversible, and it records each decision. It runs on Node.js 18 or later with no dependencies.

A short test file shows the behavior. Unknown tools and missing scopes are denied, reads run, and the irreversible send waits for a person:

This is deliberately minimal. A real system would load scopes from the user’s actual session, enforce the same scopes again in the downstream API, and store the audit log somewhere durable. The gate is a second line of defense, not a replacement for authorization in the systems the agent calls.

The Model Context Protocol has become a common way to connect agents to tools. Anthropic donated MCP to the Agentic AI Foundation, a directed fund under the Linux Foundation, in December 2025. Block contributed goose and OpenAI contributed AGENTS.md as founding projects, which shows how central the ecosystem has become.

A standard protocol is good news. It also means you can install a tool server in minutes and expose every endpoint of your issue tracker, repository or CRM to your agent, whether it needs them or not. Easy connection quietly pushes more power to the agent.

The protocol’s own security best practices document covers several relevant risks, including confused deputy problems in proxy servers, token passthrough, local server compromise and scope minimization. For scopes, it recommends starting with a minimal set and elevating only when a privileged operation is first attempted, and it lists wildcard or omnibus scopes as a common mistake.

A sensible rule is to treat every tool server like a third-party dependency running with production credentials. Review the tools it exposes, switch off the ones the workflow does not use, pin versions and log every call. If you would not let a new contractor do it on day one, the agent should not do it either.

Human-in-the-loop design is often implemented as “approve everything”. That fails in a predictable way: people click approve forty times a day and stop reading by lunchtime.

A better rule is to gate on reversibility. Reads run freely, reversible writes run and get logged, and irreversible actions such as sending, paying or deleting wait for a person. Everything leaves a trace that a developer can replay when something looks odd. The gate above implements exactly this split.

Some teams build these pieces themselves, and others buy them. Elsewhen, a London-based consultancy that works on agentic operations for enterprise teams, describes its own service in similar terms. Its page lists approval gates where people sign off consequential decisions, an audit trail of what each agent read and which systems it touched, and a shared layer of permissions and guardrails that is reused across workflows. Elsewhen also says its Planet Agent report found that agents tend to start at the edges of an organization, where tasks are repeatable and mistakes are cheap, and then move toward the core. Treat that as the vendor’s own finding, and ask for the underlying evidence before relying on it.

The same logic applies outside engineering. The first question worth asking any AI partner is what they have actually put into production, and developers can ask the same of their own work.

No single control is enough. The table summarizes what each safeguard does well and where it falls short.

It is the OWASP term for an agent that can do more than it needs to, caused by excessive functionality, permissions or autonomy.

A prompt rule can be bypassed or ignored, while a scope check in the downstream API is enforced regardless of what the model says.

Usually not. Gating on reversibility keeps humans focused on actions such as sending, paying and deleting, and avoids approval fatigue.

Treat them like any third-party dependency. Review the tools they expose, limit scopes, pin versions and log calls.

The agentic projects that survive the next two years will probably not be the ones with the most ambitious autonomy. They will be the ones where someone sat down early and decided what the agent could touch, what it had to ask about and how anyone would know what it did.

That work does not demo well and it will not trend on social media. It is still the part that decides whether your agent becomes infrastructure or ends up among the projects that get cancelled.

#10176. GTA VI PC recompilation project shut down by Take-Two

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Modders are racing to get Grand Theft Auto VI running on PC.

Modders are racing to get Grand Theft Auto VI running on PC.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Game modding communities are racing to get Grand Theft Auto VI running on PC, but one big effort has been taken down by Take-Two today. Leonida, a fan project by 15-year-old “NotRexed,” has vanished after a DMCA takedown notice from GTA VI publisher Take-Two.

Leonida was designed to translate a leaked GTA VI Xbox build to run natively on Windows. Cyberleek, the leaker who posted multiple GTA VI gameplay videos over the summer, published a full Xbox build of the game earlier this week. The leak has kicked recompilation efforts up a notch, as modders race to reverse-engineer the game’s code and rebuild it into native PC code.

Some have managed to get the game to boot on PC, but recompiling the full game won’t be easy — even with help from AI tools like Claude. Take-Two is trying to slow this progress, and its IP enforcement team targeted the main GitHub repository for Leonida and more than 2,900 forks of it, claiming that it “infringes upon Take-Two’s copyrighted works and facilitates copyright infringement of Rockstar Games title, Grand Theft Auto VI.”

PortWatch VI is tracking the efforts to recompile, emulate, or port the leaked Xbox build over to PC. At the time of publishing, the VIctory project and forks of it are leading the way. People are also trying to use XCL, a compatibility layer that runs Xbox Series X games on a Windows PC, to get GTA VI running.

At this point, it seems like Take-Two might not be able to avoid the inevitable here. The clock started ticking down to the release of the PC version last week, and if recent reports are true, we might see it in a few months. If GTA VI is close to being officially ready for PC, we could hear about a release before modders manage to get the game running on PC unofficially.

Following his recent resignation from the company and his return as CEO earlier this week, Jensen Huang has received a glowing statement of support from Denny's, the chain where he famously began his working life before founding Nvidia. #10167. The Motorola Signature 27’s haptics finally feel more like a Pixel, and it’s about time

Motorola has been content with mid-grade haptics for years, but the Motorola Signature 27’s haptics rightfully upgrade the experience.

Two days ago, I unboxed the Motorola Signature 27 and talked about my experience a bit in my Motorola Signature 27 hands-on. It’s as gorgeous as the pictures make it look, and the texture on the back of my “Capulet Olive” green colorway is the stuff of dreams. But the thing that sealed the new ultra-high-quality design for me wasn’t the comfortable curved edges or the textile back: it was the haptic motors.

Now, don’t get me wrong. The Motorola Signature 27’s build and design are probably my favorite of the year. I’ll need to use it for just a bit longer to be 100% sure, but the pebble-like aesthetic of the curved front, back, and edges feels tangibly amazing to hold and use. It reminds me a bit of the Galaxy S3’s design, just without the nasty, slimy plastic back. It’s curved just enough to feel amazing, but not enough to be an annoyance like some waterfall display phones were in the past.

But a haptic motor is completely intangible to you as the reader. You can’t see it in pictures, and even if I describe it, you may not fully understand why it makes such a difference. If you’ve been using Google Pixel phones for a long time, you’ve probably forgotten how good a quality haptic motor can feel, but let me remind you for a moment just how important such an invisible thing can be to completing the experience.

Typing on the Signature 27 is pure bliss because of it. Tapping each key feels more like the press of a physical keyboard key. Unlocking the phone emits a satisfying light punch to my thumb, signalling a successful read. When I send a reply on X (Twitter), the button tap and subsequent animation feel like the message is being sent through the phone onto the web. It’s wonderful, and it’s one of the reasons people love the Pixel experience so much.

Unfortunately, the Motorola Signature 27’s haptic responses aren’t applied to as many parts of the UI as Pixel phones do, and that’s where the real miss comes in. Adjusting the brightness or volume doesn’t feel like a physically rotating vertical dial the way it does on Pixel, and I’d love to see Motorola add this in an update. But at least the upgraded haptic engine is a huge step in the right direction for Motorola and a good way to make the Signature 27 feel like a proper flagship phone.

#10164. A Practical Guide to Browser Developer Tools for Debugging and Improving Websites

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a website involves more than writing HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. Developers also need to test how their code behaves in different browsers, identify layout problems, investigate JavaScript errors, and understand why certain pages take longer to load.

Browser developer tools make these tasks easier. They provide a way to inspect the code currently running in a browser without constantly changing files or guessing what might be wrong.

Whether you're developing a new website or maintaining an existing application, learning to use these tools can improve your debugging workflow and help you write more reliable code.

Let's explore some useful browser development tools and how to apply them to everyday web development tasks.

Browser developer tools, commonly called DevTools, are built-in utilities available in modern browsers such as Google Chrome, Mozilla Firefox, Microsoft Edge, and Safari.

They allow developers to examine a webpage's HTML structure, modify CSS properties, debug JavaScript, inspect network requests, and test responsive layouts.

You can usually open them by right-clicking a webpage and selecting Inspect. On Windows and Linux, the F12 key or Ctrl + Shift + I shortcut also opens DevTools in most browsers.

The following table highlights some commonly used features:

Understanding what each panel does makes it easier to select the right tool for a particular problem.

The Elements panel is one of the most frequently used features in browser DevTools.

It displays the Document Object Model (DOM), which represents the structure of the webpage currently loaded in the browser. Developers can select individual elements and examine the CSS rules affecting their appearance.

For example, imagine that a heading has more spacing than expected. Instead of immediately editing the stylesheet, inspect the heading and review its computed styles.

You might discover that the spacing comes from a margin inherited through another CSS rule or from a conflicting selector.

You can also experiment with CSS properties directly inside DevTools.

If the card appears too wide, you can temporarily change its width or padding in the Styles panel.

The browser immediately displays the result, allowing you to compare different values without repeatedly editing and saving your stylesheet.

However, these temporary changes do not automatically update your original CSS files. Once you've identified the correct solution, apply it to your project source code.

For more detailed CSS debugging techniques, developers can also read SitePoint's guide on CSS Debugging and Optimization: Browser-based Developer Tools.

JavaScript errors can prevent interactive features from working correctly. A navigation menu might stop opening, a form might fail to submit, or a button might not trigger the expected action.

The Console panel helps developers investigate these problems.

For example, consider the following JavaScript:

If the page does not contain an element with the ID submitButton, the selected value will be null. Calling addEventListener() on that value causes an error.

You can prevent this particular issue by checking whether the element exists:

The Console panel also allows developers to execute JavaScript expressions manually.

This returns the number of image elements currently present in the document.

You can use similar expressions to inspect elements, check values, and test small pieces of functionality.

Sometimes a JavaScript error is not immediately visible. A function may execute successfully but produce an unexpected result.

In such cases, the Sources panel in Chrome DevTools or the Debugger panel in Firefox can help.

A breakpoint pauses JavaScript execution at a specific line. Developers can then inspect variable values, examine the call stack, and execute the code step by step.

If the application displays an incorrect total, you can place a breakpoint inside calculateTotal().

When the function executes, the browser pauses and allows you to inspect the values of price, quantity, and total.

This is more useful than repeatedly adding console.log() statements throughout a large application.

Breakpoints are particularly helpful when debugging complex functions, asynchronous operations, and event handlers.

A webpage may contain several resources, including images, stylesheets, JavaScript files, fonts, and API requests.

If one of these resources fails to load, part of the website may stop working correctly.

The Network panel provides information about requests made by the browser.

It displays request URLs, HTTP status codes, response sizes, loading times, and other useful details.

For example, the following table explains some common HTTP responses developers may encounter:

Suppose an image is missing from a product page. Open the Network panel, reload the page, and filter the requests by images.

If the image returns a 404 response, check whether the file path is correct and whether the resource exists on the server.

The same process can help investigate failed API requests.

For example, if a frontend application requests product information from an API, the Network panel allows you to inspect the request method, response status, and returned data.

This helps developers distinguish between frontend problems and backend errors.

A website that looks correct on a desktop computer may not work properly on a smartphone.

Text can overflow, navigation menus can become difficult to use, and images may extend beyond their containers.

Browser DevTools include responsive design and device emulation features that allow developers to test different viewport sizes.

In Chrome, you can enable the device toolbar from the DevTools interface. Firefox provides a similar feature called Responsive Design Mode.

The desktop layout displays three columns, while smaller screens display one column.

You can test this behavior by changing the viewport width inside DevTools.

A useful responsive testing process includes:

Checking common mobile and tablet widths.

Testing both portrait and landscape orientations.

Checking whether navigation remains usable.

Verifying that buttons and links are easy to interact with.

Device emulation is useful during development, but it does not completely replace testing on physical devices.

A website can have correct HTML, CSS, and JavaScript but still provide a poor experience if it loads slowly or performs unnecessary work.

The Performance panel helps developers investigate browser activity over time.

It can record page interactions and provide information about scripting, rendering, painting, and other browser operations.

For example, a page might become unresponsive when a user opens a navigation menu. A performance recording can help identify whether a long-running JavaScript function is blocking the main thread.

Developers can also use tools such as Lighthouse to audit performance, accessibility, SEO, and other aspects of a webpage.

Instead of making random optimization changes, use the available measurements to identify the actual problem.

Some useful areas to investigate include:

Performance analysis should be repeated after making changes to determine whether the optimization actually improves the result.

These features are useful, but they can also introduce debugging challenges.

For example, an application might continue displaying outdated information because an old value remains stored in local storage.

The Application panel in Chrome DevTools allows developers to inspect and manage several types of browser storage.

If the application behaves unexpectedly because of stored data, inspecting local storage can help identify the problem.

Be careful when clearing storage because doing so may remove authentication sessions, saved preferences, or other application data.

Knowing individual DevTools features is useful, but developers benefit even more from using them in a consistent order.

The following workflow can make troubleshooting more efficient:

This process helps developers identify the cause of a problem before changing multiple parts of the application.

It also makes debugging easier when working with other developers because the issue can be reproduced and explained using specific observations.

Browser developer tools are essential for modern web development. They help developers inspect HTML and CSS, debug JavaScript, analyze network requests, test responsive layouts, and investigate performance issues.

The most effective approach is to select the appropriate tool for the problem instead of making changes based on assumptions.

Whether you're working on a small website or a complex web application, becoming familiar with DevTools can make your development workflow more efficient and help you build websites that are easier to maintain.

Rather than treating debugging as something that happens only after development, make it a regular part of your workflow. This allows you to identify problems earlier, verify your solutions, and deliver more reliable web experiences.

#10155. How to Add Idempotency Keys to a Node.js API with PostgreSQL

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A mobile app sends POST /orders. The server creates the order, but the response is lost when the phone switches from Wi-Fi to 4G. The app sees a timeout and retries. Now the customer has two orders, and your support team has a refund to process.

Retrying is the right thing for a client to do. The problem is that POST is not idempotent: RFC 9110 defines GET, PUT and DELETE as idempotent methods, but not POST or PATCH. Repeating a POST can repeat its side effects.

An idempotency key fixes this. The client generates a unique key for each logical operation and sends it with every attempt. The server does the work once, stores the response against the key, and returns the stored response for any retry. The IETF HTTPAPI working group is standardizing this pattern as the Idempotency-Key HTTP header field, and this tutorial follows its recommendations.

We'll build it with Node.js, Express 5 and PostgreSQL. By the end you'll have a table that stores keys, a function that safely claims a key under concurrent requests, an Express route that replays stored responses, and a client that retries without creating duplicates.

The client's job is simple: generate a key, usually a UUID, and send the same key on every retry of the same operation. The draft defines the header value as a quoted string:

The server's job is harder, because every request falls into one of five cases:

To tell case 3 from case 4, the server stores a fingerprint of the request alongside the key. A matching fingerprint means a genuine retry; a different one means key reuse. Case 5 is the one most implementations get wrong, because it only shows up under concurrency. A database constraint handles it for us.

Create a project and install the two dependencies:

The key table records who sent the key, the request fingerprint, whether the work has finished, and the response to replay:

Two details matter here. The primary key includes client_id, so keys are scoped per client: two customers who happen to generate the same value can't see each other's responses. And locked_until lets the server recover a key whose request crashed halfway, which we'll use in Step 3.

The draft defines the header value as a structured field string, which means it arrives wrapped in double quotes. Many clients send it without quotes, so the parser below accepts both and limits the characters and length it will store:

The fingerprint has to be identical for identical requests, even if a client serializes the JSON body with its properties in a different order. Sorting object keys before hashing takes care of that. The hash uses SHA-256 from Node's built-in crypto module:

Including the method and path means a key reused on a different endpoint counts as a mismatch, not as a retry.

The tempting approach is to SELECT the key, and insert it if nothing comes back. Under concurrency that fails: two retries arriving a few milliseconds apart both see no row, and both do the work.

Instead, let the primary key decide. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING either inserts the row or does nothing, atomically, even across many application processes. The PostgreSQL INSERT documentation describes this behavior in detail. If the insert returns a row, this request owns the key. If not, we read the existing row and decide which case we're in:

The UPDATE in the last step is also atomic. If two retries find the same expired lock, only one of them matches the locked_until < now() condition and gets the key. If the row disappears between the two queries, for example because it was released after a failure, the function reports in_progress and the client simply retries.

Set LOCK_SECONDS comfortably above the slowest time your operation takes. If the lock expires while the original request is still running, a retry can take over and run the work a second time.

Once a request owns the key, it creates the order and marks the key as completed. Both writes happen in one transaction, so there is no moment where the order exists but the key still says in_progress, or the other way round. Note that a transaction in node-postgres has to use a single client checked out from the pool, as the node-postgres transaction documentation explains:

releaseKey runs when the work fails. Deleting the claim lets the client retry with the same key instead of waiting for the lock to expire. It only deletes rows that are still in progress, so a completed response is never thrown away.

The route validates the body first. A request with invalid input never touches the key table, so the client can fix the input and resend with the same key. Error responses use the application/problem+json format from RFC 9457, which the idempotency draft also uses in its examples:

The server side only works if the client keeps the key stable. The most common client bug is generating a new key inside the retry loop, which turns every retry into a new operation. Generate the key once per logical action, before the first attempt:

The catch branch is the reason idempotency keys exist. After a timeout, the client can't know whether the order was created. With a stable key, retrying is safe either way: if the first attempt succeeded, the retry returns the stored order; if it didn't, the retry creates it. The random jitter spreads retries out, so many clients recovering from the same outage don't all hit the server at once.

In a browser or mobile app, store the key with the pending action, for example in the form state or a local queue. If the user taps "Place order" again after an error, reuse it. Only generate a new key when the user starts a genuinely new action.

Keys can't live forever, or the table grows without limit. The draft leaves the retention period to the server but says it should be published in the API documentation, so clients know how long a retry stays safe. Twenty-four hours covers most retry scenarios. A scheduled job can delete older rows in batches:

Run it every few minutes from cron or your job scheduler until it deletes zero rows. Deleting in batches keeps each transaction short, and the created_at index from Step 1 keeps the inner query fast. A retry that arrives after its key has expired is treated as a new request, which is why the retention period belongs in your documentation.

Start the server with node server.js, then send the same request twice:

The second request lists the properties in a different order, and it's still recognized as a retry because of the canonical fingerprint. Both responses carry the same order ID, and the orders table contains one row.

Now change the quantity while keeping the key. The server answers 422, because the key now belongs to a different request:

To see the concurrent case, add await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 3000)); before completeKey in the route, restart the server, and fire two requests with a new key at the same time:

One request prints 201 and the other 409. Without the ON CONFLICT claim, both would have printed 201 and created two orders. Remove the delay when you're done.

This design guarantees that the database work in Step 4 happens once per key. Two situations need extra care:

Idempotency keys don't replace other safeguards either. A unique constraint on a natural business key, such as one payment per invoice, still catches duplicates that arrive with different keys, for example when a user submits the same form from two tabs.

With one table, an atomic claim and a transaction that stores the response next to the work, POST endpoints become safe to retry. Clients get a simple rule: generate one key per action, reuse it on every retry. The server takes care of the rest, including the concurrent retries that a SELECT-then-INSERT approach misses.

The same pattern works for PATCH endpoints and for webhooks you send to other systems. Start with the endpoints where a duplicate costs real money or confuses a customer, and publish your key format and retention period in the API documentation so client developers know what to send.

#10147. How SD Card Photo and Video Recovery Works: A Practical Guide With a JPEG Carving Example

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

For photographers, videographers and content creators, an SD card is more than a storage device. It holds the raw material behind a finished project: wedding photographs, travel footage, product demos and drone recordings that may be impossible to recreate. Data loss can happen at almost any stage of that workflow, from a folder deleted to make space to a card formatted before the footage was backed up.

This article explains why files on a card become unavailable, what to do in the first minutes, how recovery tools find data, and how to compare options. It includes a small Python example that recovers JPEG files from a disk image, so you can see the mechanism for yourself. It also compares free open-source tools with paid graphical ones, using criteria you can apply yourself. Recovery is never guaranteed, and tool features change, so confirm details against each vendor’s current documentation.

Recovery is not just a matter of finding a deleted filename. Several different problems can make files unavailable, and they call for different approaches.

These steps cannot guarantee recovery, but they reduce avoidable risk.

Most tools combine two methods, and understanding them helps you read scan results.

Carving has a cost. Because file system information is ignored, names and folder structure are usually lost, which is why scan results often show generic names. Fragmented files can also be recovered incomplete, since the tool assumes the pieces are stored one after another. Free tools build on these same ideas. Wondershare Recoverit V15, which also offers a free version for Windows and Mac, adds a graphical interface, while PhotoRec and ddrescue stay closer to the command line.

The script below is a teaching sketch, not a recovery product. It reads a raw disk image, finds JPEG start markers, and writes out the bytes up to the last end marker before the next start. That detail matters because a JPEG can contain an embedded thumbnail with its own markers, so stopping at the first end marker would cut a file short.

A short test builds two real JPEGs, hides them in random bytes, and checks that both are carved and open as valid images:

Use it on an image rather than the card itself. On Linux you can make one with a read-only copy, for example with the GNU ddrescue tool, which is designed to copy data from failing media. Then run the carver against the image and save the output to a different drive. Real tools handle many more formats, trim files using header information, and deal with video containers, so treat this as a way to understand the idea.

The right choice depends on the problem, your platform and how comfortable you are with the command line. The table summarizes what each project’s own documentation states.

The order of steps matters more than the choice of tool. A cautious workflow on Linux or macOS looks like this:

Graphical tools follow the same logic and mainly change step 3, with thumbnails, filters and search that make large result sets easier to browse. Whether that is worth paying for depends on how many files you need to sift through and how comfortable you are on the command line. When comparing any tool, ask:

Features such as search by capture time, folder or AI-based tagging depend on metadata surviving the loss, and they narrow results without proving a file is intact. Treat them as convenience, not verification.

Recovering a video file does not necessarily mean recovering a playable video. A recording interrupted during writing can lack the structural information a player needs, and other files may be damaged in ways that block playback.

The distinction matters. Recovery retrieves missing or inaccessible data, while repair tries to fix damage that affects a file’s usability. Some cases need one, the other or both. Repair has limits too: if essential video data was overwritten or never recorded, software cannot recreate the original footage in full. After any recovery or repair, play the file all the way through and compare it with what you expect.

For a quick check of a recovered clip, ffprobe can show whether the container is readable:

An error or a missing duration suggests the file needs repair or is incomplete.

Often yes, if nothing has overwritten them. Stop using the card immediately and scan it or an image of it.

Not before recovery. Formatting changes file system structures and may make recovery harder.

Recovery retrieves missing data. Repair fixes a file that exists but will not open or play correctly.

Not always. PhotoRec and TestDisk are free and open source, while paid tools add a graphical interface and extras such as previews, filtering and video repair.

On a different drive from the card. Saving to the same card can overwrite data you still want.

SD card data loss creates two related challenges: getting files back, and working out which recovered files are useful. Stopping use of the card, working on an image and understanding the difference between file system recovery, carving and repair put you in a better position, whichever tool you choose. Graphical tools aim to make large result sets easier to navigate, and free tools such as PhotoRec and TestDisk remain solid options. Try your chosen tool on a copy first, verify what you recover, and keep a backup so you do not need to do this again.
