#10145. Taming Typography: Building a Modern CSS Reset

CSS can now make choices about type that used to sit outside a reset. text-wrap: pretty can prevent a stranded word, container query units can scale a heading to the column it occupies, and text-box can trim the half-leading built into a line box. Some of these features are experimental and browser support is uneven, so they need guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

I’ve put together tidy-typography.css, which gathers the useful parts into a default stylesheet: rendering and fallback rules, fluid sizing, vertical rhythm, wrapping, hyphenation, and OpenType detail.

Use it whole, or take the declaration that solves the particular irritation in front of you. I’ll walk you through all the details in this article and give you the full code at the end.

font-synthesis: none stops a browser from inventing bold or italic when the family lacks that face. The smoothing and text-rendering declarations are rendering hints, mainly for WebKit; results still vary by platform.

text-size-adjust disables mobile font inflation that can make a deliberately sized layout jump on rotation. font-size-adjust: from-font uses font metrics to reduce the apparent size change while a fallback swaps for a webfont.

(As with other snippets in this article, this ⁠:root block is a partial excerpt the final stylesheet combines it with additional custom properties defined later on.)

Viewport-based fluid type does not know how wide its element is. A heading in a narrow sidebar can therefore scale as though it fills the viewport.

container-type: inline-size makes the wrapper a container query, so its children can use cqi: a unit based on the container rather than the viewport. Replacing vw in clamp() lets the same heading scale appropriately in an article column or a 300px sidebar without component-specific media queries.

line-height includes space above the cap-height and below the baseline: half-leading. It becomes obvious when text must align tightly with another element. text-box-trim removes it, while text-box-edge measures from capital-letter caps to the alphabetic baseline. This is a metrics-aware alternative to font-specific negative-margin nudges.

Selectors Level 5 proposes :heading, matching h1 to h6 and [role="heading"]. It has not landed in browsers, so keep it behind an @supports selector() check.

Default heading wrapping can leave one short final line. text-wrap: balance distributes text across its lines and works best for blocks of up to about six lines. For paragraphs, text-wrap: pretty avoids a stranded final word without balancing the whole block. Both replace JavaScript orphan fixers. Editable text needs text-wrap: stable, which prevents each new word from reshuffling the paragraph.

hyphens: auto relies on the browser’s language dictionaries, so the document needs a correct lang attribute (for example ). Without one the browser has no dictionary to use and won’t be able to hyphenate text. Even with the correct lang, the defaults can leave tiny word fragments or several hyphenated lines in a row.

hyphenate-limit-chars controls the smallest breakable word and fragments; hyphenate-limit-lines prevents a ladder effect. hyphenate-limit-zone makes breaks less frequent, while hyphenate-limit-last protects the final word. Safari needs the prefixed equivalents, so declare both sets together.

Set code, variables, and headings to hyphens: manual: a broken code word can change meaning, and a hyphenated heading usually reads as an error.

Punctuation marks don’t carry the same visual weight as letters, so a quotation mark sitting at the start of a line can look slightly indented against the text below it. hanging-punctuation lets specified punctuation sit fractionally outside the text block’s edge.

The first value handles opening punctuation, and last handles closing punctuation at the end of a block, while allow-end lets punctuation hang past the right edge rather than force-wrapping. The result is a text block whose edges read as genuinely flush.

Underlines have a related problem. Browsers apply flat, one-size-fits-all thickness and position regardless of the font’s actual weight.

text-decoration-thickness: from-font reads the thickness the font’s designer intended. text-underline-offset gives descenders room to breathe, and text-decoration-skip-ink: auto interrupts the underline wherever a descender crosses it rather than drawing straight through the letter.

Variable fonts often include an optical size axis, or opsz. It lets a typeface use thinner strokes at large display sizes and thicker, more legible strokes when set small.

font-optical-sizing: auto lets the font make that adjustment based on its actual rendered size. The @supports block covers the older font-variation-settings syntax some browsers still need, while font-variant-ligatures and font-kerning enable the font’s own ligatures and kerning pairs.

Light text on a dark background genuinely reads heavier than the same weight on a light background. Where a font ships a GRAD, or grade, axis, the dark mode block dials that perceived weight down without changing glyph shape or width the way an actual weight change would. Fonts without a GRAD axis safely ignore the setting.

Mixing a monospace font into running prose often exposes a mismatched x-height at the same declared font-size. The size-adjust keyword, set once at the @font-face level, scales that font’s rendered output specifically, leaving the actual font-size value untouched.

Numbers in a table need identical widths so they stack into a clean column on the screen. Conversely, numbers in flowing text don’t need the same treatment and should sit naturally alongside the surrounding words.

The tabular-nums and lining-nums keywords give table digits a fixed width and full cap height. oldstyle-nums and proportional-nums let digits sit alongside lowercase letters without sticking up and disrupting the sentence’s texture.

font-variant-emoji makes the choice between full-colour and monochrome emoji explicit. Without it, the browser or platform gets to choose a fallback, and the result can vary more than you expect. Apply .emoji-color or .emoji-mono to containers where you want to enforce that choice, rather than setting it globally on the body.

Quote style and character order differ by language and region. English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention, and French uses guillemets with a thin space.

The quotes property declares the order for each language through :lang(). The native q element then inserts the right pair through open-quote and close-quote. The same markup can render correctly in each locale without hardcoding characters into the content.

Wrapping the rules in :where() keeps them at zero specificity, so a project can override the quote style with one plain class.

Default sub and sup styling is synthesized. The browser just shrinks the text and shifts the baseline, rather than using glyphs actually designed for that position.

font-variant-position switches to the font’s own purpose-built sub and superscript glyphs where they exist, resetting vertical-align, font-size, and line-height so they don’t fight against the real glyph’s intended size and position. Most typefaces only include proper numerals rather than letters, so check the specific font before applying this broadly.

:where() always resolves to zero specificity, which is useful for a reset. Every rule here is an opinionated default, and wrapping it in :where() lets a project override a property with a plain class.

:is() solves a different problem: repetition. The heading selector list appears several times in a typographic stylesheet, and grouping it avoids the risk of forgetting a level.

Unlike :where(), :is() carries the specificity of its most specific argument.

Here’s the complete reset, combining the stable layers. Unsupported declarations are ignored by the browser, so the stylesheet can be adopted gradually.

Much of this CSS removes work that used to leak into other parts of a build. text-wrap can take over orphan handling from JavaScript, container query units keep a component’s type scale tied to its own width, and text-box-trim avoids the negative margins once used to compensate for leading. quotes, font-variant-position, and font-optical-sizing hand several smaller details back to the language and the font instead of a collection of one-off fixes.

Browser support will continue to change, especially for text-wrap and text-box-trim. Unsupported declarations are ignored, but that only helps when the unenhanced typography is acceptable in its own right.

Some of these rules are still too new for a baseline reset. A news article, a marketing page, and an editable product interface do not ask the same things of their text; the set that belongs in yours depends on the font, the content, and the browsers you still need to support.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

"English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention,"

"English nests double quotes inside single quotes, American English reverses that convention (like it usually does),"

It’s nowhere near that cut-and-dried, and double has historically and geographically been much more popular. For some reason, UK publishing mostly switched to single for the 1900s, but they seem to be returning to double these days. Very few other countries followed them to single—Canada and Australia, for example, stuck with double.

A couple of links to start with (and you can find plenty more elsewhere): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quotation_mark#In_English, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quotation_marks_in_English#Quotations_and_speech

Overall, :lang(en) double-first with :lang(en-GB) single-first is a decent balance. If you had :lang(en) single-first, you’d want :lang(en-US, en-CA, en-IN, en-AU, en-NZ, …) for double-first.

A more accurate wording of the first sentence would be:

Quote style and character order differ by locale and house style.

I don’t understand the reason to use font-synthesis: none;. Isn’t synthesis just a fallback for when the author makes a mistake with the font faces or when the font file doesn’t load? Users’ fallback font-families likely have weight and style variants but probably not small-caps, maybe it at least should be allowed to be synthesized.

Instead of max-width: 65ch; being applied to p, li, dd, I’d consider applying it to p, dl, ol, ul. Limiting the width of the lists, rather than the list items, prevents longer items from “sticking out” relative to the paragraphs above and below, do to the items’ inline-start margins, padding.

The browser should automatically use French-style quotes (« ») within lang=fr; appears the British quote convention is not automatically used when lang=en-GB, at least not on my USian computer.

It might be worth noting more clearly that some of these are experimental/straight up don’t work today.

hanging-punctuation is Safari-only and doesn’t seem to support quotation marks in my testing, so the text is perhaps inaccurate on that point? Many of the hyphenation properties also only work in Safari, and require the prefixes described as “legacy” in the article.

It’s mentioned briefly in the intro that some stuff is experimental, but being more explicit in the sections about what does/doesn’t work currently would be great.

Definitely cool to explore the upcoming features, but feels a bit odd to frame it as a “reset” when it will likely change how text looks in the future in untested/unexpected ways, and includes superfluous code that won’t actually impact rendering today.

Limiting body, p, li and dd width is not the job of a reset. That’s a per-site choice and should be with other per-site css, not part of a reset block. And there’s lot’s of other very opinionated stuff as well. I’ll stick with my old friend Eric Meyer.

-webkit-font-smoothing: antialiased; is contentious (ill-advised); text-rendering: optimizeLegibility; is unwarranted; font-synthesis: none; has implications the typical user may not realise or like (though avoiding the need for synthesis is definitely preferable); font-size-adjust: from-font; has problematic side-effects, trading one problem for another that is in my opinion at least as bad; text-box is not widely supported and has layout implications that mean your layout will be somewhat broken in other browsers; Given the preceding ruleset, [contenteditable="true"] { text-wrap: stable; } doesn’t work if the content is within an inner p, li, dd (which it typically will be); Hyphenation is just all-round dodgy and generally better avoided; Hanging punctuation is contentious and current implementations are really not very pleasing; Turning contextual alternatives on generally is not necessarily a good idea—there’s a reason it’s opt-in and separated from the normal set of ligatures; font-kerning: normal; is ill-advised: let the browser decide, if it decides not to do kerning, it’ll be for a good reason; font-variant-position: [sub|super]; is a terrible idea when combined with font-synthesis: none, because you can’t know what font the text will be rendered in (no, your web font is not guaranteed to work; more people than you might realise block them and/or force the use of their own chosen fonts—I for one, it makes the web so much better), and the fallback font is unlikely to support the feature; and finally, :is(h1, h2, h3, h4, h5, h6), p, blockquote, figcaption, li is a completely useless example of :is().

Thank you for gathering these typography rules into one tidy reset — the section on font-size-adjust stood out to me, as fallback fonts so often change the apparent size of a page while a webfont loads. I also appreciated the honest note that text-box-trim and hanging punctuation are still unevenly supported, so the guardrails matter as much as the declarations themselves. It is a genuinely useful starting point that I will try on a small project first.

A reset that handles fluid type and vertical rhythm together is the part most resets skip. Hyphens in the same file is a nice touch.

I'd test the trimmed text beside an SVG icon with the webfont blocked, especially when the label wraps.

Thank you for pulling all of this together into tidy-typography.css, Stu. The section I keep coming back to is the one on rendering and fallback sanity. I spend a lot of time previewing font pairings, and the fallback swap is exactly where most sites lose their rhythm — a display face loads, the fallback is visibly narrower or taller, and the whole layout jumps. Your point about font-size-adjust: from-font reducing that apparent size change while a webfont swaps in is the clearest explanation of that fix I have read, and pairing it with font-synthesis: none so the browser does not invent a bold or italic face the family does not actually have makes the reset feel considered rather than just defensive.

The fluid typography section was the most useful part for me in practice. Using container query units so a heading scales to the column it occupies, instead of clamp() with vw scaling it as though it were always in a full-width article, solves a real problem I hit constantly: the same heading in a 300px sidebar should not behave like the same heading in the main column. The same goes for text-wrap: pretty and text-wrap: balance replacing the JavaScript orphan fixers I used to reach for, and the hyphenation tuning with hyphenate-limit-chars and hyphenate-limit-lines — I had never thought carefully about the ladder effect of several hyphenated lines in a row until this article. I also appreciated the honest framing that text-box-trim and hanging-punctuation are still unevenly supported, so they need @supports guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

One practical habit from my own work that fits well with this reset: when I test a new pairing, I preview it at small sizes with font-optical-sizing: auto on, because a face that looks crisp at display size can turn thin and hard to read at body size, and I check the same text with the webfont blocked so I can see the fallback in isolation. For anyone who wants a quick place to try out different styles side by side while testing a pairing, I keep a handy reference at https://fontsgenerators.net/ — it is useful for seeing how a style holds up before committing it to a stylesheet like this one. I will be trying tidy-typography.css as a base on my next project and keeping the experimental declarations behind @supports, exactly as you suggest.

#10140. What an AI Marketing Agent Actually Does, and How to Build a Guarded One

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI marketing agent is a system that pairs a language model with access to real tools, such as a CRM, an ad platform API or a CMS, and lets it work toward a goal instead of following a fixed script. That is the core idea. Much of what gets labeled agentic marketing is either a wrapper around that loop or a scheduled job with a new name.

This article breaks down the mechanics in code terms, walks through a minimal guarded agent loop with tests, and gives a framework for deciding what to build, what to buy and what to keep away from any autonomous system. Product capabilities change quickly, so check each vendor's current documentation.

● An agent is a goal, a tool set and a loop. The model chooses the next action at runtime instead of following branches you wrote in advance.

● Guardrails belong in code. Spend caps, allow-lists and approval thresholds should never depend on the prompt.

● The prototype is the easy part. Validation, retries, logging and evaluation are where the weeks go.

● Match autonomy to reversibility. Let agents do reversible, measurable work and keep irreversible actions supervised.

● Treat vendor claims as claims. Ask for logs and test on your own data.

Many marketing features have been renamed in the last couple of years. Email sequencing, lead scoring and campaign tools are now often described as agents. The honest question is whether a given product is a real architecture shift or marketing automation with a chat box added. Both exist: some systems run a perceive-reason-act loop with dynamic tool selection, while others are if/else branches with a model writing the subject line.

Strip away the marketing language and an agent has three parts:

● A goal, expressed in natural language or as a measurable target.

● A tool set: functions the model is allowed to call, each described by a schema.

● A loop that runs until the goal is met, a limit is hit or a human steps in.

The reasoning step is a model call. You pass it the current state, the goal and the available tools, and it responds with text or a structured request to call a tool. Your code runs the call, feeds the result back and asks again. OpenAI's function calling guide describes this as a conversation between your application and the model, which is the primitive underneath most agent frameworks.

Compare that with a cron job. A cron job runs a rule such as pausing ads below a click-through threshold every morning. It cannot decide that cost per acquisition matters more than click-through this week. An agent can, because the branching is chosen at runtime by a model rather than at write time by you. A useful test: if you can draw the full decision tree before the system runs, it is automation. If the path depends on what the model decides after seeing the data, it is an agent.

● Perceive. Read current state from an ad platform's reporting endpoint, a CRM webhook or a database. Ordinary code, no model involved.

● Reason. One model call containing the goal, the state, a summary of past actions and the schemas of every tool the agent may use.

● Act. The model returns a structured tool call. Your code validates the arguments against the schema and your guardrails, then executes.

● Observe. Capture the result, including failures. A rate-limit error or validation error is information for the next step.

● Update memory. Record the action and outcome so the agent does not repeat itself.

● Repeat or stop. Loop until the goal is met, an iteration cap is reached or a guardrail escalates to a human.

Steps two and three rely on function calling, where tools are declared with typed parameters and the model returns matching arguments. The Hugging Face function calling guide shows the same pattern. For connecting tools across systems, the Model Context Protocol is an open protocol for integrating LLM applications with external data sources and tools. Instead of writing an adapter for every CRM and ad account, you point an MCP client at a server that exposes those tools with discoverable schemas.

Lead scoring and routing. The agent reads CRM state, enriches it with lookups, scores the lead and writes back an owner and next action. It is agentic when it chooses which enrichment tools to call based on what is missing, and not agentic when the score is a fixed model output piped into a rule.

Ad creative generation and testing. The agent produces variations from brand inputs, launches a structured test, reads performance data and shifts budget toward what works. This is among the clearest closed feedback loops because the outcome signal is numeric and fast.

IBM's overview of AI agents in marketing frames a similar progression, from scripted chatbots at one end to agents that decide, design workflows and use function calling at the other. Most products sit somewhere in between.

Vendors sometimes blur this line. Four criteria help even from the outside:

Practical tests: give it a goal it has no preset workflow for and see whether it composes a path or returns an apology. Change the data mid-run and check whether the next step differs. Ask for the tool call log, because a real agent produces one. Ask what happens when a tool call fails; a script throws, while an agent should retry or re-plan.

Three questions decide this, and none are about how clever the model is: can errors be detected quickly, can the action be reversed, and what does one bad decision cost?

● Reasonable to hand over: drafting copy variations, summarizing campaign performance, flagging data mismatches, writing internal briefs, pausing clearly underperforming ads.

● Keep supervised: budget reallocation across accounts, legal, medical or financial claims, pricing and discount changes, messages to named customers and final brand-voice sign-off.

A human approval step costs minutes. An unsupervised mistake in those areas can cost far more.

For many teams the practical answer is to build the orchestration that touches proprietary data and internal systems, and to evaluate off-the-shelf tools for narrow, high-volume tasks that other teams have already solved. What people underestimate when building is not the prototype, which can take an afternoon, but everything after: schema validation, retries, idempotency, rate-limit handling, prompt versioning, cost controls, an audit log and an evaluation harness.

Ad creative is a common candidate for the existing-tool route because volume is high and the loop is similar across businesses. Vendors in this space describe their products differently, so compare scope, integrations and pricing. As one example, Larven describes an AI marketing agent that runs Meta ads, pulls products and copy from a store such as Shopify, and connects to your own ad account. Treat any such description as the vendor's own, and verify it with a trial on your data before committing.

The goal: keep spend inside a daily cap while letting a model propose budget changes. The model is injected as a function, so you can wire in any provider's function-calling API or a stub for tests. The important part is that every limit is enforced in code. The model proposes, and your code decides.

"""A minimal guarded agent loop. The model is injected as a function, so you can swap in any provider's function-calling API, or a stub for tests.""" from dataclasses import dataclass, field @dataclass class Guardrails:     allowed_campaigns: set     daily_cap: float            # max total daily budget across campaigns     max_change_pct: float = 0.2 # larger single changes need a human     max_actions: int = 5        # per run @dataclass class State:     budgets: dict               # campaign_id -> daily budget     log: list = field(default_factory=list)     pending: list = field(default_factory=list) def check(call, state, g):     """Return 'run', 'approve' or ('deny', reason). Enforced in code, never by the prompt."""     if call["name"] != "adjust_budget":         return ("deny", "unknown tool")     cid, new = call["args"]["campaign_id"], call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]     if cid not in g.allowed_campaigns or cid not in state.budgets:         return ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     if new < 0:         return ("deny", "negative budget")     others = sum(v for k, v in state.budgets.items() if k != cid)     if others + new > g.daily_cap:         return ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     old = state.budgets[cid]     if old and abs(new - old) / old > g.max_change_pct:         return "approve"     return "run" def run(model, state, g):     """model(state) -> a tool call dict, or None when it has nothing to do."""     for in range(g.maxactions):         call = model(state)         if call is None:             break         verdict = check(call, state, g)         state.log.append({"call": call, "verdict": verdict})         if verdict == "run":             state.budgets[call["args"]["campaign_id"]] = call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]         elif verdict == "approve":             state.pending.append(call)     return state

The tests use a stub in place of a model. They confirm that small changes run, large changes wait for approval, the daily cap and allow-list are enforced, an unknown tool is denied, and a model that never stops is halted by the action limit with everything logged:

from agent_loop import Guardrails, State, check, run G = Guardrails(allowed_campaigns={"a", "b"}, daily_cap=200) def call(cid, new): return {"name": "adjust_budget", "args": {"campaign_id": cid, "new_daily_budget": new}} def test_small_change_runs():     assert check(call("a", 105), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "run" def test_large_change_needs_approval():     assert check(call("a", 150), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "approve" def test_cap_and_allow_list_are_enforced():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 90, "c": 10})     assert check(call("a", 115), s, G) == ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     assert check(call("c", 11), s, G) == ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     assert check({"name": "delete_account", "args": {}}, s, G) == ("deny", "unknown tool") def test_loop_stops_at_action_limit_and_logs_everything():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500, max_actions=3)     run(lambda st: call("a", st.budgets["a"] + 1), s, g)   # a model that never stops     assert len(s.log) == 3 and s.budgets["a"] == 103 def test_denied_and_pending_actions_do_not_change_budgets():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500)     seq = iter([call("a", 150), call("a", 600), None])   # needs approval, then breaks the cap     run(lambda st: next(seq), s, g)     assert s.budgets["a"] == 100 and len(s.pending) == 1

Run them with pytest. In a real deployment, the model function would call your provider with the state, the goal and the tool schemas, then return the parsed tool call.

● It handles one tool. A real agent needs several, such as reporting, pausing ads and requesting approval.

● It keeps state in memory. Production systems need a durable store and idempotent actions so a retry cannot apply a change twice.

● The thresholds are placeholders. Set your own caps and percentages from your spend and risk tolerance.

● It validates the call but not the data. If upstream numbers are wrong, the agent will act on them.

Runaway spend. Budget APIs act instantly and changes compound. Without a hard ceiling in your own code, a reasoning error becomes a billing event.

Dirty upstream data. Stale CRM records, a broken product feed or a misattributed conversion. The agent can act confidently on wrong inputs and leave a tidy audit trail of well-reasoned bad decisions.

Missing audit trails. If you cannot reconstruct why an agent acted, you cannot fix or defend it. Deloitte's State of AI in the Enterprise reports that close to three-quarters of companies plan to deploy agentic AI within two years, and that only 21% of those companies report having a mature model for agent governance.

● Least-privilege credentials. A separate API key per agent, scoped to exactly what it needs, never a shared admin token.

● Hard caps in code. Daily spend ceilings and a maximum percentage change per adjustment, enforced outside the model.

● Human-in-the-loop thresholds. Define which actions always need approval.

● Log every tool call. Inputs, outputs, errors, model and prompt version, with timestamps you can query.

● Start read-only. Run in suggest mode and compare recommendations with what your team would have done.

A system that pairs a language model with real tools and lets it pursue a goal. It reads state, decides on an action, executes it, observes the result and decides again.

Automation starts from a trigger and follows branches defined in advance. An agent starts from a goal and selects tools at runtime.

Not on current evidence. They handle high-volume, measurable, reversible work well. Strategy, positioning and final brand approval still sit with people.

Function calling, where tools are declared by schema and the model returns matching arguments, and increasingly the Model Context Protocol for connecting tools.

It depends on how quickly errors are detected, whether actions are reversible and what a bad decision costs. Automate the cheap and reversible; supervise the rest.

Wrong tool calls, runaway spend, confident action on dirty data and brand drift at scale. Missing audit trails make all of them harder to diagnose.

An AI marketing agent is less magic than it sounds: a goal, a set of tools and a loop with limits. The model's intelligence matters, but the system around it decides whether it is safe to run. Define the tools narrowly, enforce limits in code, log everything, start read-only and widen autonomy only as the evidence supports it.

#10138. Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

#10137. CSS-Tricks, Version 20

I’ve been bashing on updating this site’s design for the last couple of weeks. It’s high time things get a fresh coat of paint, right? The last version of CSS-Tricks was pushed five years ago. That’s ancient in web (and dog) years.

It’s not like the site has simply sat all that time. We pushed lots of changes and shipped new features, particularly since 2024, but we never really got the paint brushes out of storage.

Now, before there’s a chorus of “things don’t look thaaaat different” I want to say I’m rarely a fan of complete re-builds and tend to lean into iterative releases. And the new v20 theme is what I’d call an iterative rollup that’s worthy of a version bump.

Let’s go over the most notable updates to see why.

First and foremost, some of the biggest changes are what you don’t see. As you might imagine, this site has carried over plenty of pieces across 19 years. I decided to excavate the theme and definitely found some ghosts to bust.

For example, the global compiled JavaScript file retained some scripts that just weren’t being used anymore. Things like handling embedded versions of the newsletter from before we began self-hosting the posts. There were relics that catered specifically to the Flexbox Guide for handling toggles back before we had proper HTML

. There was a jQuery lazy-loader thing that is well-covered by WordPress core now. We had some scripts for serving third-party ads that are no longer needed now that we’re self-hosting ads. Things like that. The overall JavaScript was nicely trimmed from 151.6 KB (55.3 KB gzipped) to 133.8 KB (48.1 KB).

I also decided to formally retire the CSS-Tricks Forum. We put it in “archive mode” back in, like, 2018. None of those discussions are particularly valuable for today as they were over 10 years ago, so I deactivated the bbPress plugin that powered it and cleared out the database… and wow, that carved a whopping 400 MB from the ol’ database, not to mention it allowed me to drop several custom templates from the theme.

There were more ghosts busted along the way and, to be sure, there are still some lurking around. But it’s way less scary poking around the theme directory now, and further cleanup should be a lot easier from here on out.

Yeah, yeah, raise your hand if you’re still running Gulp. We are 🙋‍♂️. That’s not a bad thing in and of itself, but keeping it in a fixed state for all this time is not the greatest. I think we’re sporting a more modern workflow than before, even if there’s still room to grow. A smattering of the related commits:

For years, I endured the same errors every time I fired up a new terminal. Those are gone and things run super cleanly, not to mention quickly. Very happy here.

Oof, this is everyone’s favorite job, right?! Aside from being able to spot and fix a few unescaped custom field calls here and there, I decided to ask Claude to help me with a holistic audit. I prompted it to review recent WordPress security release notes and identify areas where the theme could be hardened to comply with what WordPress is doing.

Whoa, you used AI? Yeah, I sure did. But for things like this where it could be my little gopher and allow me more time to do what I do best: front-end. I’m still able to make most of the patches myself based on the output recommendations, but it’s nice to have a gut-check for things where I’m clearly no expert, like security.

I still use and like Sass very much. I mean, yes, I would like to be straight-up vanilla CSS everywhere, but I can’t tear myself away from partials in particular.

The previous theme had a fairly nice “flat” structure — very few partial directories and most things all in the same top-level source. Totally cool. But an organization nut like me (you should see my house) has to have a place for everything to live. It ain’t perfect, but I’m happy with what I was able to put together without leveling the building in the process.

There are some compromises. Like, /header should really be in /layout. The ordering is really important, though, and some of my ideals were simply not possible. I’m hoping to eventually work in some sort of Cascade Layers structure.

I’m pleased as punch to see the overall size of CSS drop in the process. It went from 180 KB (31.7 KB) to 169 KB (24.6 KB). Not hugely significant, but not not significant either. I had to do a couple tricky things to cheat my way there, like serving all the form styles I’m about to talk about into a separate forms.css file and serving it just on pages where WooCommerce, Restrict Content Pro, and Gravity Forms are enqueued.

Are you starting to notice a trend? We’re still talking about fairly invisible work. CSS-Tricks has so many nooks and crannies that it’s nearly impossible to make things perfectly consistent across the board. But because of those nooks and crannies, so many fixed values, magic numbers, one-off styles, and older utilities were hiding about. I decided to tackle a good chunk of that while reorganizing the Sass.

For example, there are now semantic design tokens that abstract away one-off colors. So many grays, let me tell you. Now there’s a maintainable palette that’s referenced throughout. Same with fonts, font styles, and font sizes. Same with paddings and margins. It feels a lot better to make a change at the “system” level with confidence that the change is taking effect everywhere it needs to.

But this is also where some clearly visible changes happened! Most notably, link styles are consistent all over the place with a small handful of intended and maintained variations. Not only is it more pleasing visually, but it improves accessibility throughout because the inherited styles default to proper visual cues that do not rely solely on color or opacity changes.

Oh, and my favorite piece of this? Forms and tables have both been redone. That’s tough to do in a WordPress theme where these components don’t always come from one source. For example, forms could come from any one of the Gravity Forms, WooCommerce, or Restrict Content Pro plugins, and they all have their own stylesheets. Resetting those allowed me to bring a certain level of harmony in all those instances.

OK, something even more visual. This is the first time CSS-Tricks has had more than one color scheme! It certainly could have been done a long time ago, but I’d also argue that the design hasn’t really called for a light/dark toggle over the last 8 years or so. It’s a nice blend of both light and dark elements that marry the best of both worlds no matter your theme preference.

But I had to take a stab, right? The site has predominantly been dark despite the content being against a white background, so I treated things as though a “dark” mode already existed. The result is a creamy light theme that is largely just a change in background and component styles. And I used some of what I learned from the recent “toggle” debate to handle system, dark, and light themes with a single toggle.

What hasn’t changed is the main content container you see in things like articles. That was already white, even in a “dark” mode, and I’m not confident that simply toggling it between white and black is the best approach. Something on the radar for sure.

Oh, and it’s all wired up to a view transition that slides the new theme over the previous one.

I hope you think the site’s header and footer are cute. Yes, cute. That’s what I was going for when adding a colorful series of dots as the backdrop.

Sorta like a subtle candy texture, right? Or a colorful group of stars? I dunno, but I’m calling it “Jawbreaker” for lack of a better term and because it reminds me of the jawbreaker candy I loved as a kid. Naming still sucks.

It’s all part of the same SVG. I really, really (really) wanted to random()-ly position dots around, but that would have taken a lot of empty individual elements (e.g., ) in the markup. Not my favorite. So, I generate the elements in PHP:

I really, really wanted to random()-ize all the things, but inlining random coordinates on each was the way to go. Same deal with the inlined styles that set a fill, and sizing for each circle in the texture, and variables that set a --delay and --dur on the subtle animation that twinkles them in and out.

I’ll have to look at generating the SVG using or whatever later since that pattern repeats in the footer, too.

That’s carried through to the 404 page as well:

If your browser supports border-shape and random() (only Chrome, I think), then the homepage gives you a blobby-blob shape behind the grid of author avatars. Hover it and it gets, you know, even blobbier.

It takes an existing pattern I’m calling .gradient-card:

…and sets a gradient on the component’s :before pseudo:

That sits underneath the :after pseudo that’s translucent, allowing the :before‘s gradient to show through a smidge:

The differing border-radius values allow :before to bleed outside, giving the impression of a border around the entire deal. That’s the basic shape. The border-shape is where things get blobby:

Those variables? They’re mapped to shape()s. I grabbed the first one from Temani Afif’s blob generator:

It’s called “static” because that’s the default state. Hover or focus on the component and it swaps out those points out with a “hover” version of the shape:

Those variables? They’re mapped to random() values:

So, the hover state is different on each page load. And we animate between the two:

I’m pretty stoked on this one. The Almanac has grown, evolved, and just plain gotten great the past few years. But the primary page for it… not so great. It was definitely overshadowed by work on the pages inside it and never revisited beyond clickable cards to get to each feature subset.

Now the page is a real index of the CSS features that gets you where you need to go quickly. Someone somewhere sometime did some sort of CSS periodic table. I forget where I’d seen that but the general idea sat with me and I thought I could pull it off. There’s nothing “tricky” about it but I love what the layout adds to the overall site.

This is another area that sorely needed love. Our guides have always been a vertical stack of items. The book effect was definitely cool, but I started believing that leaning on it as the main visual hook was holding things back from being more functional. With something like 27 guides, we’re talking about a lot of scrolling with no clear idea of what you’re going to see as you go.

Again, nothing “tricky” but I like the overall usefulness of the new layout. There’s a featured guide, a widget of newly added and updated guides, and a categorized grid of everything below that.

The pages that show all of an author’s published articles got a little spruce-up, too.

It’s a little change, really. The right sidebar was moved to the left and the author image and bio moved in, allowing the articles to creep up to the top. I hope to do more here since the small layout tweak opens up more opportunities.

Also need to do the larger Authors index, but another time.

This one’s just for those of us working in the WordPress editor. I don’t need a complete visual editor to get things done, but it is a nice affordance to have some of the front-end styles sneak into the editing UI for a better idea of what you’re working with.

Using Lighthouse to measure things like performance, accessibility, and best practices is woefully fraught with caveats, but there’s no reason to chuck it completely. I find them more fun than fact and I don’t mind the recommendations for improving things.

So, after a few runs and tweaks, here’s how things shook out theme-to-theme.

I’m happy here. There are some things I just don’t have the control to fix when it comes to performance, so seeing v20 come in consistent with v19 is just fine by me. I’m happier with the boosts to accessibility and best practices. SEO? Meh. Don’t really care unless it’s hurting more important things like accessibility.

Again, very happy. There’s little I can do with time to first byte but everything else seems to clock in at least marginally better. The total page weight is a clear win and that’s probably what I care most about here.

Oh gosh, there’s a lot more but they’re mostly minor things that don’t bubble up to the front end. Sort of the same way browser changelogs have some generic line of “bug squashing” or whatever. In this case, it’s things like better post queries and breadcrumbs and function management and naming and… you get it.

There’s definitely so much more to do. And plenty of things I’m sure I missed or broke along the way. That’s just life and those will have to come in another iterative release less worthy of a version bump. You can let me know what I muffed up in the comments.

I hope you at least like where this is going!

#10133. CSS Optimization: Is Using Declarations Just Once (UDJO) Still Effective Today?

CSS optimization is a deceptive topic. On the one hand, it feels like something simple that we should already have all the tooling for — especially when it comes to minification. On the other hand, the idea that we already exploited “everything” there is around CSS optimization may be considered naive. Such a claim looks hard to defend for a language that evolves almost daily.

In this article, I’d like to pick a technique that’s at least 18 years old — UDJO, or “using declarations just once.”

Aware of an older source? Please comment!

The technique is inspired by the principle of DRY, “don’t repeat yourself”, in that it works by identifying and reducing (if not eliminating) declaration repetition.

So, if there’s something like display: flex and it appears two or more times, it gets reconciled in one rule:

The outcome of the technique depends on how intelligently the deduplication is done and how long the selectors are. Intelligence determines if UDJO leads to an actual maintainability advantage — can the change of a declaration really be done to all the selectors attached? And the selector length influences how big file size savings are.

If you’re interested, my article on “DRY CSS” describes the mechanics in more detail. The question here is: Is UDJO still effective and still relevant today?

When we try to answer this question, however, we end up with a problem: When sites are complex and their style sheet declarations have not been de-duplicated before, UDJO can require a lot of work.

To save some time, I built tooling for this work. While it didn’t seem like it would immediately be as comprehensive as manually going through and testing style sheets, it seemed realistic enough that a tool could catch some repetition.

Skipping ahead, I created CSS Dedup not only to identify repetition, but also to deduplicate what could safely be optimized (CSS Dedup has some documentation on this but in essence, deduplication affects selector locations, which is part of how the cascade works). This, then, gives us some data:

For the first batch, I arbitrarily picked 20 websites to analyze. I then used CSS Dedup to check what the file size changes would be if we deduplicated what seemed provably safe, and also what seemed probably safe, but only if it led to file size savings.

I picked those two options to provide the “natural” result of deduplication, but also the one site owners may be most interested in to reduce repetition but also decrease transfer size. After all, UDJO as a CSS optimization technique can improve both maintainability and performance.

Important note: These values cannot be used to tell CSS quality—they depend on selector length and also on verifying if deduplication, in the respective case, actually leads to better maintainability. Also note that this leaves out changes after compression—this was not part of the experiment but would be fair to test, too.

For the question whether UDJO still works today, this should be a yes. It works as well as to even come with file size savings.

Maybe you’re curious, too, about what the situation looks like in our field, among web developers. Let’s take some samples on this, too.

It would be valuable to expand this analysis to take more samples and investigate the differences across samples, but there may be something to web professionals using shorter selectors so as to be able to benefit more from UDJO.

As mentioned at the beginning, UDJO’s effectiveness generally depends on the kind of repetition as well as on the length of selectors.

This can be managed, however: While tools like CSS Dedup make no difference between declaration use cases (and are therefore blunt), it is possible to configure them so as to reap the performance benefits.

As the data taken for this article suggest, file size savings may be achieved in most cases. Considering that this is based on safe changes, this means a low-cost performance optimization vector — especially when applied to minified output. It’s still a good idea to couch that against the use of further compression (e.g. gzip/Brotli), but at face value, deduplication shows how it can reduce the size of CSS.

For anyone generally interested in CSS optimization, maintainability, and performance, UDJO should probably be part of the overall approach to CSS. But since the technique has been niche and since tooling is new, I invite you to review, test, and explore this vector. It seems hard to argue that we have reached the ceiling of how far we can take CSS optimization.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

and also to add that in the last weeks I’ve been testing some approaches to using CSS Dedup. It has proven quite effective, especially in the following setup: On static sites, reaping only savings via css-dedup CSS_FOLDER -p "(?

I’ll write more about this shortly (on my website)—and am curious about your thoughts and observations!

I’d be curious to see the minuscule performance implications, if any at all.

If up to 11% savings in a small sample are true – even pre-compression – then that’s not minuscule. Especially not with the average size of a stylesheet.

I’d like to see more sites being tested both pre and post compression.

#10128. Beyond 12 Columns: Designing Layouts With Personality

When I started designing websites thirty years ago, David Siegel had just published Creating Killer Web Sites, the book that popularised HTML tables and spacer GIFs for laying out web pages. CSS eventually gave us more control over layouts through floats and positioning. Neither was intended for page layout, and inconsistent browser behaviour could make using them frustrating. Still, that didn’t stop me and a generation of other designers from using floats and positioning.

When CSS frameworks began providing ready-made grid systems, things got a little easier. Blueprint CSS) offered a 24-column grid, while Nathan Smith’s 960 Grid System offered 12- and 16-column options. These frameworks gave designers and developers a shared structure, making layout planning and implementation considerably easier.

Twelve-column grids weren’t invented in the 2000s. Graphic designers had used column-based and modular grids for decades, and twelve is a convenient number because you don’t need to be a maths genius to divide it into halves, thirds, and quarters to create the 2-up, 3-up, and 4-up components we see everywhere online.

Frameworks like Bootstrap and Foundation followed, helping establish twelve-column grids as the de facto standard for page layouts. I get why that happened. But somewhere along the way, one layout option became the default starting point for almost everything.

CSS Grid changed what’s possible for page layouts, but I still see designers start by dividing a page into twelve equal columns, then deciding how to fit their content into them. The designs they end up with might be perfectly fine, but they can also look remarkably similar.

Still, I don’t think twelve-column grids themselves are the problem. It’s reaching for a familiar solution before considering what makes the company I’m designing for distinctive and what its website needs to communicate. So what happens when I start with those questions instead?

Before I decide how many columns a layout needs, I want to understand the company I’m designing for. What makes it different from its competitors? What does it want people to remember? Does it have a personality that could influence how its website looks and feels?

These questions matter because a layout does more than arrange content. The size, position, and relationships between elements influence how people experience a website. A restrained, orderly composition can communicate something very different from one that’s full of energy and movement. So to demonstrate what I mean, I’ll use Wheelman. It’s a fictional company I invented for my book, Standout Designs for the Web.

Pre-orders for Standout Designs for the Web are now open in the CSS-Tricks store.

Wheelman provides products and services for getaway drivers, including legal advice, robbery insurance, and an online shop selling break-in essentials. It also publishes a magazine with advice, news, and stories about life behind the wheel.

Wheelman isn’t an ordinary company, so why should its website look like an everyday collection of components? It takes its business seriously, but isn’t afraid to have fun with its design. It’s rebellious, but doesn’t want to look off-putting. Its audience is criminals, but they demand professionalism. Wheelman has plenty of personality to work with, and I want its designs to reflect that.

That doesn’t mean making Wheelman different for difference’s sake. Its customers still need to find what they’re looking for, understand what it provides, and buy its products and services. But I can make those experiences more memorable by choosing layouts that fit the company, rather than settling for familiar arrangements.

Let’s imagine I’m designing a new landing page for Wheelman’s online shop. It includes two products, one with a gigantic headline, product images, and the type of functionality and information you’ll find on most stores.

Before I decide how to arrange these elements, I want to look beyond them and consider how they relate. I ask myself questions like:

Only then do I start thinking about visual design questions: “Would a large photograph set the scene? Should I make the introduction more prominent? Can whitespace lead the eye and create spaces to pause? How can I bring images and text together into a considered composition?”

These questions help me understand what the layout needs to accomplish before I decide which grid might help me achieve it.

I could arrange everything in a single column for a straightforward reading experience. That would make responsive design considerably easier. But I won’t.

Wheelman has a rebellious personality, and I want its shop to reflect that. A more energetic layout seems appropriate, but I still need to establish a clear hierarchy between the products and their information. Two equal-width columns give both products equal prominence.

Changing their proportions creates a hierarchy, even when most elements stay the same size.

I could base those proportions on a twelve-column grid, but I won’t. Columns only divide space vertically, and I want a layout that adds another dimension.

Dividing a layout into columns is a useful starting point, but adding rows creates another dimension and opens up more creative possibilities. Where columns and rows intersect, they form rectangular or square units called modules.

Combining these modules creates larger spatial zones that can establish hierarchy, control whitespace, and add variety to a composition.

Using a modular grid, I can add drama, establish hierarchy by varying scale, and make the composition feel far less predictable. Starting with four columns and four rows gives me sixteen modules to work with, but I don’t need to fill them all.

I can create drama by spreading the large graphic headline and product image across four modules, while the product information and shopping tools occupy one module each.

The modular structure becomes even more apparent in the grid of alternative products. Most occupy just one module, while others fill two, either horizontally or vertically. Varying their sizes gives some products more prominence and makes the overall arrangement feel less predictable.

The result still feels like a product page, with a clear reading order and space for the content to breathe. But it also feels unmistakably like something Wheelman would publish.

I don’t expect this composition to work at every screen size, so I can adjust the size and quantity of modules to improve the reading experience.

And if one grid offers this much flexibility, imagine what combining two or more grids can do.

One thing I want from Wheelman’s website is variety. Its magazine, shop, and services serve different purposes, and I don’t want every page to feel like I’ve poured its content into the same template.

Wheelman needs layouts which feel organised without being predictable. Compound grids give me the structure I need while leaving room for less conventional arrangements.

A compound grid combines two or more grids of any type — column, modular, or hierarchical — on the same page. These grids can overlap, sit side by side, or be stacked one on top of the other. Combining grids creates possibilities which neither grid offers on its own.

I can start by overlaying a two-column grid with one containing three columns. Their combined grid lines create four columns of two different widths, giving me more flexibility without losing the underlying structure.

Those proportions offer more possibilities for arranging content. I might use a wider area for a photograph or headline, then place a pullquote into one of the narrower spaces. Another page could use the same underlying grid but combine its spaces differently to suit another story.

Combining grids with different column counts gets even more interesting as those numbers increase. In Standout Designs for the Web, I explore several combinations, including mixing four- and five-column grids to create different compositions for Wheelman’s pages while maintaining a connected visual structure.

The point isn’t to make every page look different for its own sake. It’s to give each section a layout that suits its content while keeping them all feeling like part of the same company.

Twelve-column grids are useful, and I’m certainly not suggesting you should stop using them entirely. But they’re only one option among many, and starting every design with the same structure limits your possibilities before you’ve even considered the story your design is telling.

There’s also nothing inherently wrong with the 2-up, 3-up, and 4-up patterns a twelve-column grid inspires. They can sometimes be exactly what’s needed. But reaching for them automatically can leave little room for the character and personality that make websites memorable.

Wheelman gives me plenty of reasons to choose different layouts. Its stories, products, and services don’t all need the same arrangements, and its personality gives me opportunities to make those arrangements more distinctive. The grids are simply tools that help me put those ideas into practice.

And this applies to much more than layout. The same thinking should influence the colours I choose, the typography I use, the images I commission, and the way I bring those elements together.

That’s why I wrote Standout Designs for the Web. I want to encourage designers to look beyond familiar solutions and make more deliberate decisions about colour, typography, imagery, and layout. Not to be different for difference’s sake, but to create websites that look and feel like they belong to the companies behind them. And no one else.

Pre-orders for Standout Designs for the Web are open! Everyone who pre-orders will also receive the Hardboiled Client Interview Guide for free — a practical companion to help designers ask better questions and discover what makes a company distinctive.

The book will be published on November 20, priced at $14.99 for the DRM-free EPUB and PDF editions.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

#10127. On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

#10125. How AI-Powered Cameras Are Changing Content Creation: What Developers Should Know

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating high-quality visual content once meant specialized cameras, technical knowledge and hours of editing. Today, software does a growing share of that work. Cameras recognize subjects, adjust exposure, stabilize footage and hand clips to editing tools that transcribe, reframe and tag them.

Digital photography made images easy to capture, review and share. Smartphones then folded capture, editing and publishing into one device. The next shift was computational photography: instead of relying only on the lens and sensor, the device combines several captures and processes them in software.

High dynamic range (HDR) imaging is the clearest example. A camera captures frames at different exposures and merges them so both bright skies and dark foregrounds keep detail. Research indexed on PubMed, published in IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence, examines how deep learning has advanced HDR imaging, particularly in reconstructing visual information across different exposure levels.

Machine learning extends this approach from merging pixels to understanding them, which is what lets a camera decide what is the subject and what is the background.

Most AI camera features fall into a few groups.

Because of these failure modes, a pipeline should check what the camera produced before running expensive downstream steps.

A cheap pre-check can reject a frame that is too soft or badly exposed before you spend time on transcription, upscaling or model inference. The script below uses numpy and Pillow. It measures sharpness as the variance of a Laplacian, a standard blur indicator, and measures exposure as the share of crushed and clipped pixels.

The test builds a detailed random frame and a blurred copy, then checks that the sharp frame scores far higher, that a mostly black frame is flagged as underexposed and that a mostly white frame is flagged as overexposed:

Run the tests with python3 test_frame_quality.py. In our run, the detailed test frame scored about 33,000 for sharpness and its blurred copy scored under 2.

Traditional capture means holding a camera, propping up a phone or setting up equipment. Wearable cameras let creators record from their own point of view while their hands stay free, which suits travel video, instructional content, outdoor activities and behind-the-scenes footage.

Camera-equipped smart glasses are one form of this. Several makers sell them, and consumer listings such as ai glasses with camera show how the category is presented to buyers. Depending on the device, users start recording with physical controls or voice commands. Specifications differ widely, so compare field of view, storage, battery life and software support before choosing one.

For developers, wearables change the input. Footage is shaky, framed by head movement and often recorded outdoors, which is exactly where a quality check and stabilization matter. Battery capacity, camera position, storage and recording duration also limit extended productions. Wearables are an additional option for perspectives that are hard to reach otherwise, not a replacement for traditional cameras.

Editing involves reviewing clips, correcting color, adjusting audio and preparing videos for each platform. AI tools automate several repetitive parts of that work:

Creative judgment stays human. Choosing meaningful moments, shaping narrative flow and keeping a consistent visual style are decisions software cannot reliably make. Real-time assistance during recording will depend on hardware, software and environmental conditions.

As cameras get smaller, responsible recording matters more. Wearable devices can capture footage without the obvious movements of a handheld camera, so people nearby may not know recording is happening. The European Data Protection Board has highlighted transparency, personal data protection and recording awareness as important considerations for camera-equipped smart glasses.

Authenticity is the second challenge. AI tools can enhance an image, but heavy alteration can also mislead. The National Institute of Standards and Technology has examined technical approaches to digital content transparency, including provenance tracking, watermarking and synthetic-content detection. On the provenance side, the C2PA coalition publishes an open standard called Content Credentials, which helps show where content came from and what edits it has undergone.

Cameras will likely get better at faster subject recognition, more efficient automated editing and stronger low-light performance as computer vision and on-device computing advance. Wearables may widen first-person storytelling, and editing tools may make multi-platform publishing simpler. Technical gains alone will not decide quality, though. Originality, storytelling and thoughtful creative decisions still do.

Not always. Toolkits such as ML Kit face detection and Apple's Vision framework run image analysis on the device, which reduces delay and keeps frames local.

It narrows the gap in many conditions, but optics, sensor size and control still matter for demanding work, and AI enhancement can introduce artifacts of its own.

Add an early quality gate that checks sharpness and exposure, as in the script above, and calibrate its thresholds on your own footage.

It depends on location and context. Regulators such as the European Data Protection Board have raised transparency and consent concerns, so check local rules and be open about recording.

Provenance standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to show a file's origin and edit history, though they only help when tools and platforms support them.
