#10115. A2UI 1.0: Build delightful, fast, reliable, agentic experiences

User interactions with agents today are where computers were in the terminal era: capable, and answering almost everything in text. Users can ask an agent for anything by typing or talking, and what comes back is usually a wall of text, a poor way to compare three products or edit and approve a detailed plan.

Today, we're releasing A2UI 1.0, the first stable version of our open protocol for generative UI. It lets AI agents answer with interactive screens, such as a form to confirm or a chart. Developers can use it now with open-source renderer SDKs for the web, Android, and iOS and agent SDKs for Python, TypeScript, and Dart. Companies such as Atlassian, Alation, Bloomberg, and ByteDance are already building with it.

With A2UI, the agent describes what to show, and every app draws the UI with its own native components. The application receives A2UI messages and each app renders the content securely and at native speed. Users get the same low latency, high trust, and complete native fidelity whether they reach a company's agent in its own app or in a third-party AI assistant that hosts MCP apps, such as Gemini or Claude.

You wake up sick. Getting through the morning takes six apps: book a video visit, move your meetings, tell your team, turn on an out-of-office reply, ask your partner to do school pickup and order soup. Ask a text-only agent instead, and you get a numbered wall of text, then round after round of “which ones?”. A2UI won't handle your sick day, but it gives developers the framework to build an agent that can. That agent could answer with one view of all six apps. Changing your plan is a couple of taps instead of another round of messages, and nothing is sent until you confirm.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The same agent output works on any screen. Ask an agent to “move dinner with Priya to Friday”, and it can answer with one view that lines up your calendar, open tables from a restaurant booking app and prices from a rideshare app, so the time that works for everyone is obvious. The developer builds that view once with A2UI, and it renders natively on your phone and on your laptop.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both examples, A2UI is the framework that simplifies building these experiences across platforms. Developers build the agent and choose the platforms it works with. A2UI gives them the protocol the agent uses to describe each view, a way to combine components from different apps in one view, and renderers that draw it natively on the web, Android, and iOS.

New to A2UI? Start with the A2UI Playground by CopilotKit or check out industry examples.

Atlassian is bringing A2UI to Jira, where teams can assign work items to AI agents. When an agent needs a follow-up from the team to finish a work item, such as a missing detail or a decision, it asks with an interactive card in the work item, and the team answers right there in Jira.

The Alation Intelligence Operating System (AIOS) builds its feeds using A2UI: metric cards, charts, and more. Each chart can show the SQL query and data behind it. Ask Claude for a feed about profitable products, and the feed renders in the conversation using A2UI to render metric cards, charts, and custom components. The same feed with the same information can be viewed directly inside Alation with a nearly identical layout.

ByteDance released an A2UI renderer for Lynx, its cross-platform framework, in June 2026. Lynx keeps streamed screens smooth and ships a default set of components built on its `lynx-ui` library. In the Lynx GenUI Playground, you can watch A2UI messages rendered natively, for example, as a two-day trip planner for Kyoto. Lynx supports A2UI 0.9 today and is adapting to 1.0 with the A2UI team.

Ask Gemini Notebook for a slide deck and it replies with a card that asks two things: presenter slides or a detailed deck, and which topics to focus on. A review card follows with the format, focus, and visual style, which you can edit before it generates the deck. The same cards guide Video Overviews on the web and on mobile.

Organizations can register their own agents that respond with A2UI. Gemini renders those responses using the components in its A2UI component gallery, and supports A2UI 0.9.1 today. The Google Cloud documentation covers registering an agent and hosting one on Cloud Run.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI.

Several other Google teams, including some behind our largest products, are building features with A2UI that haven’t launched yet. We’ll share more as they ship.

A2UI and MCP Apps work together. Alation already uses both to show its A2UI feeds in Claude. Our June post, A2UI + MCP Apps, covers three ways to combine them, and the A2UI in MCP Apps guide shows how to run A2UI screens inside an MCP App.

Jetpack Compose support for 1.0 is coming soon. Agents in Gemini Enterprise can already use A2UI, and we're working on upgrading Gemini Enterprise to A2UI 1.0. The A2UI roadmap covers the rest, from design-system primitives that render natively on every platform to official SDK support for decision models like Jev.

We’ve collaborated with partners who have built A2UI 1.0 demos across different industries to give you inspiration on how to adapt A2UI to your use cases. Explore what they’ve built:

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

Did the user ask for this? Securing AI agents against intent hijacking with Semantic governance policies

#10114. Staggering Scroll-Driven Animations with animation-range-start

The other day on the CSS-Tricks Slack channel, Danny Schwartz shared an interesting recent Master.dev article by Ashutosh Biswas on how to stagger CSS scrolling animations using a clever combo of several emergent CSS features and a lot of math, which Ashutosh admits is “a huge formula and can look intimidating.”

When Danny Slacked the link for the Frontend Masters article, he described it as “CSS on drugs.” Speaking of drugs, I am on heavy prescription painkillers while recovering from a serious injury, so that might not help my current comprehension skills. Nevertheless, after reading Ashutosh’s article a few times, here’s a meme showing how I’d feel using such an elaborate algorithm for something as mundane as staggering the animations of some characters as they scroll into view.

Don’t get me wrong. I know the complexity is because Ashutosh is going for a generic solution in which we can connect a “progress value to an animation, scroll progress, or any other input to drive the staggering.”

In fact, I can dream up a scenario in which this might be what we want: Imagine a CSS karaoke machine that displays a ball bouncing on text that incrementally highlights, based on the user’s progress through the vocals measured via speech recognition. Running that kind of animation “not as a collection of independently timed objects, but as a single predictable, holistic effect driven by one progress value” might be exactly what the doctor ordered. This would be true because the staggered animations could be interrelated, and the overall animation would be driven by a custom input that’s not built into the browser.

But unlike this hypothetical scenario, replacing the passage of time with scroll progress to control animations is already built into modern CSS. I contend that we’d need a better reason to roll our own implementation than the examples Ashutosh gives.

Ashutosh showcases a scroll-driven use case as “one of those ideal scenarios where the stagger formula shines.” But a red flag that we’re in danger of reinventing something we can get for free is that the spec for scroll-driven animations is cognizant that if we opt to base an animation on scroll progress rather than on time, then, as Ashutosh says, “the animation we are going to achieve is different from traditional ones because we don’t include time in our considerations.” This is no surprise to the creators of scroll timeline, and the MDN docs make Ashutosh’s point in different words when introducing scroll timeline.

The docs also point out that animation-delay is only meaningful when we are using the default time-based document timeline. Therefore, animation-delay does not affect scroll-driven animations. But this is why CSS introduced animation-range-start, which allows us to delay a scroll-driven animation relative to scroll progress and not time.

So, as esoteric and trippy as the math-heavy approach appears, that could be because we are doing something that should be simple in an unusual way.

Let’s see if I’m right by rewriting Ashutosh’s demo to remove reliance on custom CSS functions and use animation-range-start instead.

Ashutosh’s rework of Frontend Masters’ animated tally of donations in the site footer is a nifty, subtle use of scrollytelling that makes the user feel invested in revealing that number.

Sidenote: The only qualm I have about this use of scroll-driven animations for the donation tally is that it might be too subtle — if the user scrolls too quickly, the animated entrance of each digit might occur too quickly to notice. Therefore, the donation tally could be a good case study for comparing the merits of scroll-driven versus scroll-triggered animations as design options. However, that story will have to wait for another day, as the focus of this article is only to show that the staggered scrolling CSS animations can be greatly simplified.

In the video below, my eight-year-old narrates, as a reminder that simplicity is key to the web we’re choosing to leave to the next generation. He compares the complex math in the original demo source code with my animation-range-start fork. He shows the two versions working side by side, demonstrating that sometimes the only thing we lose by judiciously deleting complex code is a headache.

And for those using a Chromium-based browser, my fork of the Pen is below. You can peruse the CSS to see how it does away with the grand unified theory of staggered scrolling animations, and instead deals only with the problem at hand. Since I preserved the original functionality, you’ll need to scroll gradually to appreciate the staggered animations.

I once tackled a related problem with a mathematical formula in a previous article, which touched on how we can use sibling-index() to “increment animation-range-start to stagger the delay before characters fade in as the user scrolls.”

The truth is, if we refuse to fall into the coding trap of possibly abstracting the wrong thing, we can use one line of CSS to replace a formula that took 1.3k words in the Frontend Masters article for me to still not fully grasp:

The 2.5% increment for each sibling is because there are eight characters to stagger, and I wanted to choreograph the animations such that they are delayed in evenly-spaced increments till the last character makes its animated entrance when it reaches 30% of the containment range (10% + 20%).

It was inadvertently apt to describe the original post as “CSS on drugs.” Once you reinvent one built-in CSS feature, you can’t stop there. You find yourself having to recreate more stuff that CSS should be able to do by itself. Sure enough, even after we get through the custom formulas that make scroll timelines do what they already do, Ashutosh announces, “Unfortunately, we can’t use easing keywords or cubic-bezier() or linear() functions here. We are left on our own to create easing functions.”

We shouldn’t have to because, despite the word “timing” in the name of the animation-timing-function property, MDN is clear that “animation-timing-function has the same effect when creating CSS scroll-driven animations as it does for regular time-based animations.” I know from experience that scroll-driven animations work with animation-timing-function because I used scroll-driven animations with steps() to animate a sprite climbing a ladder in my CSS scrollytelling game.

The problem in the land of CSS on drugs is that after merging all the staggered animations into one, the easing applies to the entire master timeline rather than to each element. It’s not a flaw in CSS, but a logical consequence of the “holistic effect driven by one progress value.” Be careful what you wish for.

To be fair, over the years I have seen impressive results from developers reinventing things the browsers already do, usually because the reinvention gives us control we couldn’t obtain otherwise.

I mention this to show that reinventing something like scroll timelines isn’t an inherently bad idea if we know why we’re doing it. After all, scroll timelines are only a reinvention of what developers were doing for decades prior in JavaScript. Having said that, I believe my rewrite of the staggered scroll-driven tally demo shows that the original approach was overkill for that example. Do let me know in the comments if you see any advantage to the custom CSS functions version over using animation-range-start.

And yet the thought that stuck with me about the Frontend Masters post is that it opens the door to linking the progress of a CSS animation to any arbitrary input value that could be used in a conceptually similar way to scroll timelines.

This really appeals to me, because even when I first started writing about scroll timelines in 2024, I mentioned that “I would love to see other input controls that can manipulate animation timelines. Imagine text fields progressing animations as you fill them out.” Or how about an animation driven by how closely the new password you are typing matches a password scheme?

Sidenote: Adam Argyle’s Prop For That library, which Geoff recently wrote up, makes it more convenient than ever to expose validation information about a field via CSS custom properties.

I can add my hypothetical karaoke machine scenario to the wishlist of custom progress inputs for controlling animations. How about an animation driven by the user’s progress through a quiz or a shopping cart?

Custom animation timelines would open the door to all sorts of engaging user experiences—but the code Ashutosh has shared may be the closest we are likely to get to driving CSS animations using values other than time or scroll progress.

Let me know in the comments if you can think of other types of progress inputs that could drive CSS animations to create immersive, interactive experiences.

CSS-Tricks editor Geoff Graham sits down with author Lee Meyer to demo Google’s Prompt API and weigh it (good and bad) place in front-end development.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Staggering with animation-range-start is neat because each element can reveal on its own scroll offset without JavaScript or IntersectionObserver setup. One thing I’d consider is wrapping it in prefers-reduced-motion so sensitive users aren’t hit with moving content. Did you test fallbacks for browsers lacking support?

You are right, George, in a real production version it should definitely check for both prefers reduced motion and whether view timeline is supported. For what it’s worth this stuff is newly baseline since Firefox finally supports it. The other browsers have supported it for a while

There is a polyfill for older browsers https://github.com/flackr/scroll-timeline which does work quite well from my testing, although it can’t provide the performance improvements of native scroll timeline so it can get janky and it has some other limitations such as timeline scope not working the same

It is still better than nothing and uses the native version where possible

#10111. How to Build an Accessible Multi Step Form: A Practical Guide With a Vanilla JavaScript Example

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A long form can feel like a chore before anyone types a word. Splitting it into smaller steps lets people focus on a few related questions at a time.

The code looks simple: show one section, hide the rest and add a button. The difficult parts are validation, keyboard use, focus and what happens when JavaScript doesn't run. This guide builds an accessible multi step form with HTML and plain JavaScript. It also reproduces a bug that can leave visitors stuck on the first step.

Multiple steps make sense when questions fall into clear groups, such as project details, timing and contact information. A form with only an email address and a message usually doesn't need them.

Splitting a form doesn't make unnecessary questions useful. Remove those first. More steps also aren't a guarantee of more completed forms, so test the change with your own audience.

The W3C tutorial on multiple page forms advises dividing long forms into logical stages and telling users about their progress. Our example applies those ideas within a single page.

Without JavaScript, all three groups remain visible and Send works as an ordinary submit button. The Back button stays hidden because it has no job in that view. This approach is called progressive enhancement: start with a working form, then add the step behavior.

The action value is a placeholder. Point it to a real server endpoint in your project. If your stylesheet sets a display value on fieldsets, check that it doesn't override the hidden attribute.

Save the following code as form.js in the same folder:

The form has one submit button. Its label reads Continue until the last step. The submit handler decides whether to show an error, open the next step or let the browser send the data. Pressing Enter in a text field follows the same path.

Back is a regular button, so it never submits anything. The answers remain in their fields because the code only hides each step. It doesn't remove or rebuild it.

To test its purpose, I removed only this line, filled in the first field and pressed Enter in headless Chromium 158. The form stayed on step 1. The console reported messages like these:

Before firing the submit event, the browser checked the entire form. The empty required fields on later steps were invalid, but they were hidden. Submission was blocked, so our handler never had a chance to open step 2.

Setting noValidate stops that automatic check during submission. It doesn't disable checkValidity() or reportValidity(), so our code can validate only the visible step. Because the line lives in JavaScript, the browser's normal validation still applies if the script never loads.

For more detail on native validation rules and methods, see SitePoint's Complete Guide to HTML Forms and Constraint Validation.

The test intercepted the request, so it checked what the browser sent rather than a real server. It also doesn't show how other browsers behave.

Each legend includes the current step number, and the page title changes with it. After someone moves to another step, focus goes to the new legend. This gives keyboard users a clear starting point. The code doesn't move focus during the first page load.

The negative tabindex lets JavaScript focus a legend without adding it to the normal Tab order. Keep the focus outline visible, or replace it with an equally clear style. Screen reader behavior varies, so test with the browser and screen reader combinations your visitors use.

Native validation messages are useful for a small example. A production form may need persistent error text beside each field. If so, connect each message to its field with aria-describedby, set aria-invalid when needed and clear both after the error is fixed.

Also avoid asking for the same information twice. WCAG 2.2 Redundant Entry requires previously entered information needed again in the same process to be filled in or available to select. There are exceptions, including certain security needs and information that is no longer valid. This example alone doesn't make a whole website WCAG compliant.

Browser checks can be changed or bypassed. Your server should validate required fields, allowed choices, lengths and formats again. It should also handle spam, protect against cross site request forgery where relevant and escape stored values when displaying them.

Before release, try the complete form with only a keyboard. Check mobile layouts, zoom, browser autofill, Back and Forward navigation, slow connections and server errors. Make sure repeated clicks don't create duplicate records.

SitePoint's practical workflow for testing JavaScript forms is a helpful next step when you're preparing a broader test plan.

The example also leaves out conditional questions. If one answer can remove a later step, calculate the active steps before updating the progress label. Decide how to handle old answers from inactive branches, and enforce the same rules on the server.

Custom code gives you control over the markup and behavior, but your team must maintain validation, delivery, security and testing. A form builder can reduce the amount of interface code you manage. For example, LeadGen App lists multi step forms, conditional logic and website embedding among its features.

Whichever route you choose, test the finished form instead of relying on a feature list.

An accessible multi step form needs more than a few hidden sections. Start with semantic HTML, validate each visible step and keep progress and focus clear.

Pay special attention to required fields that appear later. A single hidden field can stop the first button from working, even when the visible answer is correct. Test that case, then check the whole journey with real users and a real server before launch.

#10108. When Everything Has Changed, What Do We Do Next?

It’s been a… year, huh? I think the most common emoji I’ve seen this year is “Face with Bags Under Eyes” (🫩) and that says a lot. One thing is for sure: the industry we make a living in is in flux, regardless of the outcomes of the AI bubble. Let’s take a look at how we can try and navigate that situation as best we can. But first…

Technology hyperscalers like Microsoft and Meta, along with AI startups like Open AI and Anthropic have transformed everything about tech and the job we do. This is true regardless of if they fail or somehow succeed in their quest to have everyone paying a subscription fee for their Large Language Model (LLM) technology, which is marketed to us as AI. I’ll refer to this technology as AI for the rest of this article for simplicity.

It’s not just tech jobs that have transformed either. Everywhere you go, you will see some slop, somewhere. Be it a flyer, website, a village poster or a food truck menu. Heck, people are even using LLMs to work out what’s special about their own children. The effects of this technology are far-reaching, way beyond our own industry.

The technology has hit us all in tech especially hard though. Mid-last year, I did some investigating because I suspected that so-called AI mandates were becoming increasingly popular and what I learned from conversations then feels like some sort of utopia compared to now. In the space between that piece being published and now, we went from token maxxing to companies realizing that actually, this technology is extremely expensive and not returning the promised outcomes.

The mandates have cooled, sure, but the demands to use AI to produce more volume, regardless of whether that volume is actually needed or not are still very much there. I’m in a fortunate position to have friendships with people all across the industry, from tech hyperscalers, to tiny little studios, and the stories I have heard from their work life are nothing short of horrifying. No one really seems to be happy with their work either.

This is such a long way away from even a handful of years ago where yes, there was a lot of problematic culture and outcomes from the tech industry, but people were generally quite happy with the work they were doing at least.

The sudden urge to sprint towards AI technologies over the last few years has had a devastating effect in so many places. The technology has encouraged suicide, devastated communities with the data center build-out and of course, displaced thousands of people’s careers. It’s worth noting that I’ve mentioned only a tiny sample of the harms there. If I listed them all, this article would be unbearably long and depressing.

Some harms I do want to touch on though are the effects on our communities in tech. The community spirit of open source and open knowledge was the foundation of the industry’s — especially the web industry’s — rapid success. At the root of everything there was people happy to share knowledge and build everyone up with them. Those people still do exist — myself included — but being one of those people is increasingly difficult.

As the numbers go up in the AI world, the numbers very much go down for independent educators, publishers and community conferences. There’s a developing and worrying culture of “I don’t need to skill up now because I can use AI for work”, so allow me to break down why that reductive view of your work and your career is harmful in the long term.

And don’t ever forget that! Let’s have a think for a second about what actually makes you a good developer, because typing characters in a text editor certainly isn’t it. What makes us good is experience, knowledge, nuance and above all else, creativity.

Look, I get it, AI really can feel like it’s a miracle machine, but it’s important to take a wider view. Along with the published studies on cognitive decline and the widening skills gap, it’s more important than ever to invest in your brain to keep your skills sharp because you’re not getting that repetitive reinforcement that writing code every day gives you anymore if AI tools do everything for you. There’s a reason that I often build most of my teaching around a linear build-along because the tangible nature of repeating patterns you’ve learned really helps that knowledge to sink in.

As always, this article is addressed to everyone, but I want to talk directly to founders (like me), decision makers, and managers for a second. A big part of our job, in fact, the most important part, is to ensure that our workers are looked after. I’m not just talking about compensation and perks here. I’m talking about our responsibility to ensure that our workers’ skills are kept sharp, their professional curiosities are satisfied and above all else that they are happy in the work they are doing. Our workers are much more than “number go up” vehicles.

You should be giving your developers more time to learn, rather than chasing endless volume. If AI enthusiasts are to be believed, there’s hours upon hours of time efficiencies created by this technology. Don’t fill that time with more volume. Instead, invest that time in developing your worker’s skills. It’s going to avoid burnout which yep, daily LLM use will likely cause in the long term.

One thing that is increasingly clear is that the health of the AI industry is not good. The bubble has already effectively burst in China and more worryingly for economy enjoyers, the “picks and shovels” — AKA the chips industry in Korea — has seen dramatic downturns. This is not how an “inevitable” outcome is supposed to happen!

The U.S. AI industry is on very shaky ground too. Circular financing, unbelievable levels of debt and a data center build out that simply cannot keep up with the required power levels to turn profits for hyperscalers and startups, mixed with a genuine hatred of the technology by the general public, creates enough risk in itself. Recent events, such as the Democrats dominating the midterms causing AI stocks to drop and as well as Nvidia’s stock because of its CEO being caught up in a spying scandal shine a light on the fragility of the industry. A healthy industry doesn’t work like this.

With all that in mind, what would be a really silly thing to do? Yeh, you guessed it: put AI at the heart of everything in your business. Sure, there’s open source models — which I think is the logical destination of the technology — but how many companies are going to be willing to maintain their infrastructure needs? Not many. The low barrier of entry of commercial AI technology is the selling point, let’s be honest.

Above everything, people need to be invested in, not pushed aside by technology like AI. As I mentioned earlier, it’s experience, knowledge, nuance, and creativity that sets humans far apart from AI. That technology does indeed keep evolving, but even the most ardent of AI supporters will concede that the rate of frontier model improvement is very much levelling out.

There’s never been a more important time to keep human skills sharp, so here’s some ideas on how to do that.

This is not just directed at individuals but also at founders, decision makers and managers. Our industry has been historically excellent at investing in skilling people up and we need to bring that back.

Give staff the time and budget to learn, to develop their skills and to engage with the community at conferences. You already know the positive outcomes that brings, so bring it back to your organization. At the absolute worst case, it’ll make the usage of AI in your organization more efficient and when the token subsidies inevitably end, more cost effective.

Again, token subsidies will inevitably end. So, getting thousands of dollars of worth from a $200 a month subscription will be something you’ll look fondly back on. When hallucinations from AI tools are suddenly costing thousands, does it make sense to force your staff to produce as much volume as possible? Of course not.

My advice is to re-evaluate those prior decisions and invest saved costs back into developing your workers instead, while there’s still a hope of them not burning out completely. Make sure you include your workers in these decisions and discuss the impacts of mandates on them. It’s important for you to understand the impact of these sort of decisions.

This advice is more aimed at developers, but stay paying attention, managers. As I said earlier, repetition (especially typing) reinforces new learning and existing knowledge. The slower pace also allows your brain to think, which from a quality of output perspective, is imperative. Doing none of that atrophies skills over time.

I recently read Jenn Schiffer’s great post. She did the Weird Web October challenge and wrote this:

[…] i encourage anyone who uses ai and/or vibe codes to consider doing an exercise like this without those tools as a way to challenge your brain and you can continue doing your regular work the way you do your regular work

I think that’s a very good idea, even if it’s messing around with a little side project — if you’ve got the energy from Producing Value all day, of course. Guess what bosses: this is another way you can make your staff happier, by giving them time to do this on the clock.

At the end of the day, you’ll miss the soul when it’s gone. It’s not gone yet though. Far from it. Independents like CSS-Tricks, my publication, Piccalilli, and Smashing Magazine are trying hard to keep the soul of our industry alive by sharing high quality educational content with the community.

Most of this content is free at the point of entry, but it certainly isn’t free to produce. We all pay writers fairly to share their knowledge with the community and we are all tiny organizations. We’re certainly not in this game to make huge profits, that’s for sure.

All of us could do more though, but funding is the ultimate barrier. For both companies and individuals, purchase courses, join the membership programs, and purchase ad space. With the right financial support, publications like ours can be a real force in pushing the good stuff back into our industry and bringing everyone up with us. That’s what we do it for.

Some course producers I recommend are Miriam, Sara, Mat, Scott, Brad and Ian, Josh, Ahmad, Christine, Vitaly, Ben and of course, myself! All of these will have generous Black Friday deals, so go ahead and ask your boss to invest in you.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

It is a tough state of affairs, but if nothing else, at the end of the day we’ve got one of the best and most resilient communities around. Front-end is built on knowledge sharing, and as long as we hold each other up and strive for the future we want to see, we’re in it with a fighting chance. It has been hard, and it’ll continue to be hard, but the challenge is all the more reason to rise to the occasion and persevere.

I seem to be one of those ‘unique’ developers who has managed to avoid using AI entirely so far. I work for a conservation not-for-profit as the only IT guy (there’s only 5 of us in total). We regularly work with legally sensitive information (protected species records) and early on I had a long discussion with my manager about the dangers to information security of using AI, as well as the various ethical and environmental costs of the technology. My viewpoint is that AI is a tool and, like any other, the decision to use it needs to be made after carefully considering the pros and cons. At the moment, the cons seem to massively outweigh the pros and I see no sign of that changing in the near future; though I may well be wrong – I’m not an expert on machine learning after all.

Despite being ‘lucky’ in regards to management supporting my decision to abstain from AI use, I’m still not immune to the depression and burnout that this new technology has brought. I spend 50% more of my time patching websites than I did 2 years ago, and trying to find packages to use in projects is far more time consuming that it used to be due to all of the noise created by slop. Frankly, it’s sucked all the joy out of my work, and I’m just on the periphery.

I think your emphasis on the dangers of not learning anymore is a serious hazard. I started writing a book on Building Websites for Beginners some years ago, but have since neglected it due to health issues and house moves etc. Maybe taking up writing again is a way to alleviate the sense of hopelessness we’re all feeling?

This really resonated — especially the “build stuff by hand” part. I run a small tool site that I built by hand, and doing it the slow way is exactly how I learned how typography and fonts actually work. AI tools are fast, but they never teach you that. The point about supporting independents hit home too — small sites like ours are the ones keeping the web human. Thanks for saying it out loud.

Great article, your viewpoint on AI always has me hooked. I feel like you’re being slowly abandoned by your peers within the community, whether they want to or not. Look up any of the (my own opinion, granted) core community sites and they’re infested with AI content, articles and functionality. A lot of the leading voices and companies in the front-end dev community, who were once idealistic and morally guided, work for some of those companies you mention and are now mouthpieces for the tech. Or a peddling the tech themselves.

Just like the AI industry, I’m not sure what the future looks like for the web community. But it’s encouraging to see sites like this come back and continue to inform and educate.

It’s the reason why I brought my indie newsletter WDRL back to life this week. It’s important that we play with new technology on our own, that we explore the details, the hacks, the pitfalls of web development and share them on blogs. If we solely(!) rely on AI for everything, that’s going to backfire soon. Let’s revive the old community style!

I was SO happy to see your newsletter return!

Amazing and well written article. I agree with all the points you said, specially the burnout one. As a webdesigner I feel the pressure to deliver as fast as a machine and is getting me depressed and tired. Also I don’t find happiness in “validating” or “patching” AI slop code :(. This week I had to see a javascript code from Claude code that was was: let kids (was the name of the variable), that had nothing to do with schools or toys or whatever the heck the AI had in mind. I’m tired of being AI’s code babysitter, I’d rather write the code myself. Thank you for all the hard work you put on this website. Greetings from Portugal (hence my grammar errors :D )

I’ve never really left a comment on CSS-Tricks, so this is my first to say I really appreciated this article and your thoughts on it. It’s been a very tired few years, lay-off laden and hype driven. And it’s nice to see someone caring about the learning, as that’s what drove me into writing code and doing mathematics in the first place. It’s a good feeling to see someone understanding that, in a field that seems to care less and less about understanding itself–hard to think of it as a “science” these days if we do not learn.

If we ignore that AI is one of several threats to kill us all (apart from climate catastrophes, a possibly nuclear world war—famines and generally decreasing living standards in consequence—, the disintegration of our societies with widespread civil unrest, and other increasingly probable threats), then I believe one way of looking at AI is that professionally, we’re in the unfortunate situation that it uproots what we’ve learned and invested in.

It’s probably normal for many (anyone in the field for 3+ years) to feel uncomfortable and unhappy about this effect of AI—especially in a world that says “everyone on their own” and knows out-groups that can be neglected or worse. But no matter how normal, the discomfort and the unhappiness (and perhaps even anger) aren’t going to help anyone, and the sooner we get out of that zone, the better it is for us individually and collectively. That seems especially true because we have important problems to address with AI, problems “just don’t use it” won’t solve (it won’t—AI isn’t going anywhere).

In it with everyone else here, I’m betting on sucking it up, adapting, and doing one’s part to push this in a constructive direction. This is not meaning to give up—it’s pragmatism and redirection of energy, because the ride has only just begun. What’s to come probably needs all our attention and energy.

(What do I know though;—and with the best wishes to this great part of our community!)

#10103. How Agent Anomaly Detection scores on benchmarks we didn't write

AI agents now read email, book travel and move money for the people who use them. Every tool call also pulls in text that someone else wrote, and whoever controls that text can try to steer the agent. OWASP calls this agent goal hijack and ranks it first in its Top 10 for Agentic Applications (2026).

In September we announced Agent Anomaly Detection in Private Preview on Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform. It reads your agents' traces and logs asynchronously, without adding latency, and flags sessions where an agent acts outside its intended boundaries. We tested this feature against benchmarks for this top risk of agent goal hijack.

Security tools tend to look good on tests their own team wrote, so we ran five non-Google-written, public agent-security benchmarks through Agent Anomaly Detection without changing anything. In four of them, the harmful instruction hides in data that a tool returns. In the fifth, the user asks for harm directly.

The model itself didn't follow about half of the harmful instructions it read. It refused three in four direct requests for harm, and ignored or failed at about 30% of the instructions hidden in tool output. Of the ones that were executed, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% for post-execution review. It raised false alarms on 2% to 16% of benign sessions, depending on the tools the agent uses. Poisoned memory is the clear gap: when instructions planted in the agent’s long-term memory made it act, Agent Anomaly Detection caught 4 of 18.

We ran each benchmark case through an agent built with the Agent Development Kit (ADK) using gemini-2.5-flash, on the benchmark's simulated apps. The agent sent its traces and logs to Agent Anomaly Detection, as a customer's agent would. Then the benchmark's own Python check read the final state of the apps, such as whether money left the account, and labeled the session. Deterministic code decides the label, not a model, so an injected instruction can't mislead the grader, and Agent Anomaly Detection cannot cheat the benchmark. Each agent first ran at least 300 benign sessions, because Agent Anomaly Detection learns how each agent usually behaves.

We also recorded whether a tool returned the harmful instruction to the agent. In many cases, the agent never opens the email or file that holds it. So each harmful session ends one of three ways:

In AgentHarm, the user sends the request, so the agent always reads it.

The five benchmarks are AgentDojo (NeurIPS 2024), InjecAgent (ACL 2024 Findings), Agent Security Bench (ASB, ICLR 2025), AgentDyn (2026) and AgentHarm (ICLR 2025). ASB plants the instruction in the agent's long-term memory. In all, Agent Anomaly Detection scored 1,774 sessions.

Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 87% of the harmful instructions that the agent carried out, and raised a few false alarms on ordinary office work. It's strongest on goal hijack and tool misuse, and weakest on poisoned memory.

Each finding carries a threat category, and four of Agent Anomaly Detection’s five categories align with the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. We graded each OWASP category on what this study showed, not on what Agent Anomaly Detection is designed to catch.

Public benchmarks concentrate on goal hijack and tool misuse, so our evidence is strongest there. We did not find public benchmarks with labeled agent traces for cascading failures or for identity and privilege abuse. Shared benchmarks for those categories would help the whole field.

By benchmark, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 85% of the carried-out harmful instructions on AgentDojo, 87% on InjecAgent, 99% on AgentDyn and 81% on AgentHarm. Over all harmful sessions, including the ones that the agent never read or resisted, it flagged 45% to 62%. In the 141 sessions where the agent never read the instruction, nothing happened, and it flagged just 1.

Results held across the apps. In 8 of the 11 apps, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged at least 95% of the carried-out harmful instructions. The lowest rate was in AgentDojo's Slack app, at 63%. Many of those instructions ask for one small action, such as a page visit, which looks like normal browsing.

Agent Anomaly Detection also flags many requests that the agent refuses. In AgentHarm, the agent refused most requests and carried out 108 of 412, and Agent Anomaly Detection still flagged 55% of the refused sessions, because the request sits in the user's own message, which it reads. When the agent didn't follow an instruction hidden in tool output, it flagged only 11% to 25%, because the instruction sits deep inside a tool result.

False alarms were rare on ordinary office work: on the three injection benchmarks, whose benign sessions are office tasks, Agent Anomaly Detection flagged 2% to 4% of benign sessions. On AgentHarm it flagged 16%. Those benign tasks are twins of the harmful ones, so they post to social media, run terminal commands and download files. High-impact tools in normal use look more like misuse.

Agent Anomaly Detection did less well in five areas. It flagged only 11% to 25% of the instructions that the agent resisted, because a resisted instruction changes little in how the agent behaves. It missed more small actions, like single page visits, because they look like normal work. And it raised more false alarms for agents whose everyday work uses high-impact tools. If your agent is one of them, expect more findings at first, and triage them by severity and threat category.

The other two areas are new agents and poisoned memory. Some Agent Anomaly Detection methods need a few hundred sessions of history: on AgentDyn, it flagged 33% of carried-out harmful instructions when each agent had about 220 benign sessions, and 99% with 360. And it caught only 4 of the 18 sessions where poisoned memory made the agent act.

Protect your agents with defense in depth. Use controls that stop harmful actions before they run, and Agent Anomaly Detection to find what gets through.

We used one agent model and simulated apps, and the benchmarks' benign tasks are likely simpler than your production traffic, so your false-alarm rate can differ. Agent Anomaly Detection returned no result for about 6% of the sessions we sent; counting those as misses lowers the overall rates by 1 to 2 points.

Agent Anomaly Detection is in Private Preview. To request access, read the Agent Anomaly Detection overview.

#10101. scroll-state()

The CSS scroll-state() query, surprisingly, has nothing to do with whether you’re doom-scrolling in a state of existential dread. It does, however, allow you to change the CSS used based on scrolling behavior. Far from doom, that’s pretty neat-o speedo. (Okay, you’re right, there’s nothing neat-o about a speedo). You can do lots of things that used to require JavaScript, like revealing the navigation when a user scrolls up or animating in a back to top link once the user scrolls down.

While scroll-state(), with those slimming parentheses, looks like a function, it’s not technically one. It’s a container query (@container) like style(). Since scroll-state() is a query, the website is basically asking the browser whether a particular feature of a container has a specific value. If it does, the styles within the brackets will apply. If it doesn’t have that feature at all or has the feature, but the value is different, the @container query is false and the styles in the brackets will not apply.

In the following demo (which we’ll dissect in a bit), a “Back to Top” link slides in from the bottom as you scroll down the page.

scroll-state() has four possible features and each of those has a variety of possible values.

Asks if a container has been scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a container can be scrolled in a given direction:

Asks if a sticky-positioned element is stuck:

Asks if a snap target has been snapped on a particular axis:

First let’s look at the scrolled feature. This is the demo we saw at the beginning where we animate a back-to-top link when we scroll down:

When the scroll container is scrolled toward the block-end (downward in this case) the translate applies and the .toplink element animates up into the visible area. Because we’re using a transition, when we scroll a different direction (up and down/block-start and block-end are the only options here), it nicely animates out.

While the scrolling direction in the demo above is pretty clear, what if this page was translated into a different language? Or we had a hero image that covered the entire viewport? One way we could indicate the scroll direction would be to have an arrow or other signifier animating and pointing in the correct direction. (I’m more a believer in constructing your page such that scroll direction is pretty obvious; however, I can foresee situations where an indicator was the best user experience or where translations/writing-mode changes could obscure what scrolling direction was appropriate.)

One nice thing about the scrollable feature is that when you scroll to the end of the scrollable area, it shuts off. So when we scroll to the block-end of the page (bottom of the page in this instance) scrollable: block-end is no longer true and our scroll indicator will revert to its default (in this case, display: none).

You could also combine the scrollable and scrolled features to remove the scroll indicator once the container has been scrolled either in the block-start or block-end direction. (We need both, because if we’re down in the middle of the page and we scroll up, scrolled: block-end will no longer be true and the scroll indicator will appear again.)

Another way we could enhance our demo is to have our navigation stick at the top while we’re scrolling down and change the look of the navigation once it’s stuck. The stuck feature must be on the element that gets stuck (as opposed to the scroll container, as was the case above). Also, because the container is set on the element that gets stuck, you cannot select the element itself from within the container query. You’ll need to position: sticky a wrapper element and “demote” all styles you want to change to the child.

The work of setting up the stuck feature happens in that .header-wrapper, but since we can’t apply styles to the container from within the container (not even with :has()). We need most of the styling to be in the

element.

Changing the size of an element in the header that affects the size of the header can lead to conflicting results. i.e. you scroll to a position where the header is stuck, which initiates the size change, but that reduces the size, which pulls it away from the stuck point, which cancels the size change which makes it stuck again, which initiates the size change and round and round we go. Even a small change can make it impossible to even scroll past the stuck point. Adding a transition can help with this and make scrolling work again, but you can still scroll to a place where the resizing happens in a loop. In the following demo, if you scroll down to a pixel or so past where the header touches the top of the viewport, you should see the stutter step.

There is one weird trick to fix this, though. No, really, there is! (shakes fist at the internet for making “one weird trick” an unbelievable phrase.) The one weird trick is to set a height on the element that’s getting stuck (.header-wrapper in the case above). Ideally, set it smaller than the final size of the header if you’re making the header smaller. This prevents the element from resizing because of a change in its content’s size. This does require the header content to overflow. If that’s a no-go for you, you’ll have to change its size with transformations. The transformed element has a sort of placeholder that stays in the DOM where the transformed element originally lived. This placeholder is what’s used to determine the sticky position and does not change based on what happened in the animation.

We can also add some styles when scroll snapping has been applied and that scroll has snappity-snapped (technical term). Similar to the stuck feature, snapped should be defined on the element that is snapped, not on the scroll container. If scroll snapping has not been applied to the element, then the snapped feature returns false and the container query does not apply.

First, we set up our scroll snapping in the scrolled container.

Note: As with most scrolling CSS (scrolling animations, etc.), if you’re using the document’s default scroll, the element is what’s being scrolled, not the .

The slide

s are what we’re going to snap to the size of the viewport:

As said above, the container type and name go on the snapped item. And then we modify descendants of the slide once it snaps:

Again, as with stuck, you cannot apply styles directly to the snapped element/the container, but only to the children of that element/container.

#10096. Node.js Hosting Between Serverless and VPS: Predictable Costs Without Cold Starts

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The first sign of trouble is rarely a crash. It is the invoice.

A small team ships a Node.js app on a serverless platform because it is quick to set up. Traffic grows, and the bill grows with it, in ways that are hard to forecast. Some visitors also wait a beat longer for their first response, because the function has to start up before it can answer.

The usual alternative is a virtual private server (VPS). The price is fixed, but the work is not. Someone has to patch the operating system (OS), configure the web server, renew SSL certificates and keep backups running.

Between the two sits a third option: managed hosting for Node.js. Here is how the three compare, and how to choose.

Platforms such as AWS Lambda charge for what runs. Lambda's event functions are priced on the number of requests and on duration, measured in GB-seconds, and the memory you choose sets the CPU and the cost per second.

That suits small or spiky workloads. A new app with little traffic can sit inside the free tier, which covers one million requests and 400,000 GB-seconds a month.

The surprises come with growth. Billed duration is multiplied by the number of requests, so even small inefficiencies in a handler can turn into significant costs at scale.

Then there is the cold start. When the platform has to create a new execution environment, the first request waits. AWS's own decision guide puts Lambda cold starts at roughly 100 milliseconds to over 1 second.

Since August 2025, AWS bills for that initialization (INIT) phase across all function configurations, so a cold start now shows up on the invoice as well as in the response time.

AWS's own analysis found they occur in under 1% of invocations, so steady traffic makes them far less. For a checkout page that does hit one, it is still a pause the customer feels.

There are fixes. Provisioned concurrency keeps functions initialized and ready, but it is billed for the time it is enabled. Newer options let one execution environment handle several requests at once, which means fewer cold starts.

Limits matter, too. A Lambda function can run for 15 minutes at most, and accounts start with a default of 1,000 concurrent executions. Long-running jobs need a different home.

A VPS fixes the pricing problem. A plan costs the same every month, whatever traffic does.

On an unmanaged VPS, the team owns everything above the hardware. That means operating system updates, a firewall, a web server such as NGINX in front of the app, a process manager such as PM2 to restart it if it crashes, SSL certificate renewals and backups that someone has to test.

None of it is exotic. All of it is a job. Left undone, it is how small teams end up with unpatched servers and no way to restore one.

For a team without a sysadmin, the real price of a VPS is the hours.

Managed Node.js hosting sits between the two. The app runs on a persistent infrastructure, so there is no scale-to-zero and no cold start between requests. The price is a flat monthly plan. The provider handles the work around it.

Cloudways Velocity is one example. It patches the OS, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically.

The stack is NGINX and PM2, pre-configured with caching, and PostgreSQL is provisioned on all plans. Deployment is by Git push with built-in CI/CD, and a WAF, malware protection, DDoS mitigation and Cloudflare Enterprise CDN are included.

It ships presets for 12 project types, including Next.js, Remix, Express, Fastify, Astro, Nuxt, Angular and n8n. Anything that builds to SSR or a static bundle and starts with a Node command can run under "Other". Runtime versions are selectable, with Node.js LTS 22.x and 24.x listed.

The trade-off is scaling. Velocity scales vertically: you pick a server size and move up when the app outgrows it. A traffic spike beyond that server's capacity is a capacity problem, not something the platform absorbs by adding instances.

Look at the traffic first. If it comes in bursts with long quiet gaps, serverless often wins. If it is steady, a server is usually simpler to reason about.

Take one handler and keep the assumptions the same throughout. Each request runs for half a second on 1 GB of memory. The rates are AWS's published x86 prices: $0.20 per million requests and $0.0000166667 per GB-second, after the free tier, as shown earlier.

Now compare Velocity's plans, which use a pay-as-you-go model billed on an hourly basis according to the selected plan, ranging from Starter at $20 a month (2 GB RAM, 2 vCPU, 5 apps) to Business at $400 (64 GB RAM, 16 vCPU, 150 apps). More importantly billing is strictly tied to the server plan rather than incoming traffic, a sudden spike in visits will not automatically raise your hourly compute rate.

The Lambda figures leave out API gateway charges, data transfer and logging. A single server has a ceiling, so load-test before assuming one plan carries 100 million requests.

And Velocity has metered extras: $0.033/GB for offsite backup storage, $0.02/GB for CDN bandwidth beyond the plan's allocation, and $1 per 10 GB of extra disk.

If you want to test managed hosting, you can try Cloudways Velocity during Black Friday with the promo code BFCM802 for 80% off for 2 months plus unlimited migrations, valid through December 4, 2026.

The point is the shape, not a winner. Serverless cost tracks usage. A server's cost tracks its size. Choose the one you can forecast.

The strongest case is also the most common one: steady traffic, a small team and a budget that has to be forecast.

When cloud hosting costs have to fit a budget, a flat monthly plan beats a meter. Velocity's plans give you Node.js hosting with predictable pricing, so finance can see the number in advance and it does not move with every spike.

Because the server stays on, there is no cold start between requests, and the first visitor after a quiet spell is not left waiting.

Cloudways says Velocity patches the operating system, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically. The people who built the product keep building it.

APIs, full-stack apps and automation workflows such as n8n need a process that stays up. A persistent server is built for exactly that.

When the app outgrows its server, you move up a size, and you can move back down if you don't need it.

For most small teams, that adds up to a simple choice. Velocity trades the theoretical ceiling of serverless for something many teams value more: costs they can forecast and an app that stays ready, without a DevOps hire.

Teams whose traffic is extreme, or who need code running at the edge, may still prefer a specialist platform, but that is the exception.

Serverless is strongest when work is irregular. A VPS is strongest when you want control and have the time. Managed Node.js hosting suits the large group in between: teams with steady traffic, a small headcount and no appetite for server upkeep.

Teams looking for managed hosting can explore Cloudways Velocity to deploy and scale Node.js applications efficiently.

Mansoor Ahmed Khan is a Senior Customer Editorial Manager at DigitalOcean, where he applies his background in linguistics and literature to a unique craft: turning customer success stories into powerful strategic assets. He works closely with different teams to uncover the most genuine user experiences, then uses his precise understanding of narrative and language to shape these stories. His focus is on ensuring every story is not just authentic, but also clearly communicates the real-world value of the platform, building a trustworthy bridge between the customer's voice and the company's message.

#10095. Andy Clarke answers six questions about Standout Designs for the Web

Web designer, author, and Stuff & Nonsense founder Andy Clarke has released Standout Designs for the Web, a new e-book that encourages designers to move beyond familiar templates and conventional layouts to create more personality-driven websites.

The book draws on Clarke’s thirty years of web design experience. It explores how storytelling, color, typography, imagery, and layout can work together to create websites that people recognize and remember. We asked Andy a few questions about the book and the ideas that went into it.

I’ve been designing websites for over thirty years, and I can’t remember a time when designers had more tools, techniques, and technology available to them. Yet, somehow, so many websites look remarkably similar.

I’m not suggesting every website needs a novelty design. Far from it. But I do think that — somewhere along the way—designers have become a little too comfortable with familiar patterns and predictable layouts.

I wrote Standout Designs for the Web to encourage designers to rediscover the creativity and personality that made me fall in love with the web in the first place.

Absolutely. Familiarity helps people understand how websites work, and there’s no reason to make something difficult to use just to be different. The problem is when familiarity makes every design look alike. When you visit a hotel website, an insurance company, or an online shop, you should be able to recognise something of the company behind it.

I call this sameness “commodity design,” and it’s perfectly fine when all you want is fine, but it does little to make a company recognisable or its website memorable. I’m not arguing against using conventions. I’m asking designers to make more thoughtful decisions about when to use them.

When I talk about storytelling, I don’t mean writing a lengthy company history or filling a website with marketing messages.

Every company has a story worth telling. It might be how it started, the people behind it, its personality, or something unexpected about what it does. Those details can influence everything from the words you write to the colours, typography, illustrations, and layouts you choose.

I spend a lot of time talking to clients because I’m looking for what makes them different. Sometimes, something they mention almost in passing becomes the inspiration for an entire design. The trick is recognising which details are worth developing and turning them into something visual.

Ha! Because I wanted a vehicle for my explanations and to have some fun. Wheelman is a fictional company for getaway drivers, and it gave me the freedom to explore ideas without worrying too much about the practical constraints of a real client project. Its characters, criminal connections, and rather questionable business activities gave me plenty of opportunities to experiment with personality.

But there’s a serious point behind the silliness. I use Wheelman to demonstrate how a company’s character can influence ordinary business pages, e-commerce, editorial content, and even transactional forms. If I can make a donation form for retired criminals interesting, designers have no reason not to bring more personality to everyday websites.

I think twelve-column grids have become the default setting for web design. They’re familiar and useful, but they’re only one way to organise content. When designers begin every project with twelve columns, they’re already limiting their possibilities before they’ve considered what the content needs.

In the book, I explore column, compound, and modular grids; ratios; symmetry versus asymmetry; and other ways to create rhythm and movement across a page. I’m not suggesting designers abandon column grids, but I want them to understand layout well enough to choose the right type and combine several—to make their layouts more interesting.

I’d like them to approach their next project with a little more curiosity. Before opening Figma or Sketch, choosing a typeface, or deciding on a layout, I’d like them to spend more time discovering what makes the company they’re designing for interesting.

Then I’d encourage them to question their first design decisions. Does this layout suit the content, or is it just familiar? Does this typography express the company’s personality? Can an illustration communicate something more effectively than an AI-generated image or a stock photo?

I don’t expect every website to be extraordinary, and I certainly don’t think every design needs to break conventions. But I’d like designers to feel more confident making decisions that give their work character.

You can purchase Standout Designs for the Web in the CSS-Tricks store.

Andy Clarke shows you how to create designs that people recognize and remember in his brand-new e-book, Standout Designs for the Web. Order it today!

Really interesting interview! I always enjoy seeing how experienced designers approach creativity, typography and visual storytelling on the web. A well-designed website is not just about appearance; it’s also about creating an enjoyable experience for visitors. Andy’s perspective on web design is inspiring, especially for people building and improving online platforms. Thanks for sharing this conversation!

#10090. Catch Go bugs that fuzzing misses with Gemini and a constraint solver

I help lead Google's agent skills program, currently with 175 agent skills that supplement community collections like go-skills. To keep that many skill files consistent, I wrote skill-lint, a standalone Go CLI that checks SKILL.md files and applies LLM-generated line edits before they ship. Even with skill-lint's own unit and fuzz test suite, a small slice or line-number mistake inside its Markdown parsers or edit applier can sit dormant until an unusual file or edit range triggers a runtime panic in the linter.

In Go, every slice or index operation that can panic at runtime compiles down to an explicit bounds-check trap in machine code, after the compiler's optimizer strips out the checks it can already rule out. To hunt for crashes, I compiled key functions from my code to see which panic traps survived optimization, and Gemini 4 Argon translated the path to each trap into rules for the Z3 constraint solver. An LLM-written solver model isn't a sound proof system, but it gives the agent an independent way to reason about control flow and synthesize concrete Go reproducers, and every crash it finds is real because go test runs the offending bytes directly. That workflow caught all three bugs I planted in test parsers and turned up three latent bugs in skill-lint.

In this post, I'll walk through how the Gemini and Z3 pipeline works, why go test -fuzz missed two of those crashes (and the one-line change that fixes it), and where this approach fits in a day-to-day Go workflow.

A recent post from Anthropic showed how formal verification catches latent bugs in C and C++ by having frontier AI models write machine-checked Rocq proofs across OSS-Fuzz libraries. Instead of sampling random inputs like a fuzzer, a formal tool works backward from each failure condition to solve for the exact input that triggers it. Full theorem proving is still too heavy for most day-to-day Go projects, so I wanted to see how much of that bug-hunting power an agent can get from an LLM and a constraint solver in about a minute per function.

In C and C++, reading or writing past the end of a buffer corrupts memory. Go catches out-of-bounds access at runtime instead, compiling every slice or index operation it can't prove safe into an explicit branch to runtime.panicBounds that panics, crashing the process unless recovered.

Before generating machine code, Go's SSA prove pass already eliminates most of those checks by tracking slice lengths and loop variables. That optimization pass leaves behind only the runtime.panicBounds traps the compiler couldn't rule out on its own, giving the solver a short list of candidate crash sites to target.

I compiled each target with GOOS=linux GOARCH=riscv64 go build -gcflags="-S -d=ssa/check_bce/debug=1" (-S prints the assembly listing and ssa/check_bce/debug=1 logs every surviving bounds check) and passed both the Go source and the disassembly to the model. Looking at Go source alone, you can't see which bounds checks the compiler already removed or where a register gets truncated to 8 bits; in the disassembly, both show up. I targeted RISC-V because it's the instruction set Anthropic's post targets for its Rocq proofs, and Go cross-compiles to it out of the box.

From there, Argon read the Go source and disassembly and returned a Z3 script modeling the path to each surviving trap, along with a patched function whenever it flagged a bug. Z3 then checked whether those constraints could be satisfied, and whenever it found a crashing input, go test replayed those bytes against the real function. Using an LLM saved me from translating assembly into solver constraints by hand. Argon's translation dominated the runtime, at about a minute per function, while Z3 itself finished in milliseconds.

Anthropic's approach relies on interactive theorem proving, where experts model machine semantics and Rocq checks LLM-generated proofs against those rules. In contrast, my workflow uses a constraint solver based on satisfiability modulo theories (SMT), relying on Argon to translate assembly directly into Z3 constraints. While a constraint solver doesn't provide a complete correctness proof without a semantic model, it is highly effective for finding bugs. Z3's check-sat command returns sat when input constraints can be satisfied and unsat when they cannot.

A sat answer ("definitely buggy") comes with concrete bytes aimed at the trap, and go test runs them against the real function. Once go test panics, you have a concrete bug reproducer regardless of whether Argon's model was sound. Like a Bloom filter for bugs, an unsat answer only means "maybe correct." Trading away a correctness proof is what makes the approach so practical: you get most of the bug-finding value of formal methods for a tiny fraction of the effort.

Anthropic's setup proved no-crash properties on whole binaries under a 64 MB budget; I checked individual functions on fresh 64-byte slices where cap(buf) == len(buf), so any slice past len(buf) panics. (If you want the full-binary Rocq setup for Go, theorem-labs/oss-verify includes a Go 1.27 runtime overlay that makes the runtime single-threaded and deterministic.)

For the fuzzing baseline, I ran unseeded go test -fuzz on Go 1.27.3 against each planted bug: one 3-second run and three 60-second runs, each with a fresh fuzz cache so a short time budget wouldn't explain a miss.

I started with three safe parsers to make sure the pipeline didn't flag clean code. The compiler's prove pass removed every bounds check in a TOML key parser on its own, and Z3 returned unsat on the remaining traps in an HTTP header scanner and a varint decoder.

Then I wrote three parsers with subtle panics. Here's the first one, ParseTwoChunkFrame:

In the RISC-V disassembly, the bug is much easier to see:

total := hdrLen + bodyLen adds two uint8 values, so MOVBU wraps the sum at 256 before the length guard checks it, while endBody adds the full 64-bit ints. Argon turned those instructions into a Z3 query asking whether a fresh slice of up to 64 bytes can pass the guard and still fail the endBody check at 0x0092:

Z3 returned a 59-byte counterexample with hdrLen = 48 and bodyLen = 236: their uint8 sum wraps from 284 down to 28 (so 28 + 4 ≤ 59 passes the guard), and buf[endHdr:endBody] then slices to index 288 and panics.

At 59 bytes, 19.4% of all possible (hdrLen, bodyLen) pairs trigger this crash. Yet a 3-second fuzz run missed it, and so did three 60-second runs.

Go checks a slice expression like buf[endHdr:endBody] against the slice's capacity, not its length, and Go's fuzzer rarely passes a slice without spare capacity. In a 10-second probe of 386,741 fuzz calls, 89% of inputs were views into a shared ~100 MB buffer, and only 0.07% had cap == len. With that much spare capacity, endBody (at most 514) never exceeds cap(buf), so the buggy parser returns bytes past the end of buf instead of panicking.

The second target, ExtractQuotedToken, hit the same blind spot when a trailing backslash pushed the index one byte past len(buf). Z3 caught it immediately with the 2-byte input []byte{'"', '\\'}, while the fuzzer absorbed the overrun in spare capacity.

Clipping capacity to length with a full slice expression turns any slice past len into a panic:

After that change, the fuzzer crashed both ParseTwoChunkFrame and ExtractQuotedToken in all three runs (median 0.28 s and 0.17 s). The third target, ParseMagicPacket, was a control because its signed int8 length bug panics on a negative index regardless of capacity, and the fuzzer found it in every run either way. Fuzzing still can't give you an unsat signal across the remaining paths, which is how I checked the three safe parsers and Argon's patches.

Next, I pointed the pipeline at skill-lint itself. A whole Go CLI is too large to hand to a solver directly. Even a 10-line parser links 1,559 symbols from the Go runtime, and the 28 MB skill-lint binary has 32,321 symbols in total. Following Functional Core, Imperative Shell, I left CLI flags, network calls, and file I/O to go test -fuzz and go test -race, and ran the solver only on pure functions and slice helpers extracted from file-reading code.

Of the five functions I tested, two scanners came back unsat (including a CRLF line splitter with six surviving bounds checks), and the other three had bugs. The sharpest was in ApplyReplacementChunks, which validates LLM-generated {StartLine, EndLine} edits against the initial line count and sorts them bottom-to-top so earlier edits don't shift later line numbers:

Z3 immediately returned sat because sorting by StartLine doesn't prevent two chunks from overlapping. In the extracted helper, which checks bounds once up front, replacing lines 3 and 4 of a 4-line file with one line shrinks lines to 3 elements, so a second chunk spanning lines 2 through 4 evaluates lines[4:] and panics with slice bounds out of range [4:3]. In the shipped ApplyChunks, a per-iteration bounds check turns that case into a misleading range error, while overlapping chunks that stay within len(lines) silently overwrite the earlier edit. Rejecting overlapping chunks after the sort fixes both.

The other two bugs were similar accounting errors. In the local fixer, target line numbers were checked once per violation before the inner chunk loop applied deletions that shrank the slice (a latent panic if a fixer ever returns a second chunk after a deletion, though DeleteLineFix only returns one today). In the prompt packer, a truncation check reserved a flat 100 bytes for a ~70-byte wrapper plus file path, overshooting the 2 MB context budget in the shipped code when a path exceeds about 30 bytes and panicking in the extracted helper.

When you check a function modularly like this, the target has to include its own entry-point guards (like the bounds check and sort in ApplyReplacementChunks). If an internal helper assumes preconditions that are only enforced by its caller or stated in a doc comment, Z3 will happily invent inputs that violate them. Because each extracted helper included its entry guards, false positives weren't an issue: across all six buggy functions in my tests, the first sat assignment Z3 returned triggered a real panic in go test without needing to filter out non-crashing candidates or loop back into the solver.

Once I pulled these helpers away from file I/O, a 3-second fuzz run caught all three bugs too. To make sure Argon wasn't leaning on source comments in my test files, I also reran the skill-lint targets with all comments stripped; Argon and Z3 still caught all three bugs and returned unsat on the two scanners and all three patches.

Staying entirely inside the target language makes a whole class of bugs hard for an agent to see. When an LLM reviews Go source directly, a line like if int(total)+4 > len(buf) looks like a textbook bounds check, and when it runs go test -fuzz, target-language runtime details like spare slice capacity or multi-step precondition checks can hide the crash indefinitely. Asking the agent to translate Go and assembly into a constraint solver forces a change of representation that strips away surface syntax and makes every bit width and slice bound explicit, while go test turns any solver hit back into a concrete Go reproducer.

For most Go code, fuzzing pure helpers with slice capacity clipped to length will catch bounds bugs in seconds. A solver is worth the extra minute on the traps that survive both the compiler's range prover and a quick fuzz run, especially in parsers and editors that compute offsets from multi-field headers or line ranges.

Single-threaded solver models don't see data races or deadlocks, so concurrent code still belongs with the race detector, deterministic concurrency testing, and Go 1.27's goroutine leak profiles.

If you're experimenting with solvers or coding agents on Go, let's compare notes on X or LinkedIn.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval
