#10083. Using Fallbacks for a Robust Front-End

Anyone who has been working in web development for a while will be familiar with this now-legendary meme from xkcd. It illustrates just how fragile the technical infrastructure of a website or app can be.

That’s why it’s important to use fallbacks to build a robust front end, either to quietly and discreetly avoid the error or to communicate it to our users.

The issue of robustness is so important that it is actually one of the four principles of the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG)! So how can we provide a modern front end without excluding users who use browsers and hardware that are only partially compatible or not compatible at all?

The answer lies in implementing multiple safety nets in HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, so that we are prepared for the worst-case scenario and can provide our users with the best possible experience.

The main difference between the use of fallbacks and progressive enhancement lies in the directions in which the two approaches aim.

While progressive enhancement is about automatically making certain features available as long as they are supported (forward), fallbacks are about what happens when something is not available or accessible (backward).

For example, we can offer a shiny new CSS feature that makes interacting with our website even more enjoyable. But if this feature isn’t available in some browsers, no one will notice, because a version that’s supported by older browsers is available instead. Progressive enhancement in action!

Another example: If JavaScript cannot be loaded for a critical application, we provide a fallback that either offers basic functionality or, at the very least, displays a message explaining what the problem is.

While one doesn’t necessarily exclude the other, their approaches are quite different. My personal recommendation is to always work with progressive enhancement as a guiding principle and to consider fallbacks whenever possible.

To evaluate new features intended for production, we must first determine the extent to which a feature is supported.

Since 2023, the Interop project has been in place, in which major browser vendors collaborate to release new features for the web platform more or less simultaneously. This serves as a general overview.

Related to this is the Baseline project, which shows, for specific features, the extent to which they are supported and how long they have been available. However, it is extremely important to note that it does not track browser support for accessibility features built into the web platform!

Another important resource for checking compatibility is Can I Use. Here, you can get a quick overview of browser support for various features.

Let’s start with the foundation of the World Wide Web: HTML. Modern tech stacks place a strong emphasis on client-side rendered JavaScript, which can offer many benefits to users in more complex applications. However, there can be just as many reasons why JavaScript might fail to load, so we need a strategy to address this.

In the event of a total failure, we should redirect users to an appropriate error page where they receive information about what went wrong and what not only they, but also we, can do to prevent this in the future. If you want to know how to write error messages that actually help users rather than frustrate them, check out Amy Hupe’s amazing article.

Because this kind of error feedback is so essential, the static site generator Astro, for example, offers the ability to add custom 404 and 500 error pages. You could discuss other possible error codes with your back-end team to implement additional safeguards.

But what if we also want to make individual sections or components more robust in case JavaScript isn’t available? For this, we can use the 

If JavaScript is not available, we will still display the components, but explain in simple and clear terms that they cannot be loaded at the moment and why that is the case.

We give users the option to reload that specific component so they can resolve the issue themselves. We also offer them the option to contact us directly via email if the problem persists.

Here, you could add a pre-written text to the  tag, which could include, for example, an internal error code specific to that component in the subject line and have a corresponding body text. This way, they would only need to click the button and could send the email directly using their email program of choice. Support would then have all the necessary information to resolve the issue without needing to ask for additional details.

No matter what your use case is, this pattern can be universally adapted and extended, and ensures that it works even if everything else goes wrong.

Let’s stick with our fictional dashboard component. Say we want to use the CSS anchor() function to display the data, which is fairly new and has solid, but not great, browser support.

Since this is a feature that can include multiple attributes, we need to check whether the ones we want to use are actually available. This is exactly where the @supports CSS rule comes into play! Here, it’s recommended to use the and operator: It should only be used if all attributes are available. If not, the more robust CSS with better browser support is used.

Here’s another fictional, simplified example:

This approach also serves as documentation: Once the feature has been around for a while and is more well-established, you can look directly for the @supports rule in your code base and check whether it is still necessary, along with its fallback.

Javascript offers us the ability to make our front end even more resilient with minimal effort. To do this, we can use the if…else statement to check whether a specific feature is available. For example, if we want to use the relatively new Navigation API, we could check for it as follows:

If we use a component-based approach, where HTML, CSS, and JavaScript are all contained in a single file, we can also use the conditional ternary operator to display this component or parts of it, or do nothing at all.

Our fictional dashboard component relies on data from the back end, and it only makes sense to display it once the data has successfully reached the front end. So, for example, we could do the following:

Depending on the use case, it makes sense to display a fallback or even nothing at all. You could also define the conditions more specifically, depending on how robust you want it to be.

My experience so far at various companies has been that the topics of fallbacks and robustness haven’t played as prominent a role as they really should have.

This is surprising, because as we’ve learned, it doesn’t take much to implement various safety nets on the front end, as long as you think ahead about what (shouldn’t) happen if something goes wrong.

HTML, CSS, and JavaScript provide us with specific elements and functions to ship robust code and thus avoid upsetting our users and, in the worst case, causing them to turn away from us.

#10079. Unlocking Stable On-Policy RL on TPU with Bitwise-Zero Train–Inference Mismatch

Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is notoriously sensitive to numerical instability: even tiny discrepancies between rollout generation and gradient updates can destabilize training or stall convergence. In LLM RL, the model serves as the policy, π(action | previous tokens), and true on-policy learning requires every action in an update to be generated and evaluated by that exact same policy. While a synchronous loop ensures each rollout batch is scored using the same weights that generated it, a critical numerical requirement remains: the sampler and trainer must evaluate that policy identically.

In practice, production RL stacks run sampling and training through separate, specialized execution paths. The sampler chooses each action token during incremental decoding and records its behavior logprob. The trainer evaluates the same token in batched forward-backward passes to compute its current logprob and gradients. Under the same weights, these differing execution paths can still assign different logprobs to the same token and context. The training schedule is on-policy, but the numerical execution has introduced a hidden off-policy gap that compounds over multi-turn trajectories.

Our zero train–inference mismatch (Zero-TIM) implementation, developed in Tunix and verified at multi-host scale across 64 TPU v5p chips (DP8×TP8), closes this numerical gap. At the same weights, the sampler and trainer assign bitwise-identical logprobs to the same action and token history—bringing state-of-the-art, true on-policy stability to TPU RL training.

A reduction is a tree of floating-point additions. Floating-point addition is not associative, so changing the tree changes where rounding occurs. The effect is easier to expose in bfloat16, which retains less precision than float32.

The following bfloat16 example isolates the effect of reduction order:

The same issue appears in model reductions: matrix products accumulate partial sums, normalization computes statistics, and tensor-parallel shards combine partial outputs. In our original stack, sampler decode and trainer forward used different token-array shapes and forward implementations. JAX specializes computations to their input shapes, and the training forward also runs inside a differentiated program.

Across these paths, reduction order and intermediate precision were not consistently aligned. Floating-point rounding turned those arithmetic differences into different logprob bits. The weights and model equations were identical; the arithmetic was not.

The contract is simple: across sampler decode, serving prefill, and trainer forward, all three paths must produce bitwise-identical logprobs for every sampled action—comparing raw floating-point bytes under the same rollout weights, token history, and sampling settings, with zero rounding or tolerance.

Why three paths instead of two? In a decoupled RL stack, a direct sampler–trainer disagreement shows that a numerical gap exists, but not where it enters. Introducing serving prefill as a middle checkpoint turns alignment into a systematic two-step diagnostic that any RL stack can adopt:

Sampler decode vs. prefill verifies that the serving engine agrees with itself between incremental token generation and full-sequence re-scoring, while prefill vs. trainer verifies that the serving and training engines agree across compilation and differentiation boundaries.

Achieving Zero-TIM does not require merging the sampler and trainer into a single monolithic engine. Instead, we use a generalizable four-part recipe that bridges separate serving and training pipelines across multi-host TPU meshes: locking within-shard kernel accumulation, ordering cross-shard reductions, standardizing the logprob scoring tail, and wrapping serving kernels with custom differentiation rules.

Inside each tensor-parallel rank, batch-invariant Pallas kernels control accumulation order and intermediate precision. Projection kernels traverse fixed contraction blocks in order, keeping the reduction blocking independent of token-row count. Partial products accumulate in float32, with explicit output casts.

The production kernel initializes its scratch accumulator before the tile traversal, then applies this core to each tile:

The next tile continues from the float32 accumulator, not the cast output. This construction covers the attention and MLP projections and the LM head. Dedicated kernels fix RMSNorm reductions and SwiGLU’s intermediate casts. The existing Pallas paged-attention forward is retained.

For row-parallel projections, each tensor-parallel rank produces a partial output. We separate communication from addition: ppermute transports the partials, then every rank adds them in the same source-rank order.

The reduction order is explicit in the program rather than selected by a collective implementation.

Alignment must extend beyond model logits to the probability of the sampled action. Decode, prefill, and trainer apply matching sampling transforms, use the same fixed Pallas log-softmax construction, and select the recorded action token.

Prefill and trainer score the action generated during rollout rather than choosing another action. We also preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns so that every path evaluates the same token history.

The trainer reuses the serving prefill computation. Custom vector–Jacobian products retain the serving kernels for forward evaluation and provide their backward rules. The matmul example shows this separation:

The differentiable replica mirrors the kernel’s accumulation and casts, but is used only to construct the pullback. Forward values still come from the serving Pallas kernel.

Training places that forward inside a differentiated JAX program. We pass --xla_allow_excess_precision=false through XLA_FLAGS to the compiler process, constraining excess intermediate precision in the operations surrounding the Pallas kernels.

Long trajectories are evaluated in fixed-size chunks, carrying the paged KV cache forward. All action logprobs feed the complete train-batch RL loss. The reverse pass propagates gradients through those cache connections and combines parameter-gradient contributions before the optimizer update.

Across the AI systems community, eliminating train–inference mismatch has rapidly become a state-of-the-art requirement for stabilizing large-scale RL convergence. Recent open-source efforts—including vLLM with TorchTitan and RadixArk/LMSys Miles—have demonstrated how batch-invariant kernels and bitwise numerical alignment prevent off-policy drift during policy optimization.

Our work delivers an open-source, modular JAX/XLA and Pallas implementation of this contract for TPU in Tunix. Verified across a multi-host 64 TPU v5p (DP8×TP8) mesh, these kernel and collective patterns provide a reusable blueprint that teams can adopt to achieve bit-exact on-policy RL across distributed JAX/TPU stacks.

To benchmark our TPU kernels, we run a 200-step numerical verification test on the synthetic FrozenLake environment using a public Qwen3-8B checkpoint as a baseline test case using 64 TPU v5p chips with a DP8×TP8 mesh. The recipe uses a GSPO-token loss with leave-one-out advantages. We compare Standard training with trainer-old logprobs and no additional sampler correction, token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS), and Zero-TIM.

The Zero-TIM configuration also uses token-in/token-out (TiTO) handling to preserve sampled token IDs across environment turns. The comparison runs retain their existing tokenization paths.

Zero-TIM records zero mean absolute sampler–trainer logprob difference at every logged step in this window, while its training solve rate increases. Standard and TIS retain nonzero differences: TIS adjusts their contribution to the update without removing the underlying mismatch. Zero-TIM reaches higher observed solve rates in these runs.

We developed Zero-TIM prototype as part of Tunix, Google's open-source, JAX-native library for LLM RL and post-training on Cloud TPU. The kernel patterns and three-way verification contract are modular by design—making them straightforward to adopt across existing multi-host JAX/TPU serving and training pipelines—and we are actively extending support to linear-attention and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

#10077. Style Queries as Conditional Modifiers

This article is adapted from a lesson in Writing Resilient CSS, the newest module in Kevin’s course, CSS Demystified. The module covers configuration options with custom properties, math functions, relative colors, :has(), style queries, and more, all put to work in practical, reusable patterns. The course is 32% off until December 2, 2026.

Modifiers have been with us for a long time, often with modifier classes, but also with attribute selectors. Some common examples are things like different versions of alerts and buttons.

For a lot of use cases, either of those approaches work great, but when it comes to layout, a lot of the time we want the context of where the element is being used to control things.

A card is a nice example of this, where we might have several versions.

The issue here is, if I’m using .card--compact, I probably want to do that for all the cards within a section. That can be annoying to apply to each card, but that’s rendered moot since most people are doing some sort of looping in a template like this these days.

And this works great, as long as we always want the cards to be compact.

However, you might put this together and send it off to the design team for approval, only for them not to like that when the compact cards are lower down the page, the smaller padding isn’t needed to create visual separation between the two sections. Instead of helping with the hierarchy, it’s simply creating a strange visual inconsistency.

We could use something like a resize observer to try and deal with this, but we have a better tool at our disposal: Container Style Queries, which are now Baseline Newly Available.

While they all start with container, for the rest of this article I interchangeable use container query or size query for a container size query, and style query for what we’re talking about here.

Before we get into the good stuff, a quick look at the basics of style queries.

You’re probably familiar with size queries, which work similar to @media but look at the size of a container, rather than the viewport. Container style queries use the same @container at-rule as container size queries, but instead of looking at a size, they look at the value of a custom property.

This reads as, “if the nearest container has --theme: high-contrast, style the .card like this.”

Just like with size queries, style queries look at an ancestor, not the element itself. That means this won’t work:

The custom property needs to be set on something above the card. Sometimes this can be annoying, and it always tends to catch people out, but because of how I use them, I don’t find this to be much of a limitation.

if() is also on the way, and allows for style queries as well, but if() is able to see the value of custom properties on the element it’s being used. The big difference is it can only be used for a single property, while style queries can be used for rules with multiple properties.

Let’s take a look at a card again, first with a modifier like we might do now. To change it up, we’ll use an attribute modifier instead of a class.

Switching this up to use a style query, we could do something like this instead:

If we’re in the mindset of how we’re used to using modifiers, this feels like a lot more work with no real payoff, but now, anything that can set a custom property can switch it: a media query, a container query, a :has() selector, or a parent section. The styles for the alternative layout are written once, and there are lots of ways to switch them on, making it so much more versatile.

One easy win here is if you’re working with a CMS. Now, the author of the page can chose from an option, which just sets one custom property value for a section of the page, and the appropriate styles kick in.

Where I feel like this has the most potential is when dealing with conditional styles like the sidebar we looked at earlier, where we can enable the compact card layout however we want:

Here’s an overflow grid pattern I use quite a bit:

The problem is that it always overflows, even if I don’t have enough children to warrant it. Like here, the first row, the third card is just barely overflowing, making it awkward more than anything.

One solution is to use a container query and :has(), where we let the items fill the space when there are fewer than five of them, and there’s enough space:

And this works really well! But sometimes, we might need to make some bigger changes depending on where the elements are being used.

Let’s say, for example, our page has a hero component that goes to two columns on larger screens, with a heading on one side and a set of featured articles in an overflow grid on the other. The container query is making sure the overflow is on in the narrow space, but horizontal scrolling there seems a little awkward.

When the split happens, I’d rather the grid scrolled vertically instead, and we can leverage style queries to pull this off.

First, we give the overflow grid a configuration option for its direction, defaulting to columns:

Now the split can change when it goes to two columns:

And this works, but the result isn’t great. The grid is still sized for horizontal scrolling, so we end up with a tall, awkward column, and no overflow at all.

This is where style queries come in. The overflow grid can “listen” for the direction change and adjust its own styles to match.

The split section isn’t styling the overflow grid directly, which is possible, but always awkard, and leads to fragile code bases. Instead, we created some configurable settings on that grid, and simply adjusted them as needed.

I don’t think style queries mean it’s time to throw out modifier classes (or data modifiers). They still work great, especially since a style query can’t look at the element it’s styling. If I simply want my button to be an accent button, or a pill shape, then a modifier makes a lot of sense.

On top of that, a regular media query or container query, or even an intrinsic pattern might get the job done, and that’s great too.

Where style queries shine is when the condition and the styles need to live in different places, or when one condition needs to affect several things at once, like the split above. Instead of a layout reaching in and styling its children, it changes a few settings, and each component decides how to respond.

That’s a big part of what I think of as writing resilient CSS: components that keep working, and adapt sensibly, no matter where they end up.

This article is adapted from a lesson in Writing Resilient CSS, the newest module in my course, CSS Demystified. The module covers configuration options with custom properties, math functions, relative colors, :has(), style queries, and more, all put to work in practical, reusable patterns. The course is 32% off until December 2, 2026.

#10069. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.

#10066. AIを活用したサイバー攻撃の増加を受け、日本がサイバーセキュリティへの緊急資金拠出を推進

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

この対策パッケージには、官民合同のサイバー対応チーム、経済省が主導するAI専門チーム、および政府の新たな能動的サイバー防衛権限の行使に関する裁量の拡大が含まれている。

Translation provided by Dark Reading's internal AI translation tool.

日本においてサイバー攻撃や情報漏洩が急増していることを受け、日本政府は、攻撃に迅速に対応し、インフラのセキュリティを強化するための産官の専門家チームを設立することを目的とした、100億円（6,300万ドル）規模の緊急サイバーセキュリティ対策パッケージを可決した。

この戦略の4つの柱には、サイバー防衛へのAI導入を含む基礎的なサイバーセキュリティ対策の全国的な徹底、国内のサイバー捜査能力の強化、サイバー攻撃の被害者への支援拡充、そして民間企業と連携してサイバー攻撃に共同で対応することが含まれている。

高市早苗首相は、国のサイバーレジリエンス（回復力）を高めるため、これらのサイバーセキュリティ対策を「迅速かつ効果的に」実施するよう求めた。

「今この瞬間も、不正アクセスや情報漏洩の事案が後を絶たず、人々の日常生活や企業活動に多大な影響を及ぼしている」と、高市早苗首相は11月6日に緊急対策パッケージを発表した際の声明で述べた。「政府のあらゆる資源を動員し、サイバー攻撃への対応能力を全面的に強化していこう。」

サイバー攻撃の急増は、日本企業や政府機関に甚大な被害をもたらしている。サイバーセキュリティ企業（旧トレンドマイクロ）であるTrendAIのデータによると、2026年の最初の9か月間で600件以上のサイバーセキュリティインシデントが公表され、2025年通年の594件を上回った。公表されている報告数は控えめな推計に過ぎない。日本の個人情報保護監督機関は、4月から10月中旬にかけて2,094件の情報漏洩事案を記録しており（原文は日本語）、これも2025年度通年の総件数を上回っている。

他の地域ではAIが攻撃者を支援しているものの、サイバーセキュリティ企業TrendAIの脅威調査共同責任者であるライアン・フローレス氏は、脅威に関する声明の中で、インシデントの増加と攻撃者によるAIの利用との間に明確な関連性は確認されていないと述べた。

Related:Japan Pushes Emergency Funding for Cybersecurity as AI-Assisted Breaches Rise

「サイバー攻撃は世界的に増加しており、その結果として侵害やデータ漏洩も増加しています」と同氏は述べた。「AIが直接関与しているわけではないかもしれませんが、攻撃者が標的を見つけたり、操作を自動化したりするのを支援するAIの能力により、サイバー攻撃は大規模化しています。組織は、被害に遭う確率を下げるために、サイバーリスクを低減し、脆弱性を解消する必要があります。」

最近の侵害事例には、冷凍食品大手のニチレイやビール・食品大手のアサヒに対するランサムウェア攻撃、およびGMOインターネットグループを標的としたデータ侵害などが含まれる。これらの攻撃の多くは犯罪的な性質を持つが、盗まれた情報が他の犯罪グループや国家主体のアクターによって、日本の国民に対して悪用される可能性があることが懸念されていると、日本の通信大手NTTのチーフ・サイバーセキュリティ・ストラテジスト、松原美穂子氏は指摘する。

「攻撃者が十分な個人情報を収集・集約できれば、個人の嗜好から経済活動に至るまで、日本国民の詳細なプロファイルを構築できる」と同氏は述べる。「そのデータは、世論を操作したり、日本の機関に対する信頼を損なわせたりするための標的型偽情報キャンペーンなど、日本に対する影響工作に利用される可能性がある」

日本政府は、緊急資金を活用して、サイバーセキュリティ体制全体を強化するための4つの広範な取り組みを推進する意向だ。閣僚会議の公表された議事録によると、政府は、最近の多くの情報漏洩は適切なセキュリティ対策によって防ぐことができたはずだと指摘し、最初の取り組みとして、各組織が基本的なサイバーセキュリティ対策を確実に実施するよう促すとしている。

2つ目の主要な取り組みは、調査および情報収集能力を向上させ、より精度の高い分析を可能にし、その結果を国民に提供することで、攻撃への備えを強化することである。第三に、政府はサイバー攻撃への対応を強化し、国際的なパートナーシップを構築するとともに、積極的なサイバー防衛措置を実施する。最後に、政府は被害者を支援する能力とインフラを整備し、市民が損害を回復し、企業が事業を継続できるようにする計画だ。

「被害を受けた組織や個人が迷うことなく支援を受けられるよう、利用しやすい相談窓口や専門家による直接的な支援を拡充し、二次被害を防ぐための情報共有を強化する」と、日本の議事録には記されている。「また、官民の専門家を柔軟に派遣するための全国的な仕組みの構築にも着手する」

10月1日、日本の「積極的サイバー防衛法」が施行され、警察や自衛隊（SDF）に対し、日本の国家安全保障にリスクをもたらすあらゆるサイバー攻撃に対して強力な措置を講じる権限が与えられた。

「多くの国が、日本がいかなる種類のサイバー攻撃に対して、どれほどの速さで、どの程度の強度で対応できるのかを知りたがっているだろう」とNTTの松原氏は述べ、次のように付け加えた。「この緊急対策パッケージは……法律の枠組みを経済全体にわたる実用的な能力へと転換し、攻撃の可能性が高まる中、あらゆる規模の組織が攻撃を予防、検知、対応できるよう準備するために必要である。」

しかし、この資金はあくまで第一歩に過ぎず、日本がサイバーセキュリティへの投資を継続するには、さらなる資金が必要になると同氏は指摘する。

「この対策パッケージの重要な点は、サイバーセキュリティを個々の組織に完全に任せておくことはできないという認識が示されていることだ」と松原氏は述べる。「セキュリティ評価やインシデント対応、復旧支援といった形の政府による支援が、大きな違いを生むことができる。」

#10062. Agents are sessions, not requests: size GKE node pools for AI agent sessions

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Your company wants to move its coding agents from developer laptops to a shared GKE cluster, for persistence, scale, and governance. Each of those agent sessions stays open all day but works only part of it, and how you run them decides how much of your node bill pays for waiting.

By the end of this article, you will know why an agent session needs a different runtime than a web request, how Agent Substrate and AX run sessions on GKE, and how to identify cost optimization opportunities in your GKE node pools. This article assumes you know Kubernetes Pods and node pools. If not, check out the GKE overview.

A web request lasts milliseconds. The server answers it, keeps nothing, and serves the next one, so Kubernetes scales a web app by its traffic: more requests, more Pods.

An agent session works another way. A coding agent's workspace can stay open for the developer's whole workday: it keeps its memory and its files between prompts, and it works only after a prompt arrives.

How much of that day does the agent really work? Much less than it waits. In a July 2026 study of agent traces, Banruo Liu and colleagues found that in a typical session with several prompts, the agent waits for the user about 80% of the time. A turn, one prompt and the agent's whole response to it, takes about 6.5 minutes of agent work on average

Put those numbers on one developer's day. With 2 prompts an hour over an 8 hour workday, which is our assumption, the agent works 22% of the day and waits for nearly 78% of it. If each session holds a machine, you pay for that waiting time

Teams usually pick one of two answers, and both work well for web apps.

The first is to give each session its own Pod. The session keeps its state, but its Pod holds 1 vCPU and 1 GiB all day. For example, a c3-standard-4 node fits 3 such Pods after GKE's reservations, so in our cost model 100 coding agents need 34 nodes and about USD 1,288 a month, for agents that work 22% of the time.

The second is to scale the Pod to zero between prompts. The node's capacity is released, and files on a persistent volume survive, but the process and its memory do not, so the next prompt starts a new agent process.

Based on that, you want a third option: keep the session, and free the machine while the session waits.

Agent Substrate and AX are built for that: the developer's session stays open, and the sandbox that serves it gives its machine back while the session waits. Together they cover the parts of an agentic platform that are the same for most teams: the control plane, the runtime, and the sandbox. Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. AX answers what the agent is.

Following the CNCF donation of gVisor, Agent Substrate, and OpenRL in November 2026, you can run Agent Substrate on GKE or on other Kubernetes clusters. Agent Substrate 1.0 is generally available on GKE.

One distinction helps from here on. The session is the multi turn interaction between the developer and the agent, with its conversation history. The compute that serves it is an isolated sandbox: an actor in Agent Substrate, or a Task in AX. Parking acts on the sandbox, and the session stays open.

Agent Substrate answers where and how an agent runs. It separates the stateful agent from the compute it runs on. An actor is the sandbox for one agent, and it holds the agent's running processes, files, and identity. A worker is a reusable sandbox drawn from a warm pool of Kubernetes Pods, and one worker hosts several actors.

An agent runs code that a model wrote, so anything in its environment can leak through a prompt injection or a bad command, API keys included. Gateways control the actor's inbound and outbound traffic and add credentials on the way out, based on the actor's identity, so credentials stay out of the agent's environment.

SuspendActor snapshots an idle actor to object storage and frees its worker, and the next request restores the actor on a warm worker with room for it. Agent Substrate snapshots memory by default and falls back to files when it cannot capture memory. Many parked sessions share a small warm pool, so in our cost model the same 100 agents need 16 nodes instead of 34.

AX answers what the agent is and what it knows. You declare a Task, which says what runs (image, command, and limits), and a Workspace, which says what the agent knows (repositories and files). AX runs each Task as an actor. ax suspend parks the task and keeps its /workspace. Today AX asks for a volume snapshot, so the files come back on resume and the agent's processes start fresh (runner guide).

AX passes suspend, resume, and delete to Agent Substrate. The AX roadmap plans to add budgets, policies, and gateways, which limit what an agent may do, and a record of what each task did.

Two details decide the bill before you size anything.

First, you decide when a sandbox parks. Agent Substrate does not suspend an idle actor by itself (#424), so your agent harness, or the service in front of it, calls the suspend API after an idle timeout. Until then, an idle actor keeps its worker. When idle actors hold every worker, a request for another actor waits up to 5 seconds by default, then fails with HTTP 503 (Agent Substrate docs).

Second, you decide how much CPU each sandbox is promised. An actor is sized by its CPU and memory limits, and each worker books those limits against the capacity you declare for its pool (API guide). Our cost model sets that capacity to what the node really has, so every actor keeps its full limit. You can declare more capacity than the node has, and then you overcommit CPU at the pool instead of in each Pod.

Our cost model prices parking for the same 100 coding agents, at Google Cloud on-demand list prices, as of November 2026, in us-central1.

With a 2 minute idle timeout, each session holds a worker nearly 29% of its open time. A warm pool sized for bursts (the 99th percentile of sessions holding a worker at once, if sessions act independently, plus 15% headroom) needs 15 nodes. Add one node that runs around the clock for the Agent Substrate control plane and its PostgreSQL database, and a job that scales the pool to zero after hours:

Parking saves nearly 40% of the node bill. The pool is full about once in 3,000 turns, so a prompt rarely meets that 503. In an hour at 2.5 prompts instead of 2, the same 15 nodes are full for 3% of turns, so size the pool for your busiest hour.

Keep in mind that parking pays most when your agents wait the most. At 2 prompts an hour, the saving is nearly 40%. At 4 prompts an hour, the agent works 44% of the hour, the pool grows to 27 nodes, and the saving is 4%. Above about 4 prompts an hour, one Pod per session becomes the cheaper option, so measure your prompt rate first.

Also, the pool size is yours to set. The autoscaling option in the GKE install guide follows worker Pods, not suspended actors, so scale the pool to zero after hours with a scheduled job: a pool that runs all 730 hours of the month costs nearly twice the one Pod per session bill.

Finally, the fleet size matters. The Agent Substrate control plane costs the same for 10 sessions or 1,000, so parking pays from about 37 coding sessions, or 13 for an assumed chat assistant that works about a minute an hour.

Overcommit changes the comparison. If your Pods request less CPU than their limit, Kubernetes places them by their requests. A Pod that requests 0.25 vCPU with a 1 vCPU limit packs 12 to a node (its 1 GiB of memory caps it there), so the same 100 coding agents fit on 9 nodes, fewer than our parked pool. Agent Substrate can overcommit too, through the capacity you declare on the worker pool, and our cost model does not price that case.

Overcommit has the same cost on both sides: agents share CPU and slow down when their neighbors are busy, during builds and tests. Compare like with like before you decide to park: Pods and actors with the same CPU guarantee.

Back to the shared cluster. Agents are sessions, not requests: size the warm pool for the sessions that hold a worker, not the sessions that are open.

We would park long lived sessions that wait most of the hour, and keep one Pod per session when developers prompt more than about 4 times an hour. Size the warm pool for working time plus a short idle timeout at your busiest hour, and compare with your current Pods at the same CPU guarantee before you park.

Thanks for reading! If you run agent sessions on Kubernetes today, how many prompts an hour do your users send? The prompt rate is the input we are least sure of. You can reach Ivan on LinkedIn or X with your numbers, questions, and corrections.

#10059. Japan Pushes Emergency Funding for Cybersecurity as AI-Assisted Breaches Rise

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

The package adds public-private cyber responders, an AI-focused team run by the economic ministry, and more leeway to use the government's new active cyber-defense powers.

To read this story in Japanese, please click here (translation by our internal AI tool).

With Japan facing a surge in cyberattacks and breaches, the Japanese government has passed a ¥10 billion ($63 million) emergency cybersecurity package aimed at establishing teams of industry and government experts to quickly respond to attacks and better secure its infrastructure.

The four pillars of the strategy include thoroughly implementing basic cybersecurity measures country-wide, including adopting AI for cyber-defense, strengthening the nation's cyber-investigations capabilities, better supporting the victims of cyberattacks, and partnering with private industry to jointly respond to cyberattacks.

Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi called for the cybersecurity measures to be implemented "promptly and effectively" to enhance the nation's cyber resilience.

"Even at this very moment, incidents of unauthorized access and information leaks continue to occur, significantly affecting people's daily lives and business activities," Takaichi said in a published statement announcing the emergency package on November 6. "Let us mobilize the full resources of the Government to strengthen Japan's overall capabilities to respond to cyberattacks."

Related:AIを活用したサイバー攻撃の増加を受け、日本がサイバーセキュリティへの緊急資金拠出を推進

While AI has aided attackers elsewhere, TrendAI has not established a firm link between the increases and the use of AI by attackers, Ryan Flores, co-lead for threat research at TrendAI, a cybersecurity firm, said in a statement on the threats.

"Cyberattacks are increasing globally, and so are the breaches and data leaks as a result," he said. "While AI might not be directly involved, its ability to help the attackers find targets and automate their operations makes cyberattacks scalable. Organizations need to shrink their cyber exposure and close the vulnerabilities in order to lower the odds of being victimized."

Recent compromises include ransomware attacks on business, such as frozen food giant Nichirei and brewery and food giant Asahi, and data breaches targeting GMO Internet Group. While many of the attacks are criminal in nature, the worry is that the stolen information could be used by other criminal groups and nation-state actors against the Japanese public, says Mihoko Matsubara, chief cybersecurity strategist for Japanese telecom giant NTT.

Related:UK Wades Into Tech Sovereignty Amid AI Policy Changes

"If attackers can collect and aggregate enough personal information, they can build detailed profiles of Japanese citizens, from personal preferences to economic activities," she says. "That data could potentially be used for influence operations against Japan, such as targeted disinformation campaigns to manipulate public opinion or undermine trust in Japanese institutions."

The Japanese government intends to use the emergency funding to pursue four broad initiatives to improve its overall cybersecurity posture. Pointing out that many recent breaches could have been prevented by good hygiene, the government's first initiative is to ensure organizations are implementing basic cybersecurity measures, according to the published Japanese notes of the ministerial meeting.

The second major initiative is to improve investigation and information collection capabilities, allowing better analysis that can then be delivered to the public to better prepare them for attacks. Third, the government will strengthen its response to cyberattacks, establishing international partnerships and implementing active cyber-defense measures. Finally, the government plans to create the capability and infrastructure to support victims so that citizens can recover damages and businesses can remain operational.

Related:UK Warns Its Critical Infrastructure Faces Grave Risk

"Accessible consultation points and direct expert support will be expanded so that affected organizations and individuals can connect to support without confusion, and communication to prevent secondary harm will be strengthened," the Japanese notes stated. "Work will also begin on building a nationwide mechanism for flexibly dispatching public- and private-sector specialists."

On October 1, Japan's Active Cyber Defense legislation came into effect, authorizing the police and the Self-Defense Forces (SDF) to take strong measures against any cyberattacks that pose a risk to Japan’s national security.

"Many countries would be keen to learn how quickly Japan could respond to which kind of cyberattacks, and with what level of intensity," says NTT's Matsubara, adding: "This emergency package is ... necessary to translate the law’s framework into practical capabilities across the broader economy, preparing organizations of all sizes to prevent, detect and respond to attacks as they become increasingly likely."

However, the funds are only enough to get started, and more will be necessary to continue Japan's investment in cybersecurity, she says.

"The important aspect about this package is that it recognizes that cybersecurity cannot be left entirely to individual organizations," Matsubara says. "Government assistance, in the form of security assessments and incident response or recovery support, can make a difference."

#10055. Where should your code live in a Docker Sandbox?

Giving an agent a sandbox also means deciding which files it will work on. You can share a checkout from your host, give it a separate working directory for a task, or keep the repository entirely inside the sandbox. Each arrangement changes how you, the human, participate in the work.

You might want to watch the diff in your local editor and make changes alongside the agent, or give it room to manage several tasks while you review the results. sbx supports both. The way you organize the checkout can change as you ask the agent to take on more of the work.

Sharing a checkout puts the agent in the working tree you already use. Run sbx run from your project directory, and it mounts that directory at the same absolute path inside the sandbox:

Open a file in your local editor while the agent works on it, and you’ll see changes as they happen. Git works from either side, so you choose whether you want to let the agent make commits or you do it yourself. Development servers on your host can pick up the edits through hot reload, using the runtime and dependencies installed on your host.

This fits close collaboration on one task. The coordination cost comes from sharing that working tree: switching branches changes the files the agent sees, and your edits become part of its ongoing work. If you want to switch to something unrelated while the agent continues, you’ll need another checkout.

NOTE: Dependencies written into the shared checkout are shared too. Packages with native binaries, such as esbuild, may work in the Linux sandbox and fail on a macOS or Windows host. Reinstalling on the host can reverse the problem. Keep separate dependency installations, or run the app consistently in one environment.

Git worktrees give each task a directory and branch. You can mount each worktree in its own sandbox, while your original checkout remains available for other work. The agent’s edits appear in the worktree. You can inspect either with your local editor and development tools. Each task also gets its own running environment, with tools to prepare and a sandbox and worktree to clean up afterward.

There is a Git limitation with mounting only a linked worktree. Its .git file points to metadata outside the mount, which the agent can’t reach. It can edit files, but checking status, branching, and committing have to happen on the host. The future of software development still needs you to run git status.

sbx supports a clone mode that gives the agent a separate checkout with Git available inside the sandbox. You keep your local project, but sbx mounts the host source as read-only and creates an independent clone for the agent. Its branch changes and dependency installations stay in its checkout.

Bringing the result back becomes a handoff. While the sandbox is running, you can fetch committed branches through the Git remote sbx adds on your host. Or the agent can push to the repository’s remote and you can retrieve the branch through your usual Git workflow. Your local editor and development server see the changes after you bring them into your checkout.

A mountless sandbox has no host workspace directory attached. Clone the repository inside it, and the sandbox holds the checkout and its development environment. Your host copy of the project shares no connection with the sandbox checkout.

This workflow resembles working on a remote development machine. Agents connect to the project environment, and you can open a shell with sbx exec or connect an editor to the sandbox over SSH. Changes and dependency installations stay separate from your host. To bring work back, you push a branch to the Git remote.

One sandbox per project can be a useful mental model here. The project and its tools persist across agent sessions and sandbox restarts. An agent can create worktrees per feature it’s working on, push branches, and create pull requests for review. Starting another task doesn’t require you to prepare another host worktree and sandbox, and the agent has Git access in each working directory. Clone mode supports this internal-worktree approach too.

Those tasks share tools and services, so running servers or changing the environment still needs coordination. Separate sandboxes are useful when tasks need independent environments as well as separate files. Branches alone don’t isolate simultaneous edits in one working directory.

That independence adds some distance when you want to try the result. Your usual local editor isn’t looking at these files, and a development server running inside the sandbox needs a published port to reach it from your browser.

Start the development server on port 8080, bound to 0.0.0.0.

Then publish the port from another terminal on the host:

Open http://localhost:8080 in your browser. It’s possible to work interactively this way, but there’s more ceremony than opening a project already on your filesystem.

An environment file can make setup repeatable, selecting tools and cloning the repository at creation with something like the community github-clone kit. Recreating the sandbox then gives you a fresh environment. Push or copy out work you want to retain first: removing a sandbox deletes files held only there.

An API change might need a corresponding frontend change, or another repository might provide useful reference material. You can mount several directories into one sandbox, with read-only access for reference repositories. Local editing and previews remain available, but each writable checkout needs branch and change coordination. Task-specific checkouts also leave directories to clean up afterward.

A workspace manager such as jganoff/wsp can assemble selected repositories for a task. Mounting that workspace gives the agent the projects it needs together. These are standard clones with their own Git metadata, so the agent retains Git access, unlike a mounted linked worktree.

The same scope fits inside a mountless sandbox: clone the repositories needed there and keep the whole workspace in the sandbox. The number of repositories and where their files live are separate choices.

The checkout’s location, the sandbox’s lifetime, and the repositories it contains are choices you can combine. You can keep a project sandbox around for many tasks, or mount a workspace assembled for one job. What suits you may change when you want to work more closely with the agent, or give it more room to manage the work itself.

The Git workflows documentation covers the mechanics of direct mounts, clone mode, and host worktrees.

Docker launches a new brand built on trust. Learn why Docker is evolving for AI agents, what stays the same, and how to run agents securely with Docker.

Coding agents can reach more than their task needs. See how Docker AI Governance controls network, filesystem, credential, and MCP access, then test it.

Docker publishes fixed images for both Java4Shell and the NGINX HTTP/2 in the Docker Hardened Images (DHI) catalog.

#10053. The Ecosystem is the Answer: Docker, the Blueprint Alliance, and the Importance of Collaboration in the Agentic Era

In 2025, the average global Fortune 500 enterprise had fewer than 15 AI agents in use. Gartner expects that number to explode to more than 150,000 by 2028. Only 13 percent of organizations think they have the right governance for today, and AI is evolving and moving faster than any other technology paradigm in our history.

Today, most security leaders face an agentic Sophie’s choice: allow AI adoption without the right controls and hope for the best, or block it and become the obstacle to a priority the business has already set (while adoption continues in the shadows, anyway).

But there is a third option, one in which organizations fully tap into the transformative power of agentic agents without having to live in fear of a catastrophic security failure. The good news is history has already shown how to get there.

Historically, technology adoption has followed a reliable pattern. A capability spreads, each vendor and team builds its own approach, the inconsistency becomes expensive and ineffective, leading the industry to agree to a shared baseline. Adoption then accelerates.

Take the credit card industry, for example. The big credit brands each ran their own security programs, and merchants had to satisfy every one. This was, of course, cumbersome for the vendors, but also for the customers, too. Every time a customer wanted to adopt a new technology, they had to manually build out security and adoption plans, adding significant adoption and procurement delays.

Rather than continue on their own, the credit card companies came together to create PCI DSS 1.0 in December 2004 and formed the PCI Security Standards Council in 2006. Buyers now had one thing to ask for, and auditors had one thing to check. Collaboration overcame complexity, and everyone benefited.

Security-minded technologists can find another example closer to home in the Center for Internet Security (CIS). In the early days of the internet, cybersecurity was a veritable wild west. Enter the CIS, which was founded in 2000 because the industry lacked prescriptive, recognized hardening standards. To this day, each CIS Benchmark is built by consensus among volunteer experts, vendors, and academics.

Docker itself is no stranger to the development and furthering of shared standards. As just one example, in 2015, Docker contributed its image format and runC runtime to the Open Container Initiative. A shared baseline at the runtime layer let registries, runtimes, and clouds interoperate.

Each of these examples played a different role: PCI mandated, CIS guided, and OCI standardized.

Agents are in the everyone-builds-their-own phase. Each product solves a real piece of the problem, but which piece, and how the pieces fit, is rarely clear. “Agent governance” or even “AI governance” can mean almost anything.

Adoption is incremental. A developer allows common commands, widens filesystem access, then connects GitHub, a cloud tool and an MCP server, each through a prompt that makes sense on its own. The logs mostly record that the developer acted. Security is often the last to know.

Much of the risk is familiar. Shadow adoption looks a lot like early SaaS and cloud. Credential sprawl and supply chain exposure are old problems. Inventory, least privilege, audit, and joiner-mover-leaver discipline still apply.

Existing controls still matter, but they were not built for this mix of programmability, access, and autonomy.

As with the credit industry and PCI, cybersecurity and the CIS, and containerization and OCI, collaboration offers the answer to this new era of complexity. That’s why Docker is not just focused on building tools like Docker Sandboxes and Docker AI Governance, but in taking an ecosystem-centric approach to problem solving for our customers. Docker is already a founding participant in multiple initiatives aimed at developing valuable reference architectures for AI security workflows, including the Blueprint Alliance and Agent Baseline.

Both start from the same premise: no single vendor or enforcement point can solve this.

The Blueprint Alliance sets out how the pieces fit together. Okta, AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler are founding members, with GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen as strategic advisors. The open, multi-vendor reference architecture answers four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond? Members have committed to interoperability testing over MCP, OCSF, SSF and CAEP, and to publishing reference integrations.

Agent Baseline sets out what each piece must do and how to verify it. It fills the gap between threat lists, which say what can go wrong, and the capabilities an enterprise needs in place. It defines six outcomes (Discover, Constrain, Authorize, Observe, Validate and Respond) and 35 open, versioned controls, each with a permanent identifier you can cite in an audit finding or RFP response. Docker, Snyk and Keycard drafted it, and the current release is v1.0-draft. Controls stay where they already live, in the identity provider, the sandbox and the SIEM.

Blueprint says how the pieces connect. Agent Baseline says what must be true and can be checked. Both advise starting with discovery before policy, and testing the kill switch before you need it.

For partners, the roles are defined. The Alliance whitepaper assigns integrators the operating model: assessing a customer’s current stack against the architecture, assigning ownership across the pillars, tiering risk, and running lifecycle and compliance over time. Technology partners get a published target to integrate against, and the reference integrations cross vendor lines by design. The partners who can design, integrate and operate across those lines are the ones customers keep.

Most Alliance members govern agents from the outside: who the agent is, what it can reach, and what it is observed doing. Docker governs the inside: the environment where the agent executes. As agents move from suggesting actions to taking them, trust has to extend beyond the model to the places where they run. Docker has helped define a shared baseline for the runtime before, and now we’re working with the Alliance and our partners to make that baseline concrete, open, and testable in the age of agents.

The execution environment is where policy gets enforced and evidence gets produced.

That boundary is where policy gets enforced and evidence gets produced. The Alliance whitepaper notes that without runtime support to pause an agent without collateral impact, API-driven containment “provides a false sense of security.” Agent Baseline’s CON-03 calls for isolated, confined execution, which is the job of Docker Sandboxes, while Docker MCP Gateway and Docker Hardened Images cover tool access and the supply chain.The Secure Software Factory reference architecture, drafted in part by Docker authors, applies Agent Baseline to agents that ship code.

Baselines let adoption move faster, not slower. Security and platform leaders can assess against the baseline today. Agent Baseline controls are citable, and Okta offers an AI Blueprint Assessment Tool. Partners can start with the Docker Navigator Partner Program, which rewards AI governance work, and with the reference integrations the Alliance publishes. Docker’s VP of Partners Leon Jones explains the program here. Download the blueprint at blueprintalliance.ai and read the controls at agentbaseline.org.

Docker launches a new brand built on trust. Learn why Docker is evolving for AI agents, what stays the same, and how to run agents securely with Docker.

Docker publishes fixed images for both Java4Shell and the NGINX HTTP/2 in the Docker Hardened Images (DHI) catalog.

Learn how Docker AI Governance secures agentic AI with sandbox policies, MCP tool governance, and audit visibility to support controlled AI agent workflows.

#10051. Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

As highlighted in our announcement post, we said that each new model generation lets agents do more on their own. However, the more an agent can reach, the more one bad decision can cost. You've probably seen a few of these in the news lately:

Because these events produced no system crashes or logging errors, the teams behind these agents had no way to know until the damage occurred.

Agent Anomaly Detection on the Gemini Platform watches for exactly these issues. It reads the traces and logs your agents already produce and flags sessions where an agent goes outside the job it was built for.

Before you point it at your production agents, you'll probably want to see it work in a test environment. In this hands-on guide, we’ll walk you step-by-step through setting up Agent Anomaly Detection and using it to capture your very first agent behavior anomaly. By the end of this post, you'll have:

To get started, open the companion notebook Detect anomalous agent behavior with Agent Anomaly Detection in a separate tab (available on Google Colab or GitHub). As you read through this guide, it will prompt you when to execute notebook cells or interact directly with the Google Cloud console.

If a step can be done in the UI console, this post shows that path, with screenshots. The notebook does the same thing through the Agent Anomaly Detection API, so by the end you'll also have the code to automate enablement and pull findings into your own tooling.

2. A place to run the notebook: Google Colab, Colab Enterprise, or a local Jupyter environment. The notebook installs the latest ADK for Python itself. Agent Anomaly Detection is compatible with agents running ADK 1.2+, though ADK 2.0+ is preferred for best results.

3. A Cloud Logging bucket and an Observability dataset in either US or EU multi-region. (Both resources need to be in the same multi-region)

4. In the notebook: run the Get started cells. They install the packages, authenticate you, take your project ID, and turn on the four APIs that Agent Anomaly Detection needs.

First, let’s create a Customer Support Agent called “anomaly-detection-refund-demo”. Our example agent is responsible for helping customers check their order history and request refunds. However, it has a deliberate flaw: the agent has access to an overprivileged fetch_records tool which can read any table, including a `payroll` table containing sensitive employee data. All three parts of the lethal trifecta are here: the agent can read private data, it takes input from people it can't vet, and it sends what it finds back in its reply. Tool permissions and guardrails should stop most of this, but they only block what someone thought to write down. Agent Anomaly Detection flags the sessions that get past them, usually within a few minutes.

The notebook has the full agent. The part that matters is the tool, which never checks the table name against an allowlist:

All data is canned and in memory, so nothing real is read or refunded. This tool is intended to be over-permissive to illustrate AAD functionality. Don't copy this tool into production.

The Step 1 cells deploy the agent to Agent Runtime. Two details in the deploy cell are what make the agent visible to Agent Anomaly Detection. First, it sets three environment variables on the deployment, which turn on telemetry and write prompt and response content to Cloud Logging, where detection looks for it:

Second, it sends the agent one test message right after deploying. Agent Anomaly Detection discovers agents through their telemetry, so an agent that has never served a request does not appear under Monitored agents.

The deployment takes about 5 to 10 minutes and prints the agent's resource name and the reply to the test message:

For more information on how to create and deploy an agent, take a look at these guides: Creating an Agent and Deploying an Agent.

In the console: The sample code we provide already enables logging and telemetry when deploying the agent. Verify this by navigating to the Agent Deployments page (Gemini Platform > Agents > Deployments) and clicking “Configure” next to the recently created anomaly-detection-refund-demo agent to open the Observability Service Configuration.

Great! You now have a working agent that is successfully configured and ready to be monitored by Agent Anomaly Detection.

Now let’s set up Agent Anomaly Detection to monitor your agent’s traffic.

In the console: navigate to the Agent Platform > Agents > Audit page and click “Enable”.

Choose your agent’s Logging Bucket and Observability Dataset, making sure they are in the same region (us-multi-region or eu-multi-region) enable “Automatically monitor new agents”, then click “Agree and continue” and Agent Anomaly Detection will be set up in your project.

If you'd rather do this in code, the first Step 2 cell in the notebook creates the same configuration through the API, using the default _Default bucket and _Trace dataset with automatic monitoring on.

Setup takes around 30 minutes. Once it’s done loading you’re ready to proceed to the next step! Because you enabled automatic monitoring and the agent already served a request in Step 1, setup picks it up right away. Check that it shows as Active under Settings > Monitored agents before you continue; sessions that start earlier aren't analyzed.

Now that the agent is being monitored, let's see Agent Anomaly Detection in action! The Step 3 cells send one normal conversation and one anomalous conversation, each in its own session. The normal conversation is a customer listing their orders and getting a refund for a broken lamp. The anomalous conversation simulates a user who finds the fetch_records flaw and uses it to pull payroll data:

Notice that the user brings almost nothing: a customer ID and one first name. Asking for a table that doesn't exist makes the agent reveal the existing tables, which is how they learn there is a payroll table within reach.

The notebook prints both transcripts with the agent's tool calls, and the session IDs you'll look up next.

Anomaly Detection will scan the sessions within 5 minutes. In the landing page, you should see that a couple sessions have been processed and one anomalous session detected.

Let’s take a look at the anomalous session in more detail. Click the session id to open the detail view or paste the anomalous session id into the search bar.

In our run, the top finding was Tool misuse at High severity, with 95% probability. Your detectors and severities may differ, because the models behind the analysis are non-deterministic. The recommended solution is to restrict the `fetch_records` tool to read from specific tables.

Click an invocation in the Session pane to see its analysis in Details. Each invocation is Medium on its own, but together they make the session High.

To stay informed without watching the console, connect Agent Anomaly Detection to your existing alerting workflows.

In Cloud Monitoring: Agent Anomaly Detection publishes two metrics: Detected events, which counts anomalies by detector and severity, and Monitored agents, which tracks your detection coverage. To view these metrics, click the arrow next to "View in Cloud Monitoring" on the Anomaly detection page and select a metric to open it in Metrics Explorer.

To be notified of new anomalies, go to Monitoring > Alerting, click "Create policy", and select the Detected events metric. Leave the filters empty so the alert covers every anomaly, including the demo's, then set the rolling window to 5 minutes with the delta function, and set the threshold to above 0. Once you know what's normal for your agents, add a severity filter (for example High or Critical) so you're only alerted on the findings that matter. A metric-absence policy on Monitored agents will also alert you if monitoring stops.

In Security Command Center: Each anomaly is also published as an AGENT_SESSION_ANOMALY finding in the Security Command Center if it is enabled for your organization. Click "View in Security Command Center" to triage these findings alongside your other threats, use "Set security marks" to tag and track them, and set up notifications for high-severity findings.

You’ve now seen Agent Anomaly Detection in action: it successfully flagged the session where our agent went off-script to read payroll records, while evaluating the benign session, and correctly not flagging an anomaly. The finding told you why it was flagged and how to fix the underlying issue, and alerts in Cloud Monitoring and Security Command Center ensure you are notified of future anomalies.

When you're ready to secure your own production workloads, follow this rollout plan:

To review the complete list of prerequisites or to request Private Preview access for your project, head over to the Agent Anomaly Detection documentation.
