#10049. CNCF Score project improved its security posture by joining the Docker-Sponsored Open Source Program

Score, a CNCF Sandbox project, recently joined the Docker-Sponsored Open Source (DSOS) Program to strengthen the security of its container images and release pipeline. In this post, the Score maintainers share what we adopted (Docker Hub, Docker Scout, Docker Hardened Images, the Docker GitHub Builder, and OIDC connections), the results we measured, and how other open source projects, within the CNCF and beyond, can apply the same practices by default.

In September 2025, the CNCF announced a partnership with Docker to expand infrastructure support for project maintainers through the Docker-Sponsored Open Source (DSOS) Program. The partnership gives CNCF projects access to Docker’s security scanning tools, container registry, and best-practice resources at no cost.

This aligned closely with the discussions held at the Maintainer Summit NA 2025 in Atlanta, which centered on increasing collaboration across CNCF projects and demonstrating security best practices.

The Docker-Sponsored Open Source (DSOS) Program supports non-commercial open source projects with Docker tooling and infrastructure.

Before: Score publishes different container images: the CLIs of score-compose, score-k8s, score-helm and score-radius; and the sample-score-app used for demos and testing. We previously published them in the GitHub Container Registry here.

After: Through the DSOS Program, we now publish these images on Docker Hub, which increases their visibility and gives the community a verified, trusted source.

All the container images are now available on Docker Hub.

The program also provides usage analytics that show how the community uses these images. Here is an example:

Before: As a CNCF project, Score follows the OpenSSF best practices. In addition, the OpenSSF Scorecard GitHub Action runs continuously. This was a solid foundation, but we had no dedicated security scanning tool in place.

After: The DSOS Program gave us access to Docker Scout, which we now use in our pull requests and on our container registry (Docker Hub). This gives us clear visibility into the CVEs introduced or fixed by each pull request and release.

The following GitHub Actions step uses docker/scout-action on every pull request to compare the image built from the PR branch with the one built from the main branch:

Here is an example of a comment posted by this docker/scout-action step in a recent Pull Request:

The comment details the comparison between the two images (before versus after): size, packages updated, added or removed, CVEs introduced and resolved, and compliance with our policies in place (run as non-root, no critical CVEs, no packages with AGPL v3 licences).

The analysis goes beyond the images themselves: it also covers the Go libraries and binaries they contain.

The Docker Scout console also provides a single view of CVEs, packages, base images, and policies across all of our container images, which makes it easy to see what is used where, and in which version.

When Docker made Hardened Images freely available under Apache 2.0, we saw an opportunity to simplify our security posture while staying compatible with our existing Dockerfiles and workflows, even though we were already using multi-stage builds and distroless base images.

Docker Hardened Images are minimal, security-focused container base images designed to reduce inherited risk in downstream projects. They are built and maintained with security and transparency as defaults, and offer:

Take the score-radius CLI, which used a golang:alpine and a distroless images and now uses dhi.io/golang and dhi.io/static images. The changes required in the Dockerfile were minimal:

In the associated PR here, image size dropped by 0.3 MB while the number of packages stayed the same and the CVE count remained at 0. We could have stayed with Debian, but chose to move to an Alpine base image (DHI provides the two options):

The same applies to the NodeJS sample-score-app, which used nodejs on top of an alpine image and now uses a dhi.io/node image. The changes required in the Dockerfile were minimal:

In the associated PR here, 22 packages were removed (including the package manager and the shell), along with 2 CVEs:

Note: We were able to present a short walkthrough of our DHI integration during the CNCF webinar held on January 23, 2026, which accompanied the Docker Hardened Images announcement for CNCF projects.

Although not directly part of the DSOS Program, we took the opportunity to adopt the new docker/github-builder GitHub reusable workflow to release our container images.

This model provides a build pipeline maintained in the Docker organization, with a version-controlled BuildKit environment, multi-platform builds distributed across runners where beneficial, and signed SLSA provenance recording both the source commit and the builder identity.

The trade-off is intentional. You retain control over when the build runs and which inputs it uses, while the build implementation itself lives in the Docker-maintained workflow rather than in per-repository job steps.

Before, the GitHub Actions workflow to release our container images was using this sequence of actions:

After, this same workflow is now only using the reusable workflow docker/github-builder/.github/workflows/build.yml:

Docker GitHub Builder gives us a Docker-maintained build pipeline with signed SLSA provenance and SBOM attestations, helping consumers verify the builder identity and trace images back to their source commit. Signing uses GitHub OIDC, so we don’t need to manage long-lived signing keys. With caching enabled in our workflow, cache entries are also signed and verified before reuse, helping protect against cache poisoning. See the Docker GitHub Builder documentation for more information about its security model.

The following command verifies the signature on the attestation manifest for the selected image platform:

Most recently, with an update released on July 21, 2026, Docker introduced OIDC connections. This feature allows pulls and pushes to Docker Hub to use a short-lived token instead of a long-lived Organization Access Token (OAT).

An OIDC connection establishes a trust relationship between Docker and a trusted third party, so you no longer need to maintain long-lived credentials. Docker exchanges short-lived tokens with the other vendor, which can grant fine-grained access to your Docker resources.

By replacing a static, long-lived token with a short-lived one and making it issued on a per-workflow basis, we removed a credential that could otherwise be stolen and misused.

Participating in the Docker-Sponsored Open Source (DSOS) Program has been rewarding, and it has helped Score adopt a security-by-default foundation. Using Docker Scout, Docker Hardened Images, OIDC connections, and the Docker GitHub Builder has strengthened the security posture of our container images and brought broader benefits to the Score projects and repositories.

If you maintain a CNCF project or any open source project, we encourage you to explore the Docker-Sponsored Open Source (DSOS) Program. The security improvements were measurable, and the onboarding process was straightforward.

The full implementation across the Score repositories is tracked in GitHub issue #376.

Attending KubeCon NA 2026 in Salt Lake City? Feel free to connect with the Score maintainers during the Maintainer Summit on Sunday, Nov 8th and at the Kiosk #P-17B on Thursday, Nov 12th between 10:15am and 2pm, you will see Score in actions with the new Score Labspace! You can also meet the Docker team at booth #141 in Hall C!

Note: This work was completed after I joined Docker. It was, however, an initiative we wanted to pursue as CNCF project maintainers from the moment it was announced in September 2025, both to improve our security posture and to share what we learned with the broader community.

We hope this post helps you strengthen the security of your own projects.

#10046. Docker and ActiveState Extending Secure Defaults to the Packages Developers Install

Trusted content is the foundation security-conscious organizations expect all builds to start from. Docker has made that possible for everyone by providing the foundation free. Docker Hardened Images (DHI) are open source under Apache 2.0, with a catalog of 4,000+ minimal images (and growing) built from source and continuously maintained by Docker. The catalog also carries customizable Helm charts and over 65,000 hardened system packages. For most services, adopting DHI is as simple as changing the FROM line.

Teams building on a secure foundation now want the same assurance for the open source their applications install. That open source is a different layer of the supply chain, where thousands of upstream projects are pulled in at build time by pip, npm and Maven, and by coding agents as well.

Docker is committed to one standard for trusted content across everything teams build on. A new partnership with ActiveState, the company that has been securing open source since 1997, extends that standard to the packages themselves. Together, we give teams the same assurance on both layers, from the hardened images Docker maintains to the packages developers and their agents install on them. Once configured, the integration allows developers and AI coding assistants to continue using familiar package managers and development workflows, while approved dependencies are sourced through governed repositories rather than directly from public registries.

Most applications still start from a large, generic base image, and the cost of that choice keeps growing. A large image increases the attack surface and accumulates stale dependencies, and the CVEs it generates land on developers long after the image was chosen. By removing unnecessary packages, rebuilding components from source and continuously maintaining the image, Docker Hardened Images reduce the vulnerabilities inherited from the container foundation. Images can achieve near-zero known CVEs, which means less remediation work and less scanner noise before application code is introduced. The hardening and patching are handled once in the foundation, for every service that builds on it.

Above the base image sit the dependencies an application brings with it. DHI secures the operating system packages and the language runtime that ship in the image. The application’s own libraries are pulled in at build time by its package managers from public registries, where trust is assumed and seldom verified. That layer needs what the image now has, a source that admits only the versions a team has approved and stands behind the fix when one of them needs it. Coding agents now pull those packages too. As Mark Lechner, CISO wrote in April, a compromised dependency pulled by an agent has the same blast radius as a compromised developer machine.

This year’s supply chain incidents have followed one pattern. As Lechner documented, attackers steal developer credentials, use them to poison trusted packages, and the compromised packages steal more credentials. The axios compromise earlier this year put two backdoored versions of a library downloaded 83 million times a week into circulation for about three hours. Most of these attacks burn out within hours. A build that resolves fresh from a public registry, with no lockfile or cooldown in front of it, has nothing between it and a version published an hour ago. The slower failure is the vulnerability that surfaces in one of those libraries months later, when no one outside the team is accountable for the fix. Either way, without trusted content on both layers, the risk lands on your team.

Docker Hardened Images are the foundation, and Docker stands behind them. Each image ships with a signed SBOM and SLSA Build Level 3 provenance, with full, unsuppressed CVE visibility. A security team can see what is in the image and how it was built. For teams that need remediation on a predictable clock, DHI adds a 7-day SLA on critical and high CVEs, FIPS and STIG variants, and secure customization on its paid tiers.

While DHI secures the image, ActiveState hardens the open source built on it. ActiveState provides the Curated Catalog, a private, policy-governed repository of open source components that security teams curate and developers and AI coding assistants consume safely. The catalog holds only the packages a team has approved. ActiveState compiles every component from original source code in a SLSA Level 3 environment with full provenance and complete SBOMs, in place of pre-compiled binaries from a public registry. When a fix becomes available upstream, ActiveState rebuilds the component and publishes it to the catalog automatically under SLAs of 5 business days for critical CVEs and 10 for highs.

With DHI and the Curated Catalog together, both layers carry provenance, SBOMs and a remediation SLA from the hardened image to the packages built on it. That gives security teams a consistent evidence trail across the software supply chain. The artifact repository teams already run, Artifactory or Nexus, points at the Curated Catalog as its upstream the way it points at PyPI today. Developers keep the same pip and npm workflow, and coding agents are served the governed version. None of this replaces the pinned digests, cooldown periods and sandboxed agents Lechner recommends. It gives those controls a governed source to point at.

AI coding agents are changing how quickly organizations create software. They can generate applications, select dependencies and initiate builds at a pace that traditional security review processes were never designed to support. Still, faster development should not mean blindly accepting more supply chain risk. Docker Hardened Images reduce the security burden of maintaining container foundations. ActiveState’s Curated Catalog extends governance and managed vulnerability remediation to the open source libraries those applications depend on. The result is a more consistent approach to software supply chain security, with trusted foundations, governed dependencies, verifiable build evidence and clear remediation responsibilities.

The goal is not to add another security checkpoint that causes friction in your build process. It is to make the secure path the easiest path for developers, platform teams and AI coding agents alike.

Security scales when the defaults are strong and the foundation is maintained by a team accountable for it. In an increasingly agent-driven development environment, the security of what gets built depends heavily on the trustworthiness of what those agents are allowed to consume. Docker now resells the ActiveState Curated Catalog alongside DHI, and ActiveState resells DHI to its customers. The hardened image and the packages built on it are both available through Docker.

Principal Product Marketing Manager, Docker

Vishrut Iyengar is Principal PMM for Security at Docker, focused on Docker Hardened Images, supply chain security, and securing what AI agents build.

#10044. Docker Sandboxes vs. Containers: Choosing the Right Isolation for AI Agents

If you’ve used Docker for a while, “Docker Sandboxes” might read as “a container with an AI agent inside.” Containers have been the unit of nearly everything Docker ships for over a decade, and a sandbox might sound like one more way to package a workload.

They’re actually different technologies. A container is an isolated process that shares the host kernel with everything else on the machine. Docker Sandboxes are dedicated microVMs with their own Linux kernel, filesystem, network, and Docker Engine, built for autonomous coding agents that need a whole development machine to work in. A sandbox can run containers, which is part of why the two are easy to conflate.

In his WeAreDevelopers keynote, Docker President Mark Cavage said containers are doing exactly the job they were designed to do, but we’re asking them to do a different job.

Containers were built to isolate applications, which they still do well. But a coding agent is an actor: it makes decisions and operates on your systems the way a developer uses a laptop. It clones repositories, installs packages, starts databases, builds images, and tries again for hours. It can also act on instructions from files, web pages, and tool output that nobody reviewed.

That different job needs a different boundary, and where that boundary sits determines how far an agent’s mistake can travel. This post covers how Docker Sandboxes and containers isolate workloads, what changes beyond the kernel, what a sandbox costs you in setup, and how to pick.

A container is a process, or a group of processes, running on the host with its own view of the filesystem, process tree, and network. Linux namespaces provide that view, and cgroups meter CPU, memory, and I/O. Every container on the machine also shares the host kernel, which is why containers start quickly, pack densely, and carry so little overhead.

This design has more than a decade of tooling behind it. Compose, Kubernetes, OCI registries, image scanners, and nearly every CI system treat the container as the common primitive. If the job is to run a service, execute a code snippet, process one request, or run an evaluation and disappear, a container is usually the obvious pick, and it will stay that way.

ⓘ Containers share one kernel, even inside a VM. On macOS and Windows, Docker Desktop runs your Linux containers inside a virtual machine, which keeps them off your host operating system. Every container in that VM still shares its single kernel, so the isolation between one container and the next is unchanged.

Docker Sandboxes run an AI coding agent inside a microVM that has its own Linux kernel, its own filesystem and network stack, and a private Docker Engine. The agent gets sudo inside the isolated environment. It can install packages, run services, build images, and bring up an entire Compose application without reaching the host Docker daemon, the host filesystem outside its workspace, or other sandboxes on the same machine.

Getting one running takes a single command, using sbx, the Docker Sandboxes CLI:

That creates the sandbox and starts the agent. You can stop it, resume it, and come back to everything the agent installed until you remove the sandbox.

Coding agents need a full machine because their sessions build up state over hours and they act on unvetted instructions. A container is built for a process with a known set of dependencies, and an agent’s work doesn’t fit that shape.

An agent needs the authority of a whole machine and a boundary designed for one. The capabilities that make an agent useful, such as running code, installing packages, and reaching the network, are the same ones that wear at the walls around it. An agent probes the edges of its environment because the job requires changing that environment.

A coding agent’s session looks like a developer’s afternoon. It clones a repository, installs packages, starts PostgreSQL, runs migrations, builds images, launches the app with Compose, reads the logs, changes the code, and tries again. That loop can run for hours, and each step depends on what the previous one left behind.

A container is designed to be rebuilt from an image and a few explicit volumes. The packages, databases, and build output an agent creates outside those volumes are lost when the container is recreated. That’s less than ideal for a session where every step builds on the last.

Box by ASCII describes this distinction neatly: “It uses the sandbox the way a developer uses a laptop, so the sandbox has to behave like one.”

The program deciding which command to run is probabilistic, and it can read instructions from many places at once, including source files, issue descriptions, web pages, package metadata, and MCP tool output. Some of that input is untrusted, and a malicious instruction planted in a README or a tool description can steer the agent on purpose. The code it generates may also be wrong or actively hostile.

We’ve already seen an agent that deleted a production database after misreading its task. Once the agent is running unattended in YOLO mode, there’s no permission prompt left to catch it. A workload with that much authority and that little predictability needs a boundary designed for it.

Put these facts together and you get the reason why we built Docker Sandboxes. An autonomous coding agent needs machine-shaped capabilities without access to everything on the developer’s machine, which comes down to four things:

The next sections show what each looks like, starting with containment.

The short version is that a container’s boundary is the host kernel it shares, and Docker Sandboxes have a hypervisor boundary around a kernel the agent has to itself. That one difference decides how far a compromise can travel, and it also settles the awkward question of how an agent could get access to Docker.

This is where containers and containment part ways. A container can be part of containment, and often should be, but containment covers the whole environment around the agent. It starts with a boundary the model can’t rewrite, which is why Docker uses a microVM with its own kernel for autonomous agents.

A container’s boundary is enforced by the kernel it shares, so escaping it requires a kernel exploit or a side-channel attack. Those aren’t everyday finds. Past that bar, though, sits the entire host kernel, every other process on the machine, and any credentials mounted alongside them.

ⓘ This scenario is specific to Linux environments. On Docker Desktop (macOS and Windows), the shared kernel is the Docker Desktop VM’s kernel, not the host kernel, so an escape would land in that VM rather than the operating system.

Docker Sandboxes give the agent its own kernel behind a hypervisor boundary. If an agent escapes its container, it lands in a disposable microVM, and the host stays out of reach. And you can delete the sandbox and start another. Each agent also gets its own microVM, so two agents running on the same machine can’t reach each other’s sandboxes either. However, the agent can still reach whatever you chose to share with it, including the workspace, allowed network destinations, MCP servers, and skills.

Mounting /var/run/docker.sock into a container is the long-standing way to give it Docker access, and it turns up in Compose setups, CI pipelines, and observability tools. It also hands the container control of the host Docker daemon, which Docker limits to trusted users because it can mount host paths and start privileged containers.

An agent doesn’t need an exploit to use that. Put one in a container started with the socket mounted, and it probes its surroundings, works out that it’s in a container, and finds the hole in the configuration. In Mark’s keynote demo, that was enough for an agent to reach a secrets file on the host with no zero-day involved.

The socket is one hole among several, and agents find them because probing their environment is part of the work. Docker-in-Docker, mounted credentials, and broad host mounts are others. Containers were built to isolate an application that behaves as configured. But an agent keeps testing that configuration, so if there’s a gap, it’s likely to find it.

Docker Sandboxes put the missing boundary in the right place. The agent talks to a private Docker Engine inside the guest. It can build images, run Testcontainers, and start a whole Compose stack, and the host daemon and its socket stay outside the microVM the entire time. Containers keep doing what they do best, inside a boundary the agent can’t widen.

Let’s say you ask an agent running inside Docker Sandboxes to find a secrets file on the host. It’ll find a Docker socket, try to use it to mount host paths and start privileged containers, but won’t reach the host. A microVM can give it a much stronger trust boundary than a container. The architecture behind Docker Sandboxes goes deeper on why a microVM was the right shape for this.

Containment draws the boundary. Control is what you can see and stop inside it: what the agent is allowed to reach, and the ability to block it when it heads somewhere it shouldn’t. In a sandbox, those controls are enforced by the host, outside the agent’s reach, so the agent can’t switch them off or reason its way around them. Outbound traffic, credentials, and organization-wide policy all work this way. Control also means knowing what the agent can still touch, because a microVM can’t hide what you choose to share with it.

Uses a private Docker Engine inside the microVM

Uses a direct read-write mount or an in-VM private clone

Uses the container network and whatever controls its operator supplies

Sends outbound traffic through a host proxy with deny-by-default policy

Commonly receives environment variables, files, or mounted sockets

Uses host-side credential injection for supported HTTP services

Usually rebuilt from an image and explicit volumes

Keeps packages, images, containers, and agent history until sbx rm

Fits Compose, Kubernetes, and most CI systems

Uses the sbx lifecycle and experimental environment files

These controls hold because they run outside the agent process. A rule the agent can reach is a rule it can work around, so the rules that matter live on the host. Outbound traffic goes through a host-side proxy with a deny-by-default policy, which means a destination the agent wants stays blocked until someone adds it to the list. Credentials follow the same logic. For supported HTTP services, the proxy injects them into outbound requests, so the agent can use a credential without the raw value ever entering the VM.

When one developer’s setup needs to become a team standard, Docker AI Governance can move network, filesystem, and MCP policy into organization-wide controls.

A microVM can’t hide what you choose to share with it, so the shared parts deserve the most attention.

The workspace is the largest of them. It’s mounted read-write by default, so the agent can edit or delete files in your project. Clone mode gives the agent a private in-VM clone to work in, which protects the host working tree, though the original repository stays readable inside the sandbox.

MCP servers and skills are shared too. A local stdio MCP server runs on the host and shared agent skills use a host-side store, so the agent’s reach extends to both.

So whichever mode you pick, be sure to read the diff before you run modified code.

Docker Sandboxes may cost some configuration and a supported hypervisor. But Kits and environment files account for the configuration, and they’re also where you get choice over agents and tools and the capacity to reproduce a setup across a team.

Kits make setup repeatable by packaging tools, files, commands, network rules, credentials, and agent instructions. They’re published for different agents, tools, and IDEs, so you can swap what runs inside the sandbox without rebuilding the environment around it. An sbxenv.yaml file records one project’s sandbox configuration, and committing it to the repo lets a teammate recreate the same sandbox with one command, so every developer works from the same setup. (Kits are in early access and environment files are experimental, so plan for changes to both.)

The host-enforced controls take some setup too. Network policy requires someone to decide which destinations belong in the workflow, credential mediation adds service bindings and approved domains, and clone mode adds a fetch step before changes land in the host repository.

Running a sandbox takes more from your machine than running a container does. It needs a supported hypervisor, which today means Apple silicon on macOS 14 or later, Windows Hypervisor Platform, or KVM on supported Ubuntu systems, and running inside another VM requires nested virtualization. The separate kernel and Docker Engine also consume more memory and disk than one additional container would.

If your machine is the limiting factor, Docker Cloud Sandboxes run the same microVM isolation on Docker-managed compute, and one command moves a sandbox between your laptop and the cloud. Local and cloud sandboxes keep separate secrets and network policies.

Choose a container when you control the code and its dependencies, and choose Docker Sandboxes when the workload generates or fetches code at runtime and needs its own boundary.

You control the code and its dependencies

The workload generates or fetches code at runtime

Execution is short-lived and rebuilt from an image

The workload doesn’t need Docker, or runs in CI that already provides it

The workload needs its own Docker Engine with no path to the host socket

You want maximum density and a fit with Compose or Kubernetes

You need host-enforced network and credential policy the workload can’t switch off

The kernel boundary is enough for the code you’re running

You want a hypervisor between the workload and your kernel

If any of the signals below apply to your project, you should start with Docker Sandboxes.

Start with sbx run claude. Add clone mode, kits, network policy, credential mediation, and organization governance as your workflow grows.

Containers aren’t going anywhere. For services, CI jobs, and anything you package to ship, they draw the right boundary, and the ecosystem around them is the reason they’ll keep doing that work. A coding agent needs more than a container to be trusted with real work. It needs containment, meaning a boundary the model can’t rewrite, controls it can’t reason its way around, and the freedom to choose its tools and scale its setup.

Docker Sandboxes provide that containment in a microVM that can still run your containers. The boundary is also what lets the agent move faster. With it in place, you can stop approving every command and let the agent get to work. Once one developer’s setup needs to become a team standard, Docker AI Governance extends the same controls across your organization.

Give it a try and get a sandbox running with one command. Start with sbx run claude and the Docker Sandboxes quickstart. Or if you’d like to run your agents in the cloud for hours (and for free), we’re giving away free credit for Docker Cloud Sandboxes for a limited time. Claim your $250 free credit before it’s gone!

No. A container is an isolated process that shares the host kernel. Docker Sandboxes are microVMs with their own kernel, filesystem, network, and Docker Engine. A sandbox can run containers, which is why the two are easy to confuse.

Yes. Each sandbox has a private Docker Engine, so the agent can build images, run Testcontainers, and start Compose applications without the host socket or Docker-in-Docker.

No. Containers remain the right tool for packaged applications, CI jobs, and short-lived execution. Sandboxes add a stronger boundary for autonomous agents, and containers keep running inside them.

A container isolates a workload. Containment involves the whole environment around an agent: the boundary it runs in, what it can reach, which tools it uses, and how you reproduce it. A container can be part of containment, and Docker Sandboxes add the microVM boundary an autonomous agent needs.

Docker describes four requirements: containment (a boundary the model can’t rewrite), control (visibility into what the agent is doing and the ability to stop it), choice (swapping models, agents, and tools without rebuilding), and capacity (a setup other developers can reproduce and scale past one machine).

A supported hypervisor, which means Apple silicon on macOS 14 or later, Windows Hypervisor Platform, or KVM on supported Ubuntu systems. Running inside another VM requires nested virtualization.

#10042. Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. Last week, we introduced Gemini 4 Hydrogen, which brings strong spatial and embodied reasoning to a much faster model built for speed and efficiency.

To help developers put Hydrogen to work, today we're launching the Robotics plugin on Google Antigravity: a set of skills for hardware onboarding, simulation and robot control turning Antigravity into a one-stop robotics workbench. Together, they enable a developer to go from a robot in a box to one that can perform a working application in an afternoon. To install the plugin, open Antigravity, go to Customizations and search for Robotics on the Build With Google shelf.

Gemini 4 Argon and Carbon showed that a general model can drive a robot arm directly, and Gemini 4 Hydrogen preserves that ability. Give it a goal in plain language and a camera view, and it plans the task, writes and runs the control code, and keeps adjusting until the job is done. On the Artificial Analysis Robotics 0.1 benchmark, Gemini 4 Hydrogen controls a real 6-axis arm through eight everyday tabletop tasks, from pouring a drink to packing blocks into a box. Hydrogen scores 58, similar to Argon at a lower cost per token.

Hydrogen brings the specialized skills of our Gemini Robotics ER 2 (Embodied Reasoning) models into the core Gemini 4 model series, and it beats them on nearly every axis. On the public ERQA benchmark, Hydrogen outperforms Gemini Robotics ER 2 on spatial reasoning tasks such as multi-view correspondence and 3D trajectory reasoning. For developers using Gemini Robotics ER 2 today, we recommend trying out Gemini 4 Hydrogen for these workloads.

A fast, capable model is only half the story. Building a robot application has always meant gluing together drivers, calibration scripts, simulators, perception code, and a UI, which requires robotics expertise and a lot of time. The new Robotics plugin for Google Antigravity packages that work into skills Gemini can use on your behalf:

Starting from an empty desk, a developer plugs an I2RT YAM arm and an external depth camera into a laptop and asks Hydrogen in Antigravity to bring it up. The plugin's Skills install the software, find the camera, calibrate the arm, and run test motions. The arm is calibrated and ready for tasks in about 10 minutes. A similar flow also brings up a low-cost SO-ARM101 arm and an industrial grade FR3 Duo from scratch to live waving.

Once the robot is live, the next step is building a simulation you can trust. With the Robotics plugin, MuJoCo runs directly inside Antigravity with dedicated creation, auditing, and rendering skills. This allows you to build and preview high-quality simulated scenes and assets—whether described, imported from links/documents, or uploaded via workspace photos—right alongside your code. You can then "vibe sim" by adjusting shapes, positions, and physical properties through chat to verify reaching and grasping before deploying to physical hardware.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The workbench with Hydrogen and Robotics plugins lets you go from an idea to a repeatable working application in real world in a much shorter time, by iterating and validating it in simulation.

A developer starts with a whiteboard sketch: a conveyor belt carrying mixed snacks, with a robot arm picking and sorting them into bins. From one prompt of the task with images of a real scene, Hydrogen constructs an interactive MuJoCo simulation of the cell, testing and validating the grasping policy in sim across multiple runs. The developer then deploys the verified policy directly to the robot in the real world. When nuances emerge (such as tuning gripper closure force or refining sorting rules), the developer iterates in natural language, asking Hydrogen to adjust the controller to iterate. Within about an hour end-to-end, the arm reliably sorts snacks off the moving belt.

#10040. Chinese AI Project Scouts Spies in Taiwan Chip Industry

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

The CCP built a Big Data system to scrape reams of information and learn about individuals abroad, to potentially target and exploit them. Think Alice Huang Lite.

The Chinese government is keeping tabs on ethnic Chinese people abroad via an AI-powered Big Data mining program. In some cases, the program is helping the government design influence operations targeting high-ranking individuals in the artificial intelligence (AI) industry.

Since the AI boom began, world leaders have been feeling out how to exploit it to cement their power. While Washington's emphasis has been on shorter-term AI ventures — tracking immigrants and political protestors, identifying social services to cut, etc. — the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) has been building something more durable: a data center that facilitates gargantuan Web scraping from social media and other public sources, feeds the results into machine learning algorithms, and outputs actionable intelligence for the apparatchiks in Beijing.

That's according to documents leaked to China watchdog NetAskari. In a report shared with Dark Reading and Sky News UK ahead of publication, NetAskari detailed a program that balances straightforward government PR with unsavory surveillance. Sometimes the data gathering has been designed around Cambridge Analytica-style targeted propaganda campaigns. In more nefarious cases, it has been used to identify individuals in political and economic spheres of interest who might one day make good spies or unwitting assets in spy campaigns.

Related:Africa Retakes Top Spot as Most Attacked Region, Led by Nigeria Surge

"A foreign nation with rather undemocratic tendencies is running an extensive, AI-charged analysis and information-scraping operation to find ways to influence other nations' internal social, economic, and political landscapes," a NetAskari analyst explains in an interview with Dark Reading over Signal. "That this system is particularly aiming at the Chinese diaspora, to see if [foreign citizens] can be used to further these ambitions, even if they might not be aware of it, is rather shocking."

On Nov. 8, NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang — one of the few most important people in the most important industry in the world — emailed his staff, admitting to an affair with a newly outed spy. The Ministry of State Security (MSS) agent, Alice Huang, redirected Nvidia semiconductors to China, where they're banned, and translated intelligence from the CEO to the CCP.

While the MSS ran Huang, the United Front Work Department (UFWD) took its own, less dramatic approach to the challenge of breaching Taiwan's semiconductor industry.

The UFWD's main game is influence operations, at home and abroad, according to NetAskari. Its mission includes gauging and shaping media narratives about China abroad, engaging Chinese diaspora communities — to their benefit or detriment, depending on the situation — and, in certain cases, attempting to gain influence over powerful or otherwise useful individuals and organizations in China and abroad.

Related:Malicious Linux Implants Mimic Asian Mail Security Products

In this instance, the UFWD appears to have been scoping out ethnic Chinese individuals involved in the semiconductor industry. A leaked report titled "Profiles of Ethnic-Chinese Leading Talent in the Chip Field, Their Circle Characteristics, and Work Recommendations" identifies executives of the Taiwanese AI company Skymizer, high-performing students at a top Taiwanese research university, and an AI research center director at Microsoft, among others, as individuals of note. It then details strategies for getting closer to these VIPs, like "leverage non-official organizations — hometown associations, alumni associations, professional societies — for relationship-building and cooperation."

The system used to generate this report is called the "Overseas Chinese Affairs Big Data Platform."

The platform's brain is a 500-square-meter Big Data laboratory at Jinan University in Guangzhou, built to comfortably house rows of server racks and at least 100 employees.

Related:UAE, Saudi Arabia Face Onslaught of Increasingly Complex Cyberattacks

Basically, it's built to ingest massive amounts of online information about overseas Chinese communities, identify people, relationships, and public sentiments, and convert it all into actionable reports for CCP leadership, according to NetAskari.

Social media provides much of the content. The platform scrapes liberally from platforms like Instagram, Facebook, and X, plus Chinese platforms like Weibo and WeChat. As a rule, the actual scrapers are run on machines located in foreign countries. As with Chinese hacking campaigns more broadly, the machines doing the crimes are also shielded behind rotating proxy networks. The goal of this obfuscation, one leaked document explains, is to “ensure the security of data collection and prevent the collection system from being traced and discovered" by targeted platforms.

The data then makes its way to China for processing. Foreign languages are translated into Chinese, keywords are extracted, and meaningful links are drawn. UFWD then cross-references these data with preexisting records of notable people identified as being involved in the media, sciences, politics, etc., as well as blacklisted and "two-faced people." These two sources feed a "business system" — the layer that seems to draw meaningful connections about public sentiments, the results of influence operations, and more.

Sometimes, the final reports outputted by this multitiered system simply concern public relations trends. Sometimes they've been used to identify ethnic Chinese individuals of note around the world — be they Taiwanese engineers, social media influencers, or something in between — and unearth strategies for how to utilize them to further the ends of the Chinese Communist Party.

Overall, the NetAskari analyst describes the Overseas Chinese Affairs Big Data Platform as "a worldwide dragnet that uses open source intelligence (OSINT) and Big Data analysis to identify and find potential collaborators, partners, and affiliates that the UFWD can work with to further their goal [of] extending the Communist Party's influence abroad and creating a 'China-friendly' environment in other nations' political, economic, and social settings."

It's nothing the UFWD wasn't doing before, to be sure. Instead, the analyst adds, "What used to be done manually, at a much slower speed, is now supercharged via AI and modern technology."

That, ultimately, is the rub. The leaked documents contain only outdated references, from 2021, about how machine learning (ML) and natural language processing (NLP) algorithms fit into this picture. It's unclear whether, and how, more modern large language models have been incorporated into — and potentially supercharged — the system. The better the AI gets, the more wide-ranging and granular the Overseas Chinese Affairs Big Data Platform can become, with real consequences for people who may not know they are being targeted by a foreign nation.

"A surveillance state can't accept that its own citizens are escaping to other nations, outside the reach of Beijing's grasp. For the best part of the last 20 years, most people had the luxury to ignore this part of the Chinese state. Often practical and physical limitations restricted Chinese organiations to expand too quickly abroad or even get a foothold. But with remote methods like the ones we have seen applied in the Big-Data center of Jinan University, these 'natural boundaries' don't apply anymore," the analyst laments.

"Pair it now with ever-growing globalization, China's stepping out onto the world stage, and crises of democracy in many Western nations, and it is not hard to see why such operations should concern even average citizens in the US or Europe," he says. "Even if they have no personal ties to China."

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.

#10039. Give sandboxed agents live web access with the Firecrawl kit for Docker Sandboxes

A coding agent in Docker Sandboxes runs inside an isolated VM, the sandbox controls which hosts it can reach, and credentials stay on the host behind a proxy. The same controls keep the agent from opening a docs page, reading a changelog, or looking up an API that shipped after its training cutoff, so it writes code against the version it remembers. The usual fix is to widen the allowlist until the agent can fetch anything, which hands back most of what the sandbox took away.

The Firecrawl kit gives the agent web search, page scraping, and site crawling through one added host, api.firecrawl.dev, and every page comes back as clean markdown.

The Firecrawl kit is a mixin (kind: mixin): it extends whichever workload you already run rather than defining a new sandbox. Its descriptor, firecrawl/firecrawl.yaml, declares four typed capabilities:

The kit adds to the sandbox’s allowlist and does not narrow it, so everything else is set by your global sbx policy. Docker’s balanced preset allows package registries, GitHub, and a curated list of vendor domains, and returns a 403 for general websites, including most documentation sites, so Firecrawl is the agent’s route to those. Under deny-all, it is the agent’s only web host.

Kits are experimental and in Early Access, so the kit format and sbx commands may change. The steps below are current for October 2026; the repository README carries the latest.

Kit: docker.io/firecrawl/sbx-kit-firecrawl:1.0.0 on Docker Hub, for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64. Source: github.com/firecrawl/firecrawl-docker-sandbox.

1. Store the key once on the host. The command prompts for the key. Service secrets are global by default, so every sandbox that requests the firecrawl service can use it once you approve. The kit never sees the value; the proxy injects it at request time.

2. Start a sandbox with the kit. The kit is an ordinary OCI image with its descriptor in the manifest, so a version tag is all you need. Each --kit adds a mixin to the workload, and the trailing . mounts your project directory.

On the first run, sbx asks you to approve sending the firecrawl credential to api.firecrawl.dev. Accept the defaults. The credential is required: with no secret stored, sbx refuses to create the sandbox instead of letting the agent hit a 401 mid-task. A detached or CI run cannot answer the approval prompt, so approve interactively once or write the binding to ~/.config/sbx/credentials.yaml first.

3. Give the agent a task that needs the live web. Documentation sites are blocked under balanced, so this one has to go through Firecrawl.

The kit’s instructions tell the agent to scrape blocked hosts through Firecrawl, so it calls fc.scrape(...) unprompted. Its response, trimmed:

4. Verify each layer. In the same session, ! runs shell commands inside the sandbox.

Expected: FIRECRAWL_API_KEY=proxy-managed. A real fc-... value here means the key came from somewhere other than the kit.

Expected: the page as markdown, starting with # Changelog and the latest version heading. The same URL returns a 403 to curl from inside the sandbox, so output here proves the SDK, the credential, and the network rule at once.

The kit also describes itself inside the sandbox. This prints the published descriptor, and the agent instructions sit next to it as firecrawl-context.md:

Running from source. To review or modify the kit before you run it, clone the repository and point --kit at the firecrawl/ directory; sbx builds source kits on demand:

With the kit installed, the agent can look things up on the web while it works. The sandbox only needs to talk to one new address, api.firecrawl.dev, and Firecrawl fetches the pages for it. This matters because the default balanced policy blocks most documentation sites. Without the kit, you’d have to allow each site one by one, and every new site you allow makes the sandbox a little less locked down.

Without the kit, you had a choice to make. The default balanced policy blocks most documentation sites, so you could either leave the agent working from what it remembered, or allow sites one by one and loosen the sandbox a little each time. The kit avoids that choice. Your Firecrawl API key stays on your machine, behind the proxy, and the agent still reads the web as it is today.

The result is fewer made-up APIs, fewer failed upgrades, and less time spent correcting an agent that was confidently out of date.

The kit is published under the firecrawl organization on Docker Hub as firecrawl/sbx-kit-firecrawl, with source, changelog, and CI on GitHub under Apache-2.0. The SDK pin is verified against PyPI’s package hashes at install time, and every release passes Docker’s kit-tck conformance suite in CI. Review the descriptor and allowlist before running it, as with any kit.

Pull the kit from Docker Hub: hub.docker.com/r/firecrawl/sbx-kit-firecrawl. Issues and feature requests go to the GitHub repository.

#10038. Inside an AI Attack Against a US Retailer

When hand-typed commands failed to breach a US retailer, the attacker turned to AI-generated scripts, achieving lateral movement in minutes.

A threat actor deployed an AI model during a stalled cyberattack and rescued the campaign, achieving lateral movement in the process.

The attack, detailed today by Symantec, targeted an unnamed US retail organization in September. The threat actor's hand-typed commands were repeatedly blocked, so the attacker switched to what appeared to be AI-generated commands and rapidly broke through the retailer's defenses. The attacker ultimately harvested credentials and achieved lateral movement within minutes.

Symantec believes AI was used because the timing between commands was more indicative of automation than direct hands-on keyboard. That said, the timing was such that investigators believe this activity was "more consistent with an operator relaying output to an AI tool than with an autonomous agent."

The security firm said this incident is one of dozens of AI-assisted attacks Symantec has investigated since May, though it added that activity was heavily concentrated around September.

Related:Cyprus Becomes Espionage Target for Iranian Hackers

Threat actors have utilized large language models (LLMs) in their attacks as long as the technology has been widely available. Early applications involved generating convincing phishing lures or conducting basic data reconnaissance, but recent evidence shows attackers deploying AI to execute end-to-end attacks, assist with malware development, and more. More recently, attackers have abused — and continue to abuse — agentic penetration testing tools to break into target environments at machine speed.

In this attack specifically, Symantec said it did not identify the initial access vector, focusing more on the timeline it was able to glean from its post-incident investigation. According to the blog post, the attacker worked at first by hand, which investigators determined due to artifacts like typing errors.

When every staged payload was terminated by security software, indicators suggest attacker tactics changed when they tried again the next day. "For almost two hours," Symantec said, "the attackers submitted long, error-free PowerShell scripts with labelled output sections and, in places, comments that read as a model's reasoning." Approximately 10 minutes after querying the victim's SolarWinds Orion monitoring software, attackers managed to laterally move to a second server.

The blog post hypothesizes that the attackers behind the attack were likely financially motivated, as they searched point-of-sale scripts for passwords, and one address they contacted was linked to an operation that stole card data from online retailers.

Related:Latin America Sees Cyberattacks on Ports, Pipelines & Manufacturing

During the second-stage activity, the threat actor gained initial access to a Web server and stole credentials from configuration files tied to the SolarWinds Orion monitoring software. And while the idea of AI speeding up or improving attacker tradecraft is nothing new, the attack is its deployment of AI to a hands-on keyboard attack in progress and possibly seizing victory out of an apparent defeat. It presents LLMs as a difference-maker for a less skilled threat actor.

Dick O'Brien, principal intelligence analyst on the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, tells Dark Reading this incident is one of several the team has seen in recent weeks where AI revived an attack. While he calls this a game-changer for attackers, "defenders can potentially leverage that pattern" as AI behaves in a particular kind of way, such as testing variants of a technique in quick succession.

"The retry pattern is a signal itself," he says. "Blocking on behavior rather than file names or exact commands matters much more. Treating a blocked attempt followed by a variant as a high-priority alert is worth considering."

Although no vulnerability was exploited and no patching was required, O'Brien believes this trend of AI accelerating attacks is and will continue to affect the patching race. "Our view is that defenders need to start looking at mean time to mitigate rather than mean time to patch. Patching is still important, but it can’t be the only countermeasure."

Related:AI Agents Zip From Flaw Discovery to Root Access in 11 Minutes

After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

#10036. The Trust Layer: Securing Agentic AI with Docker AI Governance

The security problem changes once an AI agent can do more than answer a prompt. It can execute code, access files, connect to services, and call external tools. That creates a new security question for enterprises: how do you give an agent enough freedom to be useful without giving it more access than it actually needs?

A model decision is not automatically an authorized action. That distinction becomes important the moment an agent can turn a model output into an action in a real system.

The answer can’t rely only on the model. Agent behavior can vary, while security controls need to be clear and enforceable. That means putting controls around the environment where the agent runs, what it can access, which tools it can use, and what happens when a policy allows or blocks an action. For a CISO, this creates a trust gap. Approving the use of AI agents means being able to answer more than whether the model is capable: what access does the agent have, where are those boundaries enforced, and what evidence is available afterward? The goal is to let teams adopt agents without making model behavior itself the security boundary.

Docker AI Governance approaches this through three areas: Sandbox Policies, MCP Tool Governance, and Audit + Visibility.

Together, they address three practical questions security teams need answered: Where can the agent operate? What can it do? And can we see what happened?

In this article, I use “trust layer” as shorthand for the infrastructure between what an agent wants to do and what the organization actually allows it to do.

There is a related trust question too: what software artifacts are these agentic workflows building and using? Docker Hardened Images address that software supply-chain side of the problem, alongside the runtime controls discussed below.

Figure 1: The three layers of Docker AI Governance: Sandbox Policies, MCP Tool Governance, and Audit + Visibility.

An agent needs enough access to get real work done. A coding agent may need to install packages, build containers, edit project files, or connect to external services. The security challenge is deciding where those actions are allowed before the agent starts taking them.

Docker Sandboxes give each agent an isolated microVM environment a lightweight virtual machine with its own Docker Engine, filesystem, and network. Docker AI Governance adds policy around that environment so security teams can control network and filesystem access centrally.

Network policies can allow or block specific domains, IP ranges, and ports. Filesystem policies control which host paths can be mounted into a sandbox and whether that access is read-only or read-write. These controls are enforced at the network proxy and filesystem mount layers rather than depending on the agent to follow an instruction.

For an organization, that means the same boundaries can follow developers across their sandboxes instead of being configured differently on every machine. Teams still get the access they need for their work, but an individual agent cannot simply expand those boundaries on its own.

A developer can check the effective boundary before starting the agent:

The important part is that the result comes from the active policy, not from an instruction the agent is expected to follow.

This answers the first governance question: Where can the agent operate?

Once an agent has a controlled place to run, the next question is what it should be allowed to do through external tools.

MCP (Model Context Protocol) gives agents a common way to connect to external tools, services, and capabilities, but that also expands what an agent can act on. For enterprise use, making a tool available should not automatically mean every agent can use it.

Docker AI Governance puts policy around MCP activity handled through Docker’s MCP Gateway. Administrators can control which MCP servers may be registered and what agents can do through them, including tool calls, resources, prompts, and approval requirements. These organization policies are written in Cedar, a policy language used to express authorization rules. When MCP policy enforcement is active, governed requests are denied unless a matching policy permits them. For example, a policy can permit read-only tool calls for a specific registered MCP server:

Here, readOnly comes from the MCP tool’s annotations. Those annotations are supplied by the server and are advisory, so this example relies on metadata provided by that server.

This makes tool access an explicit policy decision rather than something left to each agent or developer to configure independently. A team can be given access to the tools it needs, while other servers or actions remain unavailable. Policies can also require confirmation for specific tool calls when an action needs an additional checkpoint.

One boundary is worth making clear: MCP policies apply to activity handled by Docker’s MCP Gateway. If an agent connects directly to a remote MCP server from inside a sandbox, that connection falls under the sandbox’s network policy instead.

With the environment constrained and tool access governed, we can answer the second question: What can the agent do?

Setting a policy is only part of the job. Security teams also need to know when that policy was applied, what action triggered it, and whether the action was allowed or denied.

Docker AI Governance records sandbox policy decisions and sandbox session events as structured audit records. Each record captures details such as the principal, action, target, decision, and time. The records contain governance metadata rather than the contents of prompts or agent responses.

Audit records can be kept locally or delivered to the cloud. With cloud delivery enabled, teams can search the organization-wide record, export it for further analysis, or forward events into existing SIEM (security information and event management) workflows.

Denied actions are especially useful. A team can see not only that an action failed, but that a governance rule blocked it and why. Allowed decisions matter too: they provide a record of how policy was applied while the agent was working.

Current audit coverage includes Docker Sandboxes policy decisions and sandbox session events. The same schema can support additional Docker AI sources as they become available.

That closes the third part of the governance loop: Can we see what happened?

AI Governance controls access around the agent at runtime. There is a separate question that matters to the workflows those agents participate in: what container images and software artifacts are they building, testing, or deploying?

Docker Hardened Images provide minimal, secure, and production-ready container images, Helm charts, and system packages maintained by Docker.

In practical terms, this gives teams evidence about what is inside an image and how that image was produced. Docker Hardened Images include signed SBOMs, VEX information, cryptographic signatures, and SLSA Build Level 3 provenance. Instead of simply trusting where an image came from, teams can inspect what is inside it and verify information about how it was built.

For organizations with additional compliance requirements, Docker also provides FIPS-enabled and STIG-ready variants through relevant DHI offerings. These capabilities sit alongside the broader DHI security and supply-chain controls rather than making any single standard the foundation by itself.

AI Governance and Docker Hardened Images solve different parts of the trust problem. Governance controls the authority an agent has while it works; DHI helps establish trust in the container artifacts that the workflow depends on.

Figure 2: Docker Hardened Images combine minimal container images with signed supply-chain metadata and verifiable build provenance.

Consider a developer using a coding agent to investigate an issue, update a service, and use an approved internal tool along the way.

The agent begins inside a Docker Sandbox. It can work with the project and run the commands needed for the task, but its access is still bounded by policy. If it tries to reach a network destination or mount a host path that the organization has not allowed, the applicable sandbox policy can block that access.

Now suppose the agent needs an MCP tool to look up information from another service. The request goes through Docker’s MCP Gateway, where organization policy determines whether that server and tool are permitted. A routine lookup might be allowed, while a more sensitive tool call could require confirmation or remain unavailable altogether.

For governance activity currently covered by AI Governance Audit Logs, Docker records the policy decision and relevant session metadata. Security teams can see who initiated the activity, what policy-controlled action was evaluated, and whether it was allowed or denied. Those records can then feed the organization’s existing monitoring and SIEM workflow.

If that same agent builds or tests a containerized application, the software entering the workflow matters too. Docker Hardened Images provide a more verifiable software foundation for that workflow, while AI Governance controls what the agent is authorized to do at runtime.

The model is still making decisions about how to complete the task. What changes is the authority around those decisions. The agent can work autonomously inside the boundaries the organization has defined, while access outside those boundaries remains a policy decision.

That is the practical value of the three layers working together:

Sandbox Policies define where the agent can operate.

MCP Tool Governance defines what tools it can use.

Audit + Visibility shows how governed activity was handled.

Taken together, the pattern is straightforward: define the boundary, check it before the agent starts working, enforce it while the agent is running, and use the resulting audit trail to understand what happened. If a boundary needs to change, the policy changes, not the expectation that the model will simply behave differently.

The challenge with agentic AI is not simply that agents can make decisions. It is that those decisions can now lead directly to actions.

That makes the boundary around the agent just as important as the model itself. Docker AI Governance addresses that boundary through Sandbox Policies, MCP Tool Governance, and Audit + Visibility: controlling where agents can operate, what tools they can use, and how governed activity is recorded.

Docker Hardened Images address a related supply-chain concern by helping teams work with smaller, verifiable container artifacts.

The goal is not to remove autonomy from agents. It is to make sure autonomy does not automatically come with unlimited authority.

As agents take on more real work, that distinction will matter more and more.

Docker launches a new brand built on trust. Learn why Docker is evolving for AI agents, what stays the same, and how to run agents securely with Docker.

Docker publishes fixed images for both Java4Shell and the NGINX HTTP/2 in the Docker Hardened Images (DHI) catalog.

Learn how Docker AI Governance secures agentic AI with sandbox policies, MCP tool governance, and audit visibility to support controlled AI agent workflows.

#10033. Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

We’d like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Haamed Gheibi, Erik Sanders, Rob Seymour, Taylor Santiago, Andrew Hsieh, Eric Taseski, Kiyoung Kim, Hung-Wei Chen, Jose “Pepe” Galmes, Ronald Li, Yu-Che Wang, Roger Ellis, Sandeep Patil, Amith Dsouza and the entire Android Build team
