#10032. Meet the New Docker: Built to Run on Trust

Today, Docker is officially launching its new brand, and I’m excited to tell you what it’s all about and why we think now is the time for change.

Let’s start with the big picture. We’re living through a transformative moment: for Docker, for developers, for the industry, and quite possibly, for humanity itself. AI is creating possibilities that would have sounded like science fiction not long ago.

But it’s also forcing the industry to confront an almost existential question: how do we harness all that power and intelligence without compromising security, governance, or control? Or, put more simply: how do we get to a point where we can actually trust agents?

That one seemingly simple yet infinitely complex question is why we’re here today, introducing you to a new Docker.

Ok, not exactly here, but once upon a time, a decade or so ago, the tech industry was at a similar inflection point. Digital transformation was the term du jour, promising a new way of engaging customers and powering the global economy. There was one big constraint, though: teams needed a better way to build, ship, and run software consistently across any environment.

Enter a company you might have heard of: Docker.

Together with all of you, Docker built the standard trusted by millions of developers. For over a decade, developers have relied on us to make software portable, open, reproducible, and secure. Docker turned the frustration of “Well, it works on my machine!” into the assurance that “Yes, it works everywhere.”

Today, of course, the challenge confronting us is new and evolving, and we all need to evolve with it. We cannot simply continue to do what we’ve always done and expect to succeed in this new era. That applies to business leaders, developers, tech companies, everyone. It certainly applies to Docker. That’s what this rebrand is about: evolving to meet the moment for our customers.

Let me be unequivocal: this is not about leaving Docker’s legacy or heritage behind. We’ve spent more than a decade earning your trust. This is about building on that foundation, not replacing it.

The qualities you value us for aren’t changing. We’re still committed to openness and industry collaboration. We’re still, and always will be, developer-first. We’re still fanatical about simplicity, and about showing up everywhere you need us.

On the product front, the containers you know and love aren’t going anywhere. Containers still play a huge role in the software development lifecycle. As long as that’s the case, Docker will continue leading the way.

Agents have introduced a new problem. Containers isolate workloads. But agents aren’t workloads. Agents are actors.

Agents make their own decisions about what to run, what to access, and what to change. Developers and enterprises need solutions built specifically to isolate and govern them, so they can give those agents real autonomy without giving up control.

Docker’s next chapter is about delivering exactly that: security, isolation, and governance, so we can all trust agents to do meaningful work without feeling like we’re always one step from catastrophe.

In case I haven’t made it blindingly obvious yet, the new Docker is all about trust.

Trust Docker because our track record speaks for itself. Trust Docker because we’re proven, neutral, and secure. Trust Docker because it’s pretty darn clear you can’t trust the agents to manage themselves.

Trust us because we have a software supply chain that is already the standard for building securely. Trust us because nobody isolates agents where they run better than we do. Trust us because we’re taking a comprehensive approach to improving governance and minimizing risk across the entire enterprise.

Trust Docker because we’re not egotistical enough to think we can solve a challenge this big alone. Trust us because everything about our history, our heritage, and our actions over the past year tells you we’ll partner with the smartest and build alongside the best, and not create vendor lock-in.

Most of all, trust Docker because we’re not just talking the talk. We’ve got the goods to back it up: trusted supply chain for your agents to build from, trusted runtime to isolate your agents, and a trusted ecosystem to ensure all the layers of trust are covered, whether made by Docker or not.

As our product team innovated to meet the moment and our messaging in the market began to shift, one thing became clear: the speed and scale of evolution happening inside Docker called for an equally momentous evolution on the outside. Something that makes it unmistakably clear that it’s a new day for Docker. Something that says and shows, loud and clear, for all the world to see, that Docker is not just about containers anymore.

I’m talking, of course, about our beloved Moby.

If you know Docker, then you know Moby, and if Docker is evolving, Moby should too. The only question was to how and to what extent? We looked at a lot of whales, folks. (I may or may not have been seeing whales in my sleep.) Ultimately, with the help of our friends at the strategic brand transformation company, Motto, we landed on a new Moby for the new Docker.

In many ways, Moby is a microcosm for Docker and the changes we’re making: the Moby you love and trust, evolved for a new era. Just as the new Docker is no longer singularly defined by containers, neither is the new Moby.

We’ve got lots to show you. Poke around our new website. Find and engage with the new Docker brand on social media. And if you want to see what trust in the agentic era looks like in practice, try Docker Sandboxes and Docker AI Governance. We’d love to hear what you think.

Wherever this new era takes us, Docker will always honor and cherish the trust you continue to place in us. Now, let’s go build what’s next.

Terri Avnaim is the CMO of Docker, where she leads global marketing and shapes the company’s positioning and go-to-market strategy for the agentic era.

#10029. Docker patches Java4Shell and the NGINX HTTP/2 with Docker Hardened Images

OpenJDK and Oracle disclosed Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938) on November 17, and NGINX released fixes for two HTTP/2 flaws the next day. Docker began publishing fixed images for both in the Docker Hardened Images (DHI) catalog starting that same day.

What we know about Java4Shell is that it’s a memory-corruption bug in the Java runtime that can let an attacker with no credentials run code inside a Java application. Many container images contain a Java runtime, including application images that bundle their own, so it affects teams well beyond the ones that write Java.

Docker Hardened Images are the trusted foundation your applications should run on, because a flaw in the runtime inside an image is fixed only when that image is rebuilt, and Docker rebuilds its hardened images as soon as upstream fixes become available. Fixed images for the Amazon Corretto, SapMachine and Azul Zulu runtimes and for NGINX are in the catalog today, with Eclipse Temurin following as soon as Adoptium completes its fixed builds.

To decode Base64 quickly, the HotSpot JVM uses a hand-written routine built on AVX-512 instructions. That routine miscalculates a length when the input ends in unexpected padding and writes up to 62 bytes past the end of a buffer on the Java heap. Calif, the researchers who found it, turned that into heap disclosure and remote code execution.

They demonstrated code execution before authentication against default installations of Keycloak, Jira, Confluence, Artifactory, Jenkins and PingFederate, and after authentication against Kafka, Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit and Spinnaker. They note there is no universal exploit: each application needs its own, and they have not published those. As of November 18 there are no public reports of exploitation in the wild.

Oracle rates the issue 8.1 (High) under CVSS 3.1. The researchers rate it 9.2 (Critical) under CVSS 4.0. CISA has issued an alert urging immediate patching.

To check a host’s CPU, run this on the host or Kubernetes node. Hardened runtime images have no shell, so it will not run inside the container.

grep -m1 ‘^flags’ /proc/cpuinfo | tr ‘ ‘ ‘\n’ | grep -E ‘^(avx512f|avx512bw|avx512vbmi|bmi2)$’

The host is affected if all four flags are printed.

Two other ways to check, for when a shell on the node is hard to get.

Ask the JVM. This works in hardened images:

docker run –rm –entrypoint java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep -w UseAVX

UseAVX = 3 means the JVM is using AVX-512 on that host. Treat the host as affected until the full check says otherwise. A value of 0 to 2 means AVX-512 is not in use. On ARM64 nothing is printed. The answer applies only to the host the command ran on.

Ask Kubernetes. kubectl get nodes -L kubernetes.io/arch,node.kubernetes.io/instance-type shows the architecture and instance type of every node. If you run Node Feature Discovery, nodes with the instruction set carry the label feature.node.kubernetes.io/cpu-cpuid.AVX512VBMI=true.

Check build runners and CI workers as well. They run Java, and they talk to the network.

Fixed. The published images report 17.0.21.1.2, 21.0.13.1.2, 25.0.5.1.2 and 27.0.1.1.4.

Fixed. The published images report 21.0.13.1+2, 25.0.5.1+2 and 27.0.1.1+4.

Fixed. The published Zulu images we checked report 17.0.21.1+2, 21.0.13.1+2, 25.0.5.1+2 and 27.0.1.1+4.

Waiting for Adoptium to complete its release. On November 18 Adoptium had published 27.0.1.1+4 for x86-64 Linux only. 17.0.21.1, 21.0.13.1 and 25.0.5.1 were still in testing. Adoptium tracks progress in adoptium/temurin#326. Our published images still report 17.0.21+9, 21.0.13+9, 25.0.5+8 and 27.0.1.0.2+1, which are vulnerable.

Java 26 reached end of support in September 2026, so no fix is expected from any vendor, and it is not among the fixed lines in the OpenJDK advisory. If you run it, treat it as affected and keep the flag set. We recommend moving to Java 25 or updating to Java 27. If you cannot migrate yet, ask us whether Extended Lifecycle Support can cover your version. It is the Docker Hardened Images Enterprise add-on for software past its upstream end of life.

Not affected by this CVE and no action is needed for it. The same November update fixes other issues in these lines, so update when new images arrive.

Application images that bundle Java, such as Keycloak, Jenkins, Kafka, Elasticsearch and Solr

They follow the bundled runtime, which for many of them is Temurin, and they are rebuilt once the runtime they use is fixed. Until then, set the flag yourself.

Set the mitigation flag yourself on every JVM that is still waiting for a fixed runtime, and check what each image already sets before you do. Treat the flag as a stopgap, because a JVM is protected only if the flag reaches it when it starts, and a deployment setting or a start script can override it or drop it. A fixed runtime is the only thing that closes the issue, so move to the fixed image as soon as it is published.

The CPU check and the mitigation flag in the steps below apply to any image that runs a HotSpot-based OpenJDK build. The version strings in this post come from Docker Hardened Images, so for an image from another source, confirm the fixed build in that source’s advisory.

The fixed build can only come from whoever builds your image, because the Java runtime and the system packages your application runs on come from the image underneath it. That dependency is why a hardened image is a necessary step toward a trusted software supply chain. With Docker Hardened Images, tracking each vendor’s fix and rebuilding on it is work Docker does for you. The images are minimal and secure by default, and they are built to serve as a trusted and verifiable upstream for your builds. You can move a service onto one by following the migration guide.

A container often runs more than one JVM, for example when a build tool forks a JVM for tests, an application server starts helper processes or a CI agent starts a JVM for every build step. The flag protects only the JVMs that receive it, and the variable most people reach for first is JAVA_OPTS, which Java does not read at all.

The JVM itself, however it is started. Includes javac, keytool and other JDK tools.

A Java 8 JVM that sees it refuses to start. Some DHI FIPS images already set this variable.

Not read by javac, keytool or other JDK tools. DHI FIPS images set it. Java 8 ignores it.

JAVA_OPTS, JENKINS_JAVA_OPTS, MAVEN_OPTS, ES_JAVA_OPTS and similar

A start script, which copies the value onto one java command line

Each script has its own rules. If nothing in the image reads the variable, it does nothing.

Five rules follow from that table. We tested each on DHI images.

JAVA_OPTS only works where a script reads it. With JAVA_OPTS=”-XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics” set and a plain java command, the intrinsic stays on. No warning is printed.

A flag on the command line stops at that process. We started a JVM with the flag on its command line and had it start a second JVM. The child ran with the intrinsic on. With JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS or JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS in the environment, the child ran with it off. A child started with a cleared environment gets nothing, whichever variable you use.

Check what the image already sets before you set anything. In Docker and Kubernetes, your value replaces the image’s value. It is not appended.

docker inspect –format ‘{{range .Config.Env}}{{println .}}{{end}}’ \  | grep -E ‘^(JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS|JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS)=’

Our FIPS images use these variables to configure the FIPS provider. dhi.io/eclipse-temurin:21-fips sets both. When we replaced its JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS with only the flag, the JVM started normally, but the org.bouncycastle.fips.approved_only property was gone. Nothing warned us. If the command prints a value, copy it and add the flag at the end:

env:  – name: JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS    value: “ -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics”

Not every FIPS image uses the same variables, so run the check for each image.

Keep JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS away from Java 8. Java 8 is not affected and does not know the flag:

Picked up JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS: -XX:-UseBASE64IntrinsicsUnrecognized VM option ‘UseBASE64Intrinsics’Error: Could not create the Java Virtual Machine.

Adding -XX:+IgnoreUnrecognizedVMOptions to the variable does not help on Java 8. Where Java 8 shares an environment with a newer Java, either scope the variable to the containers that need it or use JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS, which Java 8 ignores. Java 11 accepts the flag, so it is harmless there.

Verify the process, not the environment. Running java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version inside a container starts a new JVM. It tells you what the environment gives a new JVM, not what the JVM already serving traffic was started with. For the running process, use the startup log line. JDK_JAVA_OPTIONS prints one too, starting with NOTE: Picked up. In an image that includes the JDK you can also ask the process directly:

jcmd VM.flags | tr ‘ ‘ ‘\n’ | grep UseBASE64Intrinsics

The answer must be -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics. If the flag reached the JVM through an environment variable, it will not show in the process’s command line, so ps is not a reliable check.

Some applications discard these variables on purpose. Elasticsearch’s start script, for example, ignores JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS and expects ES_JAVA_OPTS. Check your application’s documentation.

Pull the new image and redeploy. Four things catch people out:

Then confirm with java -version that what is running reports a fixed build. After that, remove the flag from every place you set it.

The day after Java4Shell, NGINX released fixes for two memory errors in the way it handles HTTP/2 connections, tracked as CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633. A crafted request can crash a worker process, and where an attacker gets past the operating system’s memory protections it can run code inside that worker. Both flaws need HTTP/2 to be enabled on the server, and the same releases fix other NGINX issues that do not depend on HTTP/2, so update either way.

The Docker Hardened Images for NGINX were rebuilt on NGINX 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 and published on November 18, the same day as the upstream release. To patch, pull the image again and redeploy, and move any tag pinned to an older exact version to a fixed one. nginx -v prints the version, and the fixed ones are 1.30.7 and 1.31.9. The notes on pull policy, mirrors and your own images in the section above apply to NGINX images in the same way.

The vulnerable code is in the Java runtime inside the image, and if you are using DHI, shipping the fix becomes our job. Whether that code can be reached in your deployment depends on things an image cannot see: the CPU it is scheduled onto, the options you start it with, the processes it starts, and the traffic you send it.

Track every Java vendor and ship fixed runtimes in DHI as soon as they are released

Track every Java vendor yourself and rebuild your base images on each fixed runtime

Tell you which images are fixed and which are still waiting

Find out from each vendor which of its builds are fixed

Tell you what we know, and update this post

Follow the OpenJDK, Oracle and vendor advisories as they change

Three jobs stay with you whichever image you run.

It was only four weeks ago that the news was PHP2Shell and the eMudhra certificate compromise. Calif, the security research team that found Java4Shell and helped find PHP2Shell, says it used AI to find this flaw in about an hour and that the whole effort cost less than $200 in tokens. When a flaw like this can be found and weaponized at that price, it is reasonable to expect more of them.

If you lead a team or answer for how your organization builds and ships software, the questions after a week like this one are whether you were exposed and how quickly you can prove you were not. It is imperative to have a trusted foundation in place before the next flaw appears, and Docker Hardened Images is that foundation to adopt. This week Docker began publishing fixed Java and NGINX images to the catalog within a day of each disclosure, with the images still waiting listed in the status table above. Each image ships with a signed SBOM and SLSA Build Level 3 provenance, so you have a record of what it contains and how it was built.

The Docker Hardened Images catalog is free and open source under Apache 2.0, and moving one of your services onto a hardened image typically means changing one line in its Dockerfile. If you are not building on Docker Hardened Images yet, start today.

Principal Product Marketing Manager, Docker

Vishrut Iyengar is Principal PMM for Security at Docker, focused on Docker Hardened Images, supply chain security, and securing what AI agents build.

#10026. Prove your AI agent guardrails work

You ask a coding agent to clean up one repository. If it has broad permissions, it may also reach your home directory, use your development credentials, and send requests to services you never approved. That gap between the task and the access you grant can turn a routine coding session into a data leak or lost work.

In Sandboxing Claude and MCP, Hrittik walked through running Claude Code and an MCP server inside Docker Sandboxes using its command-line interface, sbx. That walkthrough started with outbound access blocked, allowed the endpoints needed for the task, and inspected requests to other destinations, including telemetry. The useful part was seeing an actual request fail at the network boundary.

Now extend that workflow to a team. Models and providers will change, but the access rules still need to hold. A sandbox limits what an agent can reach on one machine. Organization governance adds shared rules, a managed way to run agents, and records of policy decisions. In this post, we’ll look at how one laptop can affect the whole organization, review two reported incidents, and connect them to four Docker controls: network policy, filesystem policy, managed credentials, and MCP access. Then we’ll test the network and filesystem controls with a simple bank administrator demo.

If an agent exposes a developer’s SSH key or cloud token, every system that accepts that credential may need investigation. The security team must work out what left the machine, replace or revoke exposed credentials, and check whether anyone used them. Deleted work can also halt development. Keep backups of work that has not yet reached the repository.

The compliance consequences depend on the data and the organization. A personal data breach can trigger GDPR notification duties, subject to the applicable risk thresholds. HIPAA has separate requirements for covered entities and business associates following breaches of unsecured protected health information. India’s DPDP framework also includes breach duties, but those provisions are scheduled to take effect in May 2027. For a SOC 2 examination, the question is whether the relevant controls operate as described. SOC 2 is not a breach-notification law.

Data sovereignty adds another question: where does the data go, and who controls it? Before approving a model API endpoint, establish where prompts and outputs are processed and stored, who can access them, and which contracts and laws apply. A provider’s nationality alone does not answer those questions. Network policy can restrict the destination, but it cannot prove how the provider handles data after receiving it.

This gets harder when developers choose and configure agents independently. A rule on one laptop may be missing on the next. An ignore file may stop one component from reading a file while another component includes it in an upload. Written policy needs matching controls in the software that runs the agent.

In July 2026, developers reported two incidents where a coding agent went far beyond its task. In one, data left the machine. In the other, files were deleted from it.

In an X post that has since been deleted, a user said Grok had uploaded their entire user directory, including SSH keys, a password manager database, and personal files. A Hacker News discussion brought wider attention to the claim. The home-directory claim remains the user’s account. The separate investigation below tested repository uploads.

Researcher Cereblab intercepted Grok Build CLI traffic and found a background upload running alongside the coding task. In a test with a 12 GB repository, about 5.1 GiB had left the machine before the capture stopped, and the upload was still running. The data went to Google Cloud Storage. Fake secrets planted in the repository also appeared in the traffic without being hidden. At the time, turning off “Improve the model” did not stop the repository uploads.

In a reproducible follow-up, the researcher rebuilt the repository from a captured Git bundle, an archive of tracked files and Git history. The bundle included files the agent had been told not to read. Telling the model not to read a file didn’t stop the CLI from packaging and uploading it in the background.

There was a subsequent change. The researcher’s updated timeline says whole-repository uploading was disabled server-side on July 13 and remained off when retested on July 22.

The fix does not change the lesson. A coding agent is more than the model. It includes the CLI and any background processes, and those can act without the model asking. Give the whole application only the access its task needs. This is the principle of least privilege.

On July 10, Matt Shumer wrote: “GPT-5.6-Sol just accidentally deleted almost ALL of my Mac’s files.” He shared the agent’s acknowledgment and confirmed that Full Access was enabled. His follow-up described the loss of his home directory.

A separate experiment shows why blocking one command is a fragile response. Developer LinearUncle reported that, after a plugin blocked rm, the agent tried unlink, find -delete, and apply_patch. A later attempt to move files to Trash through the GUI failed on permissions; a call to Node’s fs.unlink succeeded. The plugin’s own documentation describes it as an additional guardrail and recommends sandboxing and backups.

The common issue is excessive access. A model can choose a different tool, and a background process can run outside the model’s visible workflow. Filesystem and network boundaries need to constrain both.

Docker Sandboxes runs each agent in a microVM, a lightweight virtual machine with its own Linux kernel and Docker Engine. A workspace mount shares a host directory with the sandbox. A proxy on the host checks outgoing connections against network policy. The isolation documentation explains these boundaries.

That gives a developer a useful starting point. Share the project needed for the task, restrict outbound access, and inspect failures before adding permissions. Unnecessary telemetry endpoints can remain blocked while the required model and package services are allowed.

Choose mounts carefully. A writable mount shares real host files, so changes and deletions inside it affect your machine. Check additional mounts too, including shared agent skills where enabled. Docker’s clone mode gives the agent a private copy of a Git repository so you can review its commits before bringing them back. Isolation limits access, but files in a writable host mount still need backups. Clone mode still exposes the host repository for reading, including ignored files. Keep secrets outside it.

Local settings leave a rollout problem. A teammate can choose different rules or run an agent directly on the host. The organization needs a managed way to run agents, a way to require its use, and visibility into which sessions it covers.

The diagram below shows how organization and team policies reach managed sandbox sessions, where access checks occur, and how policy decisions feed audit records.

Figure 1. Organization and team policies govern access for enrolled, licensed sandbox sessions.

Docker AI Governance lets administrators manage sandbox rules and MCP access centrally. MCP, short for Model Context Protocol, connects agents to tools and data sources. Administrators define policies in Docker Home. On developer machines, sandbox mount checks, the host proxy, and Docker’s MCP gateway enforce the relevant rules. This gives teams shared access rules and records they can review when something goes wrong.

Network policies allow or deny destinations such as domain names, IP addresses, and IP ranges written in CIDR notation. For a restricted workflow, start with access denied and allow only the destinations the task needs. Under organization governance, local allow rules cannot grant extra access. Deny rules win, and developers can add local network restrictions.

This can block a background upload to an unapproved destination. But an approved hostname may still handle several kinds of traffic. In the Grok investigation, model requests and storage uploads used different URL paths on the same hostname, so a hostname-only allow rule would not distinguish them. Docker AI Governance now supports more granular network policies: on an allowed host, rules can match the HTTP method, URL path, and query parameters. For example, a policy can permit an agent to open a GitHub pull request while blocking requests to merge it. These finer-grained rules complement filesystem restrictions, which limit the data an application can collect and send.

Filesystem policies control which host paths may be mounted. A writable mount needs both read and write permission; a read-only mount needs read. If a filesystem deny rule and an allow rule match the same request, the deny wins. The decision happens when the sandbox is created. Once a writable mount exists, the agent can modify or delete files within it, regardless of whether it uses rm, a patch tool, or a programming language API.

For proxy-managed credentials, the host proxy adds authentication to outbound requests. The agent can authenticate to an approved service without receiving the raw secret inside the microVM. Credentials can also be scoped to a named sandbox.

This reduces the need to paste API tokens into prompts or store them in the workspace. The protection applies to proxy-managed credentials. A real token placed in an environment variable, a mounted file, or a passthrough login response may still be visible inside the sandbox. Limit what each credential can access and how long it stays valid. Storing a token through the proxy does not change its permissions or expiry.

MCP access policies govern server registration and subsequent requests through Docker’s MCP gateway. With MCP enforcement active, access requires an explicit permit, and a forbid rule overrides it. Administrators can control tool calls, resource reads, and prompt retrieval, rather than treating an approved server as permission to do everything it offers.

Registration and use are separate decisions. Blocking new registrations does not stop access to a server that is already registered. Rules checked on each request handle that case. Direct connections to remote MCP servers also need network controls because they bypass Docker’s gateway. Check where each server runs too: local stdio servers run on the host, outside the sandbox VM. Their host access needs separate review.

The next diagram summarizes the four controls to review together: network access, host mounts, managed credentials, and MCP tools.

Figure 2. Network, filesystem, credentials, and MCP controls apply to the whole agent application.

Administrators manage organization and team policies in Docker Home. Group mapping can keep Docker teams in sync with the organization’s identity provider. Policy updates take up to five minutes to reach developer machines. Network changes then apply to new requests. Filesystem changes require removing and recreating affected sandboxes because existing mounts keep their original access.

Sign-in enforcement is also part of deployment. IT distributes it through endpoint management so developers must authenticate as members of an approved organization. This prevents personal-account bypass within sbx. Your endpoint controls also need to cover agents launched outside that managed workflow.

Suppose you are a bank administrator. Developers want to use coding agents, and you need to enforce the bank’s access rules. Let’s keep the demo to two simple requirements:

We’ll use fictional logs and a shell sandbox to check both boundaries without needing a model API. The results below describe what to expect when you run the demo.

Install sbx on macOS or Linux and sign in with an AI Governance licensed test user. You’ll also need permission to manage policies and audit delivery in a test organization:

Run these commands in your host terminal to create a workspace and a separate directory containing a fictional bank log:

Next, enable AI Governance for your organization. In Docker Home, open AI Platform → Manage and turn on AI Governance:

Figure 3. Enable AI Governance in Docker Home before configuring organization policies.

Next, create the bank’s network and filesystem access rules.

First, let’s look at network access. In a bank, you don’t want employees’ coding agents connecting to just any website. For this demo, we’ll allow the sandbox to reach only hrittikhere.com (standing in for the bank’s website) over HTTPS.

Once AI Governance is enabled, open AI Platform → Network access → Create policy and enter the following values:

With this as the only applicable network allow rule, all other destinations remain blocked by default.

The curl checks below request headers without following redirects, so they need only hrittikhere.com:443. If you later add -L to follow a redirect to www.hrittikhere.com, allow that exact hostname on port 443 as well.

Next, let’s protect the bank’s logs. Employees may need a coding agent to work on a project, but that agent should not be able to read or modify sensitive logs in the home directory. We’ll allow access to the demo workspace and block access to bank-logs.

Open AI Platform → Filesystem access → Create policy.

Note: Use ~ in policy paths, not $HOME; environment variables are not expanded by the policy engine.

First, allow the project workspace with the values below. A writable workspace needs both Read and Write permissions for sandbox creation to succeed:

~/sbx-bank-demo/workspace~/sbx-bank-demo/workspace/**

In the same bank-demo-filesystem policy, add a second rule named bank-demo-logs to deny access to the protected logs:

~/sbx-bank-demo/bank-logs~/sbx-bank-demo/bank-logs/**

The workspace allow rule covers workspace and its descendants; it does not include the sibling bank-logs directory. The logs are protected by the explicit deny rule.

Save the policies. You can also check their rules and scope in the Platform UI. Allow up to five minutes for policy sync, then inspect the active rules:

Figure 6. Active organization rules allow hrittikhere.com:443 and the workspace, and deny bank-logs mounts.

Create a fresh sandbox with only the approved workspace:

From the host terminal, run the following requests inside the sandbox. The –max-time 15 option caps each request at 15 seconds, so a stalled request times out instead of hanging:

The first request should return HTTP headers from the approved site:

Figure 7. The approved HTTPS request to hrittikhere.com returns HTTP 200.

Figure 8. The request to example.com returns HTTP 403; the network log confirms the policy decision.

You can also check the network log to confirm that the failure came from policy enforcement:

The network log distinguishes allowed traffic from requests blocked by policy:

Figure 9. The network log records hrittikhere.com:443 as allowed and example.com:443 as blocked.

Run both commands below from your host terminal so $HOME expands to the same host path. First read the fictional log on the host, then request that path inside the sandbox:

Figure 10. The host can read the fictional bank log.

The sandbox command should fail with “No such file or directory” because bank-logs is not mounted. This checks filesystem isolation; the next command tests mount-policy enforcement:

Figure 11. The unmounted bank-logs path returns “No such file or directory” inside the sandbox.

Now check whether a developer could add the logs as a read-only mount:

Creation should fail with a mount policy denied error:

Figure 12. The bank-demo-logs rule rejects the read-only mount with a Read policy denial.

A read-only mount still requires Read permission. The bank-demo-logs rule denies Read, so :ro does not bypass the policy.

You can also review the policy decisions in the Docker Home UI under AI Platform → Audit logs → Audit events. Filter by the test user and time, then inspect the denied mount request for bank-logs. Look for filesystem_mount, the deny decision, the resource path, and the user. If Cloud delivery was disabled during the test, enable it and repeat the mount request.

Figure 13. Audit events record the denied bank-logs mount and allowed workspace mounts.

These audit records capture governance decisions, not every file operation or the agent’s prompts and output. sbx policy log shows network traffic; use the audit view for the mount decision. Docker also supports local JSONL audit files and Cloud delivery for hosted search, CSV export, and SIEM forwarding.

The bank now has a simple access-control check: the approved site is reachable, an unapproved site is blocked, and protected logs cannot be read or mounted. This supports the bank’s compliance work; it does not establish compliance on its own.

After reviewing the results, remove the sandbox:

You can then remove the fictional demo files and the test policies.

Putting AI governance policies in place is only the first step. The real challenge is making sure those policies are practical, consistently enforced, and easy to verify as more developers start using coding agents. A few good practices can help teams reduce unnecessary access, apply the right restrictions to different workloads, and maintain visibility into what agents are doing. Here are five recommendations to keep in mind when rolling out Docker AI Governance across your organization:

The same questions apply when agents run in continuous integration (CI) jobs or other environments: which host files can they access, which services can they reach, and which credentials can they use? Check which controls each environment supports and repeat the tests there. A policy on a developer’s laptop does not automatically protect an unrelated container or production cluster.

For security teams, useful evidence connects a licensed, signed-in user to an active policy, a blocked action, and a record they can find. Check sign-in enforcement and license coverage alongside policy. An empty audit dashboard may mean some sessions were never covered.

Sandboxing limits an agent’s access on one machine. Governance applies shared rules to managed sessions across the organization. Providers will fix individual failures, but the next failure may look different. Limit which files, services, and credentials it can affect, then test those limits and check the records.

Start with the local sandboxing walkthrough, inspect your rules with sbx policy ls, and run the bank demo with your platform or security team. Use the Docker AI Governance documentation and announcement to plan a small rollout. Expand it after you have checked both the blocked actions and the audit records.

#10024. Build full-stack Android apps with Gemini in Google AI Studio

Google AI Studio, with Gemini 4 Carbon and Hydrogen, now enables you to build and ship full-stack, Android applications and publish to the Play Store in minutes. You can now go from a single prompt to a production-ready Kotlin Android app complete with data persistence, user sign-in, and AI features. Simply bring your idea and the Google AI Studio Build agent takes care of the entire setup. From configuring your database to securely calling the Gemini API, you can rapidly build and test on your own device directly from the Play Store at no cost.

Alongside full-stack Firebase backend provisioning, a major wave of upgrades is rolling out across the Android development workflow in Google AI Studio. Developers can now iterate in a faster, high-definition (HD) Android emulator with real-time audio output, send one-click emulator screenshots directly to the coding agent for visual UI changes, and style apps with custom fonts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Here is what is launching today for Android app development in Google AI Studio:

Previously, Android apps generated in AI Studio were limited to local session storage. Now, when your prompt describes an Android app that needs persistent storage, user sign-in, or generative AI features, the Google AI Studio Build agent proactively detects those requirements. With your confirmation, the agent provisions the backend and wires up your Kotlin codebase automatically:

Under the hood, the Build agent automatically adds the Firebase Bill of Materials (BoM) and generates clean, idiomatic Kotlin code for your data and AI layers:

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Building great mobile interfaces requires a tight visual and interactive feedback loop. Both the in-browser Android emulator and the Build agent's toolchain include new capabilities to help you inspect, hear, and refine your app in real time:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Testing in an emulator accelerates iteration, but nothing replaces running your app on a physical device. Historically, installing a newly built Android app onto a phone required enabling USB debugging cables, managing signing keystores locally, or setting up a full developer console account.

With Google Play integrated directly into Google AI Studio, you can set up a Play account in a few clicks from the publish panel and publish your app straight to your personal Google Play library. Google AI Studio builds and signs the Android App Bundle (.aab) behind the scenes, so you can immediately download and run your custom Android app on your phone directly from Google Play.

#10023. Extortion Group Luna Moth Flits Into Law Firms a Second Time

The cybercriminals are retargeting law firms that refused to pay its first ransom, with a social engineering con aimed at stealing Microsoft access tokens.

A data extortion outfit has returned to previously targeted law firms for a second go, via a "vishing"-based social engineering campaign where they purport to be IT staff.

That's according to the ReliaQuest Threat Research team, which today published a blog post disclosing that Luna Moth, a threat extortion group that has targeted law firms since at least mid-2023, has for now abandoned remote-management tools and manual exfiltration to steal data, instead looking to swindle victims out of access tokens. Then, they're using these tokens to access and steal information.

Joseph Keyes, author of the blog post and cyber threat intelligence analyst at ReliaQuest, wrote that this latest wave of targets are "likely" those that didn't pay Luna Moth's first extortion demand, with this second hit coming weeks-to-months later.

A ReliaQuest spokesperson tells Dark Reading that these firms were targeted a second time because the group knows these organizations can be socially engineered (so it tries again with different tactics), for a second set of data. Moreover, as fewer organizations are paying, law firms make a good fit to target, because "their client data is highly sensitive and the reputational hit from exposure is severe, so repeated leverage is worth the effort."

Related:3AM Affiliate Embraces AI-Like Loops in European School Attack

In the initial variants of the attack, Luna Moth would reach out as IT support and trick victims into connecting to Windows' built-in remote assistance tool. It would then download data directly. This time, the group sent blank-subject emails from a free email address with a broken PDF masquerading as a business report; the "report" would then fail to open. The attacker would use this pretext to reach out soon after, posing as IT support responding to a malicious alert, while at the same time offering to fix the PDF.

Once on a video call, the "IT support" directs the target to turn on screen-share functionality so they can direct the target through a "fix." The attacker then tells the target to open Microsoft Copilot from the Web browser on their work laptop, open the browser's developer tools, find the request Copilot sends to Microsoft Graph, and copy the application's access token.

The attacker instructs the target to paste the token into a public text-sharing site the attacker can access. Finally, the attacker gives the token to its own folder and downloads files through the Microsoft Graph API.

This social engineering tactic requires no malware and no vulnerability. It relies solely on tricking a target into giving an access token, effectively a master key into the front door of an organization. The use of IT support to "fix" a technical issue evokes ClickFix, as both techniques rely on human problem-solving tendencies to trick an end user.

Related:'AdFection' Turns Ad Impressions Into Zero-Click Spyware Delivery

The ReliaQuest spokesperson says the firm considers this attack representative of a gap in how controls are scoped within an organization. Because the token is issued to the victim's compliant device and handed over "willingly," security red flags do not go off.

"A token is a bearer credential, so it works from anywhere until it expires, unless CAE [Continuous Access Evaluation] with strict location enforcement (or token binding) applies," the spokesperson says. "Similar to device code phishing, the user completes a legitimate flow, so MFA is satisfied. The weakness is the assumption that authentication equals ongoing trust. Microsoft's defaults, such as the 60- to 90-minute lifetime and CAE not being enforced by default, make it easier."

ReliaQuest recommends that organizations turn CAE on by default, block browser developer tools and token-inspection pages on managed browsers, and restrict remote-support and screen-sharing tools where there's no business need. Additionally, it may be wise to inform employees of the ways threat actors attempt to steal credentials from employees.

Related:Biomemory Launches DNA Shield, First Commercial Product Built on Its Molecular IT Platform

As the blog post put it, "Tell users that IT will never ask for developer tools or tokens and that they should verify IT requests by callback."

#10018. Amazon S3 Vectors now supports metadata pre-filtering for higher recall on filtered searches

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Today, we’re announcing metadata pre-filtering for Amazon S3 Vectors, which delivers higher recall on filtered queries by evaluating your metadata filter before the similarity search. You can filter on attributes such as tenant, category, status, or time, and pre-filtering adds prefix matching with $startsWith for paths, URLs, and hierarchical keys. Each vector carries up to 2 KB of filterable metadata, and a single query supports up to 100 filter constraints. There is no additional cost, no re-ingestion, and no change to your queries.

Most applications never search a whole index. They search the part of it that belongs to a particular user, account, or category, and they express that scope as a metadata filter. Semantic search, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and agentic applications all need the same thing from a filtered query: a similarity search that covers the vectors matching the filter, and returns the closest of them. With pre-filtering, a filtered query returns more of the relevant matches your index contains, giving you higher recall on filtered searches.

Common use cases Pre-filtering applies wherever results have to be both relevant and correctly scoped:

How pre-filtering works Each vector in an S3 Vectors index can carry application-defined metadata, and a query can filter on those fields.

Every vector index has an index mode. On an index whose index mode is ENHANCED, S3 Vectors resolves your filter first, then searches only the vectors that match. On an index whose index mode is CLASSIC, S3 Vectors performs the vector search and filter evaluation in tandem, validating each candidate vector against your filter as it searches. Existing indexes use CLASSIC until you update them.

Consider a support knowledge base of 8 million tickets, where an agent searches one customer’s history for a recurring error. If that customer accounts for 400 of those tickets, resolving customer_id first means the similarity search runs across all 400 of them, so the agent sees that customer’s prior occurrences. Before the index was updated, the same query drew its candidates from the full 8 million, and the result set contained fewer of that customer’s matching tickets.

On highly selective filters, pre-filtering returns up to 5x more of the matching vectors than the same query returned before on CLASSIC indexes.

Getting started Before you start, make sure your IAM policy grants permissions for the new actions.

You can get started in three steps. The walkthrough below builds a small product-catalog index and runs a selective filter against it, the same pattern you would use for a multi-tenant RAG store or a document search scoped to one client.

The dimension must match the output size of your embedding model, and distance-metric should match how that model was trained (cosine is common for text embeddings). Second, write vectors with the PutVectors API, attaching up to 2 KB of filterable metadata to each vector:

Each vector carries the attributes your application filters on. In this example, tenant_id scopes results to a single customer, category narrows by document type, created_date records when the document was created, and active is a boolean flag. By default every metadata field is filterable, so you can query on any of them without declaring a schema up front.

Third, run a filtered similarity query with the QueryVectors API. The filter uses a compact JSON syntax where a bare key-value pair is an equality match, and operators such as $and, $or, and $gt combine or refine conditions. Pass --return-metadata so the query returns each vector’s metadata:

The expected result is a single vector, doc-001, the only one matching all three filter conditions (tenant_id, category, and active):

S3 Vectors first narrows the search space to vectors matching all three filter conditions, then returns the 50 most similar vectors from that subset. Because the filter is applied before the search, those results are drawn from across all the vectors that match it.

Prefix matching with $startsWith Pre-filtering adds a prefix match operator for filtering on paths, URLs, and hierarchical keys. A document store that encodes case and folder structure into a document ID can scope a search to a subtree in one condition:

$startsWith joins the existing operators: equality, numeric range, set membership, existence checks, and boolean logic with $and and $or.

Turning on pre-filtering for existing indexes Call UpdateIndexMode on an existing index to turn on pre-filtering:

Pre-filtering takes effect in place. Your existing vectors are not re-ingested, your queries do not change, and the new filter operators are available immediately.

Here is the difference on the same index and the same query. Before the update, a query scoped to one tenant returns two of the ten results requested:

After the update, the same query returns a full result set drawn from across that tenant’s documents:

Rolling out across your indexes Once you have validated pre-filtering on an index, set the default index mode on the vector bucket so that new indexes use ENHANCED without a follow-up call:

To bring the rest of your existing indexes across, list them and check the index mode on each one, then call UpdateIndexMode on the ones still using CLASSIC:

Now available Metadata pre-filtering is available at no additional cost in all commercial AWS Regions where Amazon S3 Vectors is available, and in the AWS China Regions. You pay standard S3 Vectors pricing for storage, PUT requests, and queries. For full pricing details, visit the Amazon S3 pricing page. For regional availability, visit Amazon S3 Vectors Regions and quotas.

Whether you’re scoping a RAG application to one tenant, scoping an agent’s searches to one user’s documents, or narrowing a catalog search to a licensing window, pre-filtering lets you apply those filters without trading away recall. To learn more and get started, visit the Amazon S3 Vectors documentation. Send feedback to AWS re:Post for S3 or through your usual AWS Support contacts.

#10015. Amazon S3 Tables now support all Apache Iceberg V3 data types

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

Amazon S3 Tables now support all data types in the Apache Iceberg V3 specification. You can create V3 tables or upgrade existing V2 tables to take advantage of V3 features like deletion vectors, row lineage, and new data types such as variant, nanosecond timestamps, unknown, geometry, and geography.

Apache Iceberg has become the open standard for managing large analytics datasets. It lets you manage petabyte-scale tables with features like schema evolution, hidden partitioning, and time travel queries, while keeping your data in open Parquet files in data lakes on object storage like Amazon S3. Amazon S3 Tables offer storage purpose-built to keep Iceberg tables performant and cost-effective as they grow, with fully managed features like automatic compaction, maintenance, replication, and Intelligent-Tiering.

Teams running analytics on Apache Iceberg V2 tables often hit the same limits as their data grows. A compliance request to delete 50,000 user records from a 2-billion-row table leaves behind positional delete files that slow queries until compaction runs. Semi-structured events land as JSON strings that every query has to parse. Geospatial coordinates and nanosecond-precision timestamps get encoded as strings or integers. Each workaround adds storage cost, query latency, and pipeline code. With V3, Iceberg solves these challenges by offering native support for semi-structured and geospatial data, faster row-level operations, and built-in row lineage for data governance.

Starting today, Amazon S3 Tables support all V3 data types, including variant, nanosecond timestamps, geometry, geography, and unknown, along with deletion vectors and row lineage. You can create new V3 tables or upgrade existing V2 tables in place, and S3 Tables continue to run compaction and maintenance for you.

Apache Iceberg V3 V3 is the latest version of the Iceberg specification. Among its many improvements, V3 introduces capabilities that address the most common pain points in V2. This includes:

Deletion vectors replace V2’s positional delete files with a compact binary format. That 50,000-row compliance delete now writes a single deletion vector file instead of thousands of small deletes, significantly reducing compaction time and delete file overhead.

Row lineage adds _row_id and _last_updated_sequence_number to each record automatically. Your downstream pipelines can query these fields to find changed rows without scanning the full table.

New data types let you store semi-structured, geospatial, and nanosecond-precision data natively instead of encoding it as strings or integers:

Variant data type stores semi-structured data in columnar format. During writes, the engine shreds variant data into hidden columns and collects statistics. At query time, those statistics enable file pruning that significantly reduces I/O compared to parsing JSON strings.

The following sections walk through how to use these V3 capabilities in practice, with examples that show how to create tables, work with the new data types, and manage data at scale.

Getting started A retail analytics team tracks user behavior across web and mobile apps. Each event has a different structure: page views include URLs and duration, purchases include items and amounts, and searches include query terms and result counts. With V3’s variant type, you store all event shapes in one table without predefined schemas:

Insert events with different payload shapes without worrying about schema evolution:

Now query the variant column directly, without PARSE_JSON at read time. With Amazon EMR Spark, use variant_get:

To enable deletion vectors for write operations, configure merge-on-read mode:

Now when you run a compliance delete, V3 writes a small deletion vector instead of rewriting data files:

S3 Tables compaction handles these deletion vector files automatically on the next maintenance cycle.

Upgrading from V2 AWS provides backwards compatibility for both versions to minimize disruption during migration to V3. Existing V2 readers continue to work on upgraded tables until you’re ready to fully adopt V3 features. For more details, see the S3 Tables Iceberg V3 documentation. Upgrade an existing table atomically without rewriting data:

On the next compaction cycle, S3 Tables remove old V2 delete files. New modifications use deletion vectors automatically. Row lineage fields initialize on the first data modification after the upgrade.

This is a one-way operation. The Apache Iceberg specification does not support downgrading from V3 to V2. Verify that all engines accessing the table support V3 before upgrading.

Using row lineage for incremental pipelines After your table has V3 data, use row lineage to build efficient incremental pipelines:

This returns only rows modified after sequence number 42. Your downstream jobs can checkpoint this value and process only new changes on each run, instead of scanning the full table.

Compatibility across AWS analytics services AWS offers the broadest native Apache Iceberg support of any major cloud provider, with Iceberg-compatible services at every layer of the data stack: ingestion, storage, catalog, and analytics. You can store and automatically optimize V3 tables in Amazon S3 Tables, write data with Amazon EMR Spark, integrate and manage data with AWS Glue, and run analytics with Amazon Redshift. To learn more about AWS analytics support for V3, see the Apache Iceberg on AWS prescriptive guidance.

Both S3 Tables and AWS Glue Data Catalog support the Iceberg REST Catalog (IRC) API, enabling interoperability across engines regardless of the catalog endpoint.

Now available Amazon S3 Tables support for all Apache Iceberg V3 data types is now available in all AWS Regions where S3 Tables are supported. Apache Iceberg V3 support is available at no additional charge; standard S3 Tables pricing applies.

To get started, visit the Amazon S3 Tables documentation or create a table bucket from the Amazon S3 console. If you want to call APIs, search documentation, find regional availability, and check troubleshooting about this feature, try using the AWS MCP Server and plugins with your preferred AI tool. Send feedback to AWS re:Post or through your usual AWS Support contacts.

#10014. Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

Traditionally, training state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) relies on a monolithic cluster of accelerators, where all devices are collocated and tightly coupled via high-bandwidth to constantly synchronize gradients. However, physical and cost constraints make scaling a single slice increasingly difficult.

To scale further, we can connect multiple compute slices across geographically distributed datacenters. However, when training across multiple datacenters over wide-area networks (WAN), standard data-parallel training suffers from severe network stalls because slow WAN transfers cannot be fully overlapped with computation.

To address this, the Google DeepMind and Google Research teams developed DiLoCo (Distributed Low-Communication), a distributed optimization algorithm designed to make training resilient to these low-bandwidth, high-latency connections. In this blog post, we discuss the core concepts of DiLoCo, how it is implemented in MaxText (Google's open-source JAX-based LLM training framework), and the performance benefits it brings to large-scale LLM training on Google Cloud.

DiLoCo was introduced by Google DeepMind in the paper DiLoCo: Distributed Low-Communication Training of Language Models. Instead of synchronizing gradients across all devices at every single training step (as in standard Data Parallelism), DiLoCo partitions training across M independent compute groups, referred to as learners, and allows each learner to perform many local optimization steps independently before communicating across groups.

Despite communicating significantly less frequently (by a factor of H, e.g., 1-2 orders of magnitude less) than standard synchronous training, DiLoCo has been shown to achieve comparable convergence and model quality.

The Vanilla DiLoCo described above reduces total communication volume by a factor of H, however, all learners still transmit their weight deltas for the entire model all at once at every H-th step. On a thin network pipe, that full-model transfer creates a large periodic stall every H steps. This issue is alleviated by Streaming DiLoCo, which is described in the next section.

In multi-slice training, communication within a single slice is handled by high-speed Inter-Chip Interconnects (ICI). When scaling to multiple slices, synchronization across slices must traverse the Data Center Network (DCN) or, if the slices are geographically distributed, wide-area networks (WAN).

Within a single datacenter, DCN bandwidth is high enough that JAX and XLA can often hide standard Data Parallel all-reduce collectives by overlapping gradient communication with backward-pass computation. Across datacenters or congested links, however, bandwidth drops and latency rises sharply. Standard DP stalls at every step waiting for global gradient synchronization, collapsing Model FLOPS Utilization (MFU).

Streaming DiLoCo eliminates this synchronization stall by partitioning the model's parameters into P disjoint fragments. Over the course of H steps, every single fragment in the model is synchronized exactly once, achieving the exact same outer-loop cadence as vanilla DiLoCo without spiky bandwidth utilization.

Using a roofline model (similar to the JAX Scaling Book), we can calculate the network bandwidth required to prevent communication stalls for a model with ∣Θ∣ parameters (at b bytes per parameter, e.g., b=2 for bfloat16) and per-step compute time tstep=tfwd+tbwd. For example, the standard Data Parallel algorithm transfers 2∣Θ∣b bytes per sync via an all-reduce collective, which is a distributed reduce-scatter where each node reduces a different part, followed by an all-gather so all participants receive all parts. To achieve zero communication stalls, we overlap the reduce-scatter of the gradients with the backward pass, which produces them and takes tbwd time. Therefore, the peak required bandwidth is Bdp ≈ 2∣Θ∣b / tbwd.

Worked Example (Qwen3-32B in bfloat16, tstep​≈6.2s, P=H=65):

To evaluate distributed efficiency under realistic network bottlenecks, we benchmarked Qwen3-32B (64 decoder layers, partitioned into P = 65 fragments with H = 65) across 2× TPU v5p-32 slices (dcn_diloco_parallelism=2) using built-in Linux traffic-control (tc) bandwidth shaping to limit inter-slice bandwidth across three network regimes: Unthrottled (Line Rate), 30 Gbit/s aggregate (Moderate Congestion), and 10 Gbit/s aggregate (Severe Congestion).

A common question with low-communication training is whether allowing learners to train independently between synchronizations degrades model quality—especially on Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, where independent updates could cause expert routers to drift apart.

We evaluated Streaming DiLoCo against synchronous Data Parallel pre-training across two MoE architectures in MaxText (Qwen3-30B-A3B and Gemma4-26B-A4B) over 4,000 training steps:

Empirical results confirm that Streaming DiLoCo preserves—and even slightly improves—validation loss:

MaxText provides a native JAX and Flax NNX implementation of both Vanilla and Streaming DiLoCo—requiring no external central parameter servers.

You can enable Streaming DiLoCo in your MaxText YAML configuration or CLI overrides by setting enable_diloco: true and enable_streaming_diloco: true. As a best practice, set both num_diloco_fragments (P) and diloco_sync_period (H) to N_layers + 1 so that exactly one fragment is synchronized per training step (Δh = 1):

In MaxText, diloco.py splits each global batch across the M learners so each learner processes GBS / M tokens per step. To overlap cross-slice communication with TPU computation in Streaming DiLoCo, weight deltas for the scheduled fragment are offloaded to M-way sharded syncer workers running on the learner host CPUs. Rather than routing through a single central coordinator, the host-CPU syncer shards execute a distributed all-reduce across the network in the background to average the fragment deltas while the TPUs continue running inner-loop training steps. Each syncer shard then applies the outer Nesterov momentum update locally and writes the updated fragment weights back to its colocated TPU learner. To dive deeper or launch your first run:

Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText decouples large-scale LLM pre-training from the requirement of a single, monolithic, high-bandwidth fabric. By combining use of an inner and outer optimizer with fine-grained parameter streaming, engineering teams can pool TPU slices across congested datacenter networks or geographically distributed metros—sustaining high hardware utilization without compromising model convergence. Streaming DiLoCo is available today in MaxText v0.2.5 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Try it today for experimenting with distributed training workloads.

#10012. Cyber Experts Weigh In: How to Fight Artex & AntiFox

Cybercriminals using agentic pen-testing tools is a proverbial genie out of the bottle at this point, so enterprises need to get a defense plan in place right away.

A month since it went closed-source, the Artex agentic penetration testing framework is spreading faster and causing more havoc than ever. Still, cyber defenders tell Dark Reading that defending against it is largely a matter of willingness, not capability.

To recap: In August, a Chinese open source (OSS) developer released a cybercrime-grade agentic red-teaming tool called "Artex." In the months that followed, attackers and cybersecurity researchers have gradually realized just how effective Artex is at probing for vulnerabilities and turning initial access into meaningful compromise at so-called machine speed.

"Artex is what the perpetual conflict era looks like," says Yuval Itzchakov, co-founder and chief technology officer (CTO) of A Security. "When our research team ran it against our open source benchmark, it found eight vulnerabilities in about 45 minutes, including a critical privilege escalation to admin, and chained them into a full attack path through a WAF, CAPTCHA, and MFA. All it took was a download, an install script, and a model key."

Related:Cyber Insurers See New Divide: AI Defense 'Haves' & 'Have Nots'

Nothing about it is so complicated, he says, "And that's the point. Take the guardrails off an AI hacking tool, and anyone can run agentic attacks at scale. In Artex, scope is just a suggestion to the model."

Though its author de-listed the original Artex on Oct. 8, new variants continue to cause trouble. The variant du jour, "AntiFox," was recently weaponized by attackers to exploit Internet-facing Web services and their associated Amazon Web Services (AWS) environments. According to researchers at Arctic Wolf Labs, threat actors used their new agentic toy to breach, deploy Web shells, and execute operating system (OS) commands on compromised Microsoft Internet Information Services (IIS) servers, as well as enumerate services, expose production secrets, and manipulate Simple Storage Service (S3) buckets across AWS environments.

Notably, Arctic Wolf Labs offered relatively banal advice for organizations defending against this quite new threat: hunt for indicators of compromise (IoCs), rotate and revoke relevant credentials and sessions. In fact, researchers say, there's little more to defending against Artex or AntiFox than basic cybersecurity hygiene. However, it's also important to avoid making assumptions; as GitGuardian pointed out in a blog post on AntiFox, "Blocking scanner tools won't stop these agents, and a leaked key stays dangerous until someone revokes it."

The prospect of every Tom, Dick, and Harry having an agentic attack framework in their pocket might be intimidating, but cybersecurity defenders still possess some distinct advantages.

Related:How Southeastern University Got a Handle on Its Data

First off, Itzchakov points out that "Artex stops at the front door. It has no lateral movement, no Active Directory testing, and no view inside. Defenders can see their whole environment and test it continuously with the same speed and autonomy, under guardrails no one can switch off. The organizations that come out ahead will treat this as permanent and build their security around it."

In a profile of a recent incident response (IR) effort against an Artex attack, Eye Research noted another serious weakness of agentic tools in general: They're loud. "The agents left their name in pod names and probes, hammered endpoints no human would touch, and tripped detections on systems with EDR installed," it explained. "Where we had visibility, we saw them immediately."

"If you want to be positive, there are a lot of small steps you can put together in order to protect networks and organizations," says Guillaume Valadon, lead cybersecurity researcher at GitGuardian. To catch Artex or AntiFox's loudness, for example, "blue teams must have in place a security information and event management (SIEM) platform and a really good detection system, to make sure that they will detect these tools in the noise."

Related:US Cyber Director Pick Signals Shift Toward Expertise

Besides active detection, organizations would be well advised to focus on secrets management. Artex and AntiFox have proven adept at finding secrets, in part because they use ordinary commands like "git" and "grep" to find what they're looking for, instead of shipping a more easily identifiable and blockable secret scanner.

"Defenders need to be looking for exposed and dormant keys, checking Git repos for exposed secrets, and heavily implementing a defense-in-depth posture," adds Justin Moore, Arctic Wolf's director of adversary operations. "What makes these frameworks so effective is not that they’re conducting novel attacks; they’re just doing what we already know, just faster than we have seen before. Good cyber hygiene is exactly what prevents these types of attacks from being so successful, whether they’re agentic or not.”

Despite all of the positive steps organizations can take, Valadon says, "If I'm being negative, I would say that it's too late."

"For years we have been telling people that they should not output secrets — that we're finding really important hardcoded secrets everywhere, publicly. On Docker, GitHub, and so on. Now, because we have these tools that can go fast from nothing to discovery to weaponization, that makes it more difficult for blue teams," he says.

"The agentic capacity for the attacker is already here, and they're going faster and faster and faster," he notes. "So if you didn't do the right stuff before, it will be difficult for you to catch up."

The cybersecurity industry has been waiting for AI-driven cyberattacks for years now. Product marketing campaigns have hinged on agentic attacks being right around the corner. Stories of lightly AI-influenced tactics, techniques, procedures (TTPs), and malware have kept food on cyber reporters' plates. Most of the discourse has been, at best, theoretical, at worst, misleading.

A meaningful, if incomplete, turning point seems to have occurred around midyear, if not among the most sophisticated attackers, then at least in the world of middling cybercriminals.

"When we all started using ChatGPT, at that time, we were just asking it simple questions. It was fun, but it was not possible to code," Valadon explains. "And then we had agents that were more capable at coding and syncing. This year you can do it all, from end to end. You just give [an agent] a rough idea, and it will do some planning, syncing, it will build the code for you, and deploy it for you. It's getting better and better at doing the whole cycle."

He also points to Anthropic and OpenAI's warnings about OSS AI earlier this fall: "Some people are saying that we are seeing these [attack] frameworks because of Chinese open-weight models. Because if you have a big GPU, you are able to run your own offensive models with Artex."

"It's getting more and more dangerous," he says. "It's all fitting into the HuggingFace, OpenAI, Anthropic narrative [warning against attackers] having access to real offensive tools."

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.
